\mathrm{Tor}(M;\mathrm{Ad}_{\rho})\in\mathbb{C}^{*}/\{\pm 1\}

is defined without making any additional choice.

Now suppose
M
is a compact, orientable
3
-manifold with boundary consisting of
n
disjoint tori
T_{1}\dots,T_{n}
whose interior admits a complete hyperbolic structure with finite volume. Let
\mathrm{X}(M)
be the
\mathrm{PSL}(2;\mathbb{C})
-character variety of
M,
and let
\mathrm{X}^{n}(M)=\cup\mathrm{X}_{k}(M)
the union of the irreducible components
\{\mathrm{X}_{k}(M)\}
of
\mathrm{X}(M)
that have dimension equal to
n.
If
M
is hyperbolic, then
\mathrm{X}^{n}(M)
is non-empty because it contains the distinguished component
\mathrm{X}_{0}(M)
containing the character of the holomony representation of the complete hyperbolic structure of
M

[
66
,
43
]
.

Below we recall two fundamental results (Theorem
2.18
and Theorem
2.20
) of Porti
[
54
]
. Theorem
2.20
was originally proved for characters in
\mathrm{X}_{0}(M),
and was generalized to characters in
\mathrm{X}^{n}(X)
by essentially the same argument in
[
75
]
. We denote by
\mathrm{X}^{\text{irr}}(M)
the Zariski-open subset of
\mathrm{X}(M)
consisting of the irreducible characters.

Theorem 2.18
.

[
53
, section 3.3.3]
For a system of simple closed curves
\bm{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})
on
\partial M
with
\alpha_{i}\subset T_{i},

i\in\{1,\dots,n\},
and a character
[\rho]
in a Zariski open subset of
\mathrm{X}_{0}(M)\cap\mathrm{X}^{\text{irr}}(M),
we have:

(i)

For
k\neq 1,2,

\mathrm{H}_{k}(M;\mathrm{Ad}\rho)=0.

(ii)

For
i\in\{1,\dots,n\},
up to scalar
\mathrm{A}d_{\rho}(\pi_{1}(T_{i}))^{T}
has a unique invariant vector
\mathbf{I}_{i}\in\mathbb{C}^{3};
and

\mathrm{H}_{1}(M;\mathrm{Ad}\rho)\cong\mathbb{C}^{n}

with a basis

\mathbf{h}^{1}_{(M,\alpha)}=\{\mathbf{I}_{1}\otimes[\alpha_{1}],\dots,\mathbf{I}_{n}\otimes[\alpha_{n}]\}

where
([\alpha_{1}],\dots,[\alpha_{n}])\in\mathrm{H}_{1}(\partial M;\mathbb{Z})\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{H}_{1}(T_{i};\mathbb{Z}).

(iii)

Let
([T_{1}],\dots,[T_{n}])\in\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{H}_{2}(T_{i};\mathbb{Z})
be the fundamental classes of
T_{1},\dots,T_{n}.
Then

\mathrm{H}_{2}(M;\mathrm{Ad}\rho)\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{H}_{2}(T_{i};\mathrm{Ad}\rho)\cong\mathbb{C}^{n}

with a basis

\mathbf{h}^{2}_{M}=\{\mathbf{I}_{1}\otimes[T_{1}],\dots,\mathbf{I}_{n}\otimes[T_{n}]\}.

Remark 2.19

(
[
53
,
43
,
26
]
)
.

Important examples of the characters in Theorem
2.18
include the character of the holonomy representation of the complete hyperbolic structure on the interior of
M,

the restriction of the holonomy representation of the closed
3
-manifold
M_{\bm{\mu}}
obtained from
M
by doing the hyperbolic Dehn-filling along the system of simple closed curves
\bm{\mu}
on
\partial M,

and the holonomy representation of a hyperbolic structure on the interior of
M
whose completion is a conical manifold with cone angles less than
2\pi.

Let
\bm{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})
be a system of simple closed curves on
\partial M
with
\alpha_{i}\subset T_{i},

i\in\{1,\dots,n\}.
A character
[\rho]
in
\mathrm{X}^{n}(M)\cap\mathrm{X}^{\text{irr}}(M)
is
\bm{\alpha}
-regular
if condition (ii) in Theorem
2.18
is satisfied. Then there is an
adjoint twisted Reidemeister torsion function
\mathbb{T}_{(M,\bm{\alpha})}
with respect to
\bm{\alpha}
defined on
\mathrm{X}^{n}(M)\cap\mathrm{X}^{\text{irr}}(M)
such that

\mathbb{T}_{(M,\bm{\alpha})}([\rho])\in\mathbb{C}^{*}/\{\pm 1\}

if
\rho
is
\bm{\alpha}
-regular, and equals
0
if otherwise.

Theorem 2.20
.

[
53
, Theorem 4.1]

Let
M
be a compact, orientable
3
-manifold with boundary consisting of
n
disjoint tori
T_{1}\dots,T_{n}
whose interior admits a complete hyperbolic structure with finite volume. Let
\mathbb{C}(\mathrm{X}^{n}(M)\cap\mathrm{X}^{\text{irr}}(M))
be the ring of rational functions over
\mathrm{X}^{n}(M)\cap\mathrm{X}^{\text{irr}}(M).
Then

\begin{split}\mathrm{H}_{1}(\partial M;\mathbb{Z})&\to\mathbb{C}(\mathrm{X}^{n}(M)\cap\mathrm{X}^{\text{irr}}(M))\\
\quad\quad\bm{\alpha}\quad\ \ &\mapsto\quad\quad\quad\mathbb{T}_{(M,\bm{\alpha})}\end{split}

up to sign defines a function which is a
\mathbb{Z}
-multilinear homomorphism with respect to the direct sum
\mathrm{H}_{1}(\partial M;\mathbb{Z})\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{H}_{1}(T_{i};\mathbb{Z})
satisfying the following properties:

(i)

For a system of simple closed curves
\bm{\alpha}
on
\partial M,
if the component
\mathrm{X}_{k}(M)
contains an
\bm{\alpha}
-regular character, then the support of
\mathbb{T}_{(M,\bm{\alpha})}

contains a Zariski-open subset of
\mathrm{X}_{k}(M)\cap\mathrm{X}^{\text{irr}}(M)
consisting of all the
\bm{\alpha}
-regular characters in
\mathrm{X}_{k}(M).

(ii)

(Change of Curves Formula).
Let
\bm{\beta}=\{\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{n}\}
and
\bm{\gamma}=\{\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\}
be two systems of simple closed curves on
\partial M.
Let
(u_{\beta_{1}},\dots,u_{\beta_{n}})
and
(u_{\gamma_{1}},\dots,u_{\gamma_{n}})
respectively be the logarithmic holonomies of the curves in
\bm{\beta}
and
\bm{\gamma}.
Then we have the equality of rational functions

\mathbb{T}_{(M,\bm{\beta})}=\pm\det\bigg(\frac{\partial(u_{\beta_{1}},\dots,u_{\beta_{n}})}{\partial(u_{\gamma_{1}},\dots,u_{\gamma_{n}})}\bigg)\mathbb{T}_{(M,\bm{\gamma})}

(2.27)

on
\mathrm{X}_{k}(M)\cap\mathrm{X}^{\text{irr}}(M)
for the component
\mathrm{X}_{k}(M)
containing a
\bm{\gamma}
-regular character, where
\frac{\partial(u_{\beta_{1}},\dots,u_{\beta_{n}})}{\partial(u_{\gamma_{1}},\dots,u_{\gamma_{n}})}
is the Jocobian matrix of
(u_{\beta_{1}},\dots,u_{\beta_{n}})
with respect to
(u_{\gamma_{1}},\dots,u_{\gamma_{n}}).

(iii)

(Surgery Formula).
Let
[\rho_{\bm{\mu}}]\in\mathrm{X}^{n}(M)\cap\mathrm{X}^{\text{irr}}(M)
be the character induced by the holonomy of the closed
3
-manifold
M_{\bm{\mu}}
obtained from
M
by doing the hyperbolic Dehn filling along the system of simple closed curves
\bm{\mu}
on
\partial M.
If
\mathrm{H}(\gamma_{1}),\dots,\mathrm{H}(\gamma_{n})
are the logarithmic holonomies of the core curves
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}
of the solid tori added. Then

\mathrm{Tor}(M_{\bm{\mu}};\mathrm{Ad}_{\rho_{\bm{\mu}}})=\pm\mathbb{T}_{(M,\bm{\mu})}([\rho_{\bm{\mu}}])\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{4\sinh^{2}\frac{\mathrm{H}(\gamma_{i})}{2}}.

Next we recall a result on the computation of the adjoint twisted Reidemeister torsions from
[
75
]
.

Theorem 2.21
.

[
75
, Theorem 1.6 (2)]

Let
(M,\mathcal{T})
be a hyperbolic polyhedral
3
-manifold with the sets of edges and tetrahedra respectively
E
and
T,
and let
N
be the
3
-manifold obtained from the double of
M
by removing the double of the edges of
M.

Let
(l_{e})_{e\in E}
be the lengths of the edges of
M;
and for each
\Delta\in T
with edges
e_{1},\dots,e_{6},
let
\mathbb{G}_{\Delta}=\mathrm{Gram}(l_{e_{1}},\dots,l_{e_{1}})
be the Gram matrix of
(l_{e_{1}},\dots,l_{e_{6}}).
Let
\rho
be the holonomy representation of the hyperbolic cone metric on
N
obtained by doubling the hyperbolic polyhedral metric of
M,
let
\bm{m}
be the system of the meridians of a tubular neighborhood of the double of the edges, and let
(\theta_{e})_{e\in E}
be the cone angle functions in terms of the edge lengths of
M.

Then the value of the adjoint twisted Reidemeister torsion function
\mathbb{T}_{(N,\bm{m})}
at
[\rho]
can be computed as

\mathbb{T}_{(N,\bm{m})}([\rho])=\pm(-1)^{\frac{3|E|}{2}}2^{3|T|-|E|}\bigg(\frac{\partial\theta_{e_{i}}}{\partial l_{e_{j}}}\bigg)_{{}_{e_{i},e_{j}\in E}}\prod_{\Delta\in T}\sqrt{\det\mathbb{G}_{\Delta}},

where
\Big(\frac{\partial\theta_{e_{i}}}{\partial l_{e_{j}}}\Big)_{e_{i},e_{j}\in E}
is the Jacobian determinant of the cone angle functions
\theta_{e}
’s with respect to the edge lengths
l_{e}
’s evaluated at the edge lengths of
M.

2.10
Saddle point approximations

The proof of Theorems
1.2
,
1.3
and
1.9
relies on the following Saddle Point Approximations.

Proposition 2.22
.

[
76
, Proposition 6.1]

Let
D
be a region in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
and let
f(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})
and
g(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})
be holomorphic functions on
D
independent of
\hbar
. Let
f_{\hbar}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})
be a holomorphic function of the form

f_{\hbar}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})=f(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})+\upsilon_{\hbar}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})\hbar^{2}.

Let
S
be an embedded real
n
-dimensional closed disk in
D
and let
(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})
be a point on
S.
If

(i)

(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})
is a critical point of
f
in
D,

(ii)

\mathrm{Re}(f)(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})>\mathrm{Re}(f)(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})
for all
(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})\in S{\smallsetminus}\{(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})\},

(iii)

the Hessian matrix
\mathrm{Hess}(f)(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})
of
f
at
(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})
is non-singular,

(iv)

g(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})\neq 0,
and

(v)

|\upsilon_{\hbar}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})|
is bounded from above by a constant independent of
\hbar
in
D,

(vi)

S
is smoothly embedded around
(c_{1},\dots,c_{n}).

then

\begin{split}\int_{S}g(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})&e^{\frac{f_{\hbar}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})}{\hbar}}dz_{1}\dots dz_{n}\\
&=\Big(2\pi\hbar\Big)^{\frac{n}{2}}\frac{g(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})}{\sqrt{\det\big(-\mathrm{Hess}(f)(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})\big)}}e^{\frac{f(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})}{\hbar}}\Big(1+O\big(\hbar\big)\Big).\end{split}

Remark 2.23
.

In
[
76
, Proposition 6.1]
, condition (3) is stated as: the domain
\{\bm{z}\in D\ |\ \mathrm{Re}{f}(\bm{z})<\mathrm{Re}{f}(\bm{c})\}
deformation retracts to
S{\smallsetminus}\{\bm{c}\},
which is automatically satisfied when
S
is smoothly embedded around
\bm{c}
as stated in condition (vi) here.

Proposition 2.24
.

[
39
, Proposition 5.2]

Let
D
be a region in
\mathbb{C}
and let
f(z)
and
g(z)
be holomorphic functions on
D
independent of
\hbar
. Let
f_{\hbar}(z)
be a holomorphic function of the form

f_{\hbar}(z)=f(z)+\upsilon_{\hbar}(z)\hbar^{2}.

Let
S
be a curve in
D
and let
c
be a point on
S.
If

(i)

f^{\prime}(c)=0,

(ii)

\mathrm{Re}(f)(c)>\mathrm{Re}(f)(z)
for all
z\in S{\smallsetminus}\{c\},

(iii)

f^{\prime\prime}(c)=0,

(iv)

f^{\prime\prime\prime}(c)\neq 0,

(v)

g(c)\neq 0,
and

(vi)

|\upsilon_{\hbar}(z)|
is bounded from above by a constant independent of
\hbar
in
D,

(vii)

S
connects two components of the region
\{z\in D\ |\ \mathrm{Re}{f}(z)<\mathrm{Re}{f}(c)\},

then there is a nonzero constant
C
such that

\int_{S}g(z)e^{\frac{f_{\hbar}(z)}{\hbar}}dz=C(2\pi\hbar)^{\frac{1}{3}}\frac{g(c)}{\sqrt[3]{-f^{\prime\prime\prime}(c)}}e^{\frac{f(c)}{\hbar}}\Big(1+O\big(\hbar\big)\Big).

The precise value of the constant
C
can be found in
[
39
, Proposition 5.2]
.

3
Asymptotics of the
\mathrm{U}_{q\tilde{q}}\mathfrak{sl}(2;\mathbb{R})

6j
-symbol

In this section we prove Theorem
1.2
and Theorem
1.3
using the saddle point approximations. Recall the variables
a_{k},u,t_{i},q_{j},
in Definition
1.1
of
\bigg\{\begin{matrix}a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{3}\\
a_{4}&a_{5}&a_{6}\end{matrix}\bigg\}_{b}
. We introduce a new set of variables
\alpha_{k}
,
\xi
,
\tau_{i}
,
\eta_{j}
as follows:

\displaystyle\alpha_{k}=\pi ba_{k}-\frac{\pi b^{2}}{2}\textrm{ for }k\in\{1,\dots,6\},\quad\xi=\pi bu,

(3.1)

\displaystyle\tau_{i}=\pi bt_{i}-\frac{3\pi b^{2}}{2}\textrm{ for }i\in\{1,2,3,4\},\quad\text{and}\quad\eta_{j}=\pi bq_{j}-2\pi b^{2}\textrm{ for }j\in\{1,2,3,4\}.

(3.2)

Let
\bm{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{6})
and let

\begin{split}U_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)=&-\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{4}\sum_{j=1}^{4}\log S_{b}(q_{j}-t_{i})+2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{4}\log S_{b}(u-t_{i})+2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{4}\log S_{b}(q_{j}-u).\end{split}

Recall
L(x)
from (
2.7
). Define

\displaystyle U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{4}\sum_{j=1}^{4}L(\eta_{j}-\tau_{i})+\sum_{i=1}^{4}L(\xi-\tau_{i})+\sum_{j=1}^{4}L(\eta_{j}-\xi).

(3.3)

\displaystyle\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)=8\pi^{2}+14\pi\sum_{k=1}^{6}\alpha_{k}-28\pi\xi-4\pi\mathbf{i}\sum_{i=1}^{4}\log\Big(1-e^{2\mathbf{i}(\xi-\tau_{i})}\Big)+3\pi\mathbf{i}\sum_{j=1}^{4}\log\Big(1-e^{2\mathbf{i}(\eta_{j}-\xi)}\Big).

(3.4)

\displaystyle\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)=\frac{U_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)-\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}-U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)}{b^{4}}.

(3.5)

Then by (
1.3
) and (
3.5
), after change of variable we have

\begin{split}\bigg\{\begin{matrix}a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{3}\\
a_{4}&a_{5}&a_{6}\end{matrix}\bigg\}_{b}=&\frac{1}{\pi b}\int_{\Gamma}\exp\bigg(\frac{U_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\bigg)d\xi\\
=&\frac{1}{\pi b}\int_{\Gamma}\exp\bigg(\frac{U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)+\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}+\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)b^{4}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\bigg)d\xi,\\
\end{split}

(3.6)

where
\Gamma
is a contour approaching
2\pi+2\pi b^{2}+\mathbf{i}\mathbb{R}
near infinity, and passes the real axis in the interval

\Big(\frac{3\pi}{2}+\frac{3\pi b^{2}}{2},2\pi+2\pi b^{2}\Big)
.

We prove Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
by applying the saddle point approximations to the integral in (
3.6
). To meet the conditions, we need to choose a suitable contour in (
3.6
) passing through the critical points.
We also need to choose a proper neighborhood of the critical points so that the integral inside this neighborhood dominates the asymptotics, and the integral outside this neighborhood is negligible. This requires the functions
U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)
,
\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)
, and
\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)b^{4}
to satisfy certain desired properties, which we will prove in Section
3.1
. We then prove Theorem
1.2
in Section
3.2
by applying Proposition
2.22
; and prove Theorem
1.3
in Section
3.3
by applying Proposition
2.24
.

3.1
Properties of relevant functions

Recall from section
2.5
that
\mathcal{L}\subset{\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}}^{6}
is the space of all truncated hyperideal tetrahedra parametrized by the edge lengths. Let

D=\Big\{\xi\in\mathbb{C}\ \Big|\ \frac{3\pi}{2}<\mathrm{Re}(\xi)<2\pi\Big\}\quad\textrm{and hence}\quad\partial D=\Big\{\xi\in\mathbb{C}\ \Big|\ \mathrm{Re}(\xi)=\frac{3\pi}{2}\ \text{or}\ \mathrm{Re}(\xi)=2\pi\Big\}.

Set
\alpha_{k}=\frac{\pi}{2}+\mathbf{i}\frac{l_{k}}{2}
in (
3.1
) so that
a_{k}=\frac{Q}{2}+\mathbf{i}\frac{l_{k}}{2}
as in Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
.

Proposition 3.1
.

For
k\in\{1,\dots,6\},
let
\alpha_{k}=\frac{\pi}{2}\pm\mathbf{i}\frac{l_{k}}{2}
with
l_{k}\in{\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}},
and let
\bm{l}=(l_{1},\dots,l_{6}).

(1)

If
\bm{l}\in\mathcal{L},
then the function
U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)
has a unique critical point
\xi^{*}
(i.e.,
U^{\prime}_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)=0
) in the domain
D
.
Furthermore,
\xi^{*}
is non-degenerate (i.e.,
U^{\prime\prime}_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)\neq 0
). Finally

U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})=-2\mathbf{i}\mathrm{Cov}(l_{1},\dots,l_{6}),

where
\mathrm{Cov}
is the co-volume function as in (
2.24
).

(2)

If
\bm{l}\in\mathcal{\partial}L,
then
there exists
\xi^{*}\in\partial D
such that
\xi^{*}
is the unique critical point of
U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)
in a neighborhood of
D\cup\partial D
. Furthermore,
\xi^{*}
is degenerate with
U^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})\neq 0
, and

U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})=-2\mathbf{i}\widetilde{\mathrm{Cov}}(l_{1},\dots,l_{6}),

where
\widetilde{\mathrm{Cov}}
is the extended co-volume function as in (
2.26
). Moreover, if
\alpha_{k}=\frac{\pi}{2}+\mathbf{i}\frac{l_{k}}{2}
for all
k\in\{1,\dots,6\},
then

\mathrm{Re}\xi^{*}=2\pi.

(3)

If
\bm{l}\in{\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}}^{6}{\smallsetminus}(\mathcal{L}\cup\partial\mathcal{L}),
then
there exist
\xi_{1}^{*}
and
\xi_{2}^{*}
lying on the same component of
\partial D
such that they are the two critical points of
U_{\bm{\alpha}}
in a neighborhood of
D\cup\partial D
. Furthermore, they are non-degenerate, and

\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi_{1}^{*})=\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi_{2}^{*})=-2\widetilde{\mathrm{Cov}}(l_{1},\dots,l_{6})

and

\mathrm{Re}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi_{1}^{*})=-\mathrm{Re}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi_{2}^{*}).

Moreover, if
\alpha_{k}=\frac{\pi}{2}+\mathbf{i}\frac{l_{k}}{2}
for all
k\in\{1,\dots,6\},
then

\mathrm{Re}\xi_{1}^{*}=\mathrm{Re}\xi_{2}^{*}=2\pi.

Proof.

We first show that in Case (1)
U_{\bm{\alpha}}
has a unique critical point in
D,
in Case (2)
U_{\bm{\alpha}}
has a unique critical point on
\partial D,
and in Case (3)
U_{\bm{\alpha}}
has two critical points on
\partial D,
following arguments adapted from
[
42
,
68
,
6
]
. (In fact our
U_{\alpha}
only differs from
[
6
, Eq. (3.2)]
by a sign.)
For
k\in\{1,\dots,6\},
let
u_{k}=e^{2\mathbf{i}\alpha_{k}},
and let
z=e^{-2\mathbf{i}\xi}.
Then by a direct computation or
[
6
, Eq. (3.9)]
we have

\frac{\partial U_{\bm{\alpha}}}{\partial\xi}=2\mathbf{i}\log\frac{(1-zu_{1}u_{2}u_{3})(1-zu_{1}u_{5}u_{6})(1-zu_{2}u_{4}u_{6})(1-zu_{3}u_{4}u_{5})}{(1-z)(1-zu_{1}u_{2}u_{4}u_{5})(1-zu_{1}u_{3}u_{4}u_{6})(1-zu_{2}u_{3}u_{5}u_{6})}\quad\quad(\mathrm{mod}\ 4\pi).

(3.7)

Here
\mathrm{mod}\ 4\pi
is due to
2\mathbf{i}\log
in (
2.7
). Then, as a necessary condition,
U^{\prime}_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)=0
implies

\frac{(1-zu_{1}u_{2}u_{3})(1-zu_{1}u_{5}u_{6})(1-zu_{2}u_{4}u_{6})(1-zu_{3}u_{4}u_{5})}{(1-z)(1-zu_{1}u_{2}u_{4}u_{5})(1-zu_{1}u_{3}u_{4}u_{6})(1-zu_{2}u_{3}u_{5}u_{6})}=1,

which is equivalent to the following quadratic equation (see
[
6
, Eq. (3.11)]
)

Az^{2}+Bz+C=0

with

\begin{split}A=&u_{1}u_{4}+u_{2}u_{5}+u_{3}u_{6}-u_{1}u_{2}u_{6}-u_{1}u_{3}u_{5}-u_{2}u_{3}u_{4}-u_{4}u_{5}u_{6}+u_{1}u_{2}u_{3}u_{4}u_{5}u_{6},\\
B=&-\Big(u_{1}-\frac{1}{u_{1}}\Big)\Big(u_{4}-\frac{1}{u_{4}}\Big)-\Big(u_{2}-\frac{1}{u_{2}}\Big)\Big(u_{5}-\frac{1}{u_{5}}\Big)-\Big(u_{3}-\frac{1}{u_{3}}\Big)\Big(u_{6}-\frac{1}{u_{6}}\Big),\\
C=&\frac{1}{u_{1}u_{4}}+\frac{1}{u_{2}u_{5}}+\frac{1}{u_{3}u_{6}}-\frac{1}{u_{1}u_{2}u_{6}}-\frac{1}{u_{1}u_{3}u_{5}}-\frac{1}{u_{2}u_{3}u_{4}}-\frac{1}{u_{4}u_{5}u_{6}}+\frac{1}{u_{1}u_{2}u_{3}u_{4}u_{5}u_{6}}.\\
\end{split}

Since
\alpha_{k}=\frac{\pi}{2}\pm\mathbf{i}\frac{l_{k}}{2}
for each
k,

u_{k}^{\pm 1}=e^{\pm 2\mathbf{i}\alpha_{k}}=-e^{\pm l_{k}}<0.
As a consequence,
A>0,

C>0
and
B
is real. Furthermore,
B^{2}-4AC=16\det\mathrm{Gram}(\bm{l})
as explained in
[
6
, Page 15]
.
Therefore by Proposition
2.7
we have

B^{2}-4AC\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccl}<0&\text{iff }&\bm{l}\in\mathcal{L},\\
=0&\text{iff}&\bm{l}\in\partial\mathcal{L},\\
>0&\text{iff}&\bm{l}\in{\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}^{6}}{\smallsetminus}(\mathcal{L}\cup\partial\mathcal{L}).\\
\end{array}\right.

(3.8)

Let

z^{*}=e^{-2\mathbf{i}\xi^{*}}=\frac{-B+\sqrt{B^{2}-4AC}}{2A}\quad\text{and}\quad{z^{**}}=e^{-2\mathbf{i}{\xi^{**}}}=\frac{-B-\sqrt{B^{2}-4AC}}{2A}

(3.9)

be the two roots of this quadratic equation.
Then by (
3.7
) we have

\frac{dU_{\bm{\alpha}}}{d\xi}\Big|_{\xi=\xi^{*}}=4k\pi\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{dU_{\bm{\alpha}}}{d\xi}\Big|_{\xi={\xi^{**}}}=4k^{\prime}\pi

for some integers
k
and
k^{\prime}.
We have the following case by case discussion:

(1)

For
\bm{l}\in\mathcal{L},
we claim that, among the two, only
\xi^{*}
is in
D
and is a critical point of
U_{\bm{\alpha}}.
Indeed, at the special case
l_{1}=\dots=l_{6}=0,
ie.,
\alpha_{1}=\dots=\alpha_{6}=\frac{\pi}{2},
we have

A=C=8
and
B=0.
Then
z^{*}=\mathbf{i}
and
{z^{**}}=-\mathbf{i}.
Hence we can choose
\xi^{*}=\frac{7\pi}{4}\in(\frac{3\pi}{2},2\pi);
and there is no
{\xi^{**}}\in(\frac{3\pi}{2},2\pi)
making
e^{-2\mathbf{i}{\xi^{**}}}=-\mathbf{i}.
A direct computation also shows that at this special case
k=0,
ie.,

\frac{dU_{\bm{\alpha}}}{d\xi}\Big|_{\xi=\frac{7\pi}{4}}=0.

Now for general
\alpha_{k}
’s, since
A>0,

B
is real, and
B^{2}-4AC<0
, we have
\mathrm{Im}z^{*}>0
. Moreover, we can choose a unique
\xi^{*}
with
\mathrm{Re}\xi^{*}\in(\frac{3\pi}{2},2\pi).

Since
k
is an integer valued continuous function on the connected space on
\mathcal{L},
it is constantly
0,
ie.,

\frac{dU_{\bm{\alpha}}}{d\xi}\Big|_{\xi=\xi^{*}}=0

(3.10)

and
\xi^{*}
is the unique critical point of
U_{\bm{\alpha}}
in
D.

(2)

For
\bm{l}\in\partial\mathcal{L},
since
A>0,

C>0,

B
is real, and
B^{2}-4AC=0
, we have
z^{*}\in\mathbb{R}{\smallsetminus}\{0\},
hence we can choose a unique
\xi^{*}
with
\mathrm{Re}\xi^{*}=\frac{3\pi}{2}
or
2\pi.
Again by the continuity of the integer valued function
k
on
\overline{\mathcal{L}},

k=0
and
\xi^{*}
is the unique critical point of
U_{\bm{\alpha}}
on
\partial D.

Moreover, if
\alpha_{k}=\frac{\pi}{2}+\mathbf{i}\frac{l_{k}}{2}
for each
k,
then
u_{k}-\frac{1}{u_{k}}=e^{l_{k}}-e^{-l_{k}}>0
for each
k.
As a consequence,
B<0
and
e^{-2\mathbf{i}\xi^{*}}=z^{*}=\frac{-B}{2A}>0
and hence
\mathrm{Re}\xi^{*}=2\pi.

Here we also observe that as
{z^{**}}=z^{*}
are double roots of the quadratic equation
Az^{2}+Bz+C=0,
we can choose
{\xi^{**}}=\xi^{*}\in\partial D.
Then we have
k^{\prime}=k=0
on
\partial\mathcal{L},
which will be used in Case (3).

(3)

For
\bm{l}\in{\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}}^{6}{\smallsetminus}(\mathcal{L}\cup\partial\mathcal{L}),
since
A>0,

C>0,

B
is real and
B^{2}-4AC>0
, we have
z^{*},{z^{**}}\in\mathbb{R}{\smallsetminus}\{0\}
with
z^{*}\neq{z^{**}},
hence we can choose
\xi_{1}^{*}=\xi^{*},

\xi_{2}^{*}={\xi^{**}}
with
\mathrm{Re}\xi^{*},

{\mathrm{Re}\xi^{*}}^{\prime}
equal to
\frac{3\pi}{2}
or
2\pi.
By the continuity of the integer valued functions
k
and
k^{\prime}
on
\mathbb{R}^{6}_{>0}{\smallsetminus}\mathcal{L}
and the fact that
k^{\prime}=k=0
on
\partial\mathcal{L}
as discussed at the end of Case (2), we have
k=k^{\prime}=0
on
\mathbb{R}^{6}_{>0}{\smallsetminus}(\mathcal{L}\cup\partial\mathcal{L})
and
\xi_{1}^{*}
and
\xi_{2}^{*}
are both critical points of
U_{\bm{\alpha}}
on
\partial D.
Next, if
B>0,
then both
\frac{-B\pm\sqrt{B^{2}-4AC}}{2A}<0
and hence both
\xi_{1}^{*}
and
\xi_{2}^{*}
are on
\Gamma_{\frac{3\pi}{2}};

and if
B<0,
then both
\frac{-B\pm\sqrt{B^{2}-4AC}}{2A}>0
and hence both
\xi_{1}^{*}
and
\xi_{2}^{*}
are on
\Gamma_{2\pi}.

Moreover, if
\alpha_{k}=\frac{\pi}{2}+\mathbf{i}\frac{l_{k}}{2}
for each
k,
then
u_{k}-\frac{1}{u_{k}}=e^{l_{k}}-e^{-l_{k}}>0
for each
k.
As a consequence,
A>0,

B<0
and
e^{-2\mathbf{i}\xi_{1}^{*}}=z^{*}=\frac{-B+\sqrt{B^{2}-4AC}}{2A}>0,

e^{-2\mathbf{i}\xi_{2}^{*}}={z^{**}}=\frac{-B-\sqrt{B^{2}-4AC}}{2A}>0
and hence
\mathrm{Re}\xi_{1}^{*}=\mathrm{Re}\xi_{2}^{*}=2\pi.

Next, we compute the value of
U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})
in Cases (1) and (2) and the value of
\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})
in Case (3) following the same argument as in
[
6
]
. For
\bm{\alpha}=(\frac{\pi}{2}\pm\mathbf{i}\frac{l_{1}}{2},\dots,\frac{\pi}{2}\pm\mathbf{i}\frac{l_{6}}{2}),
define

W(\bm{\alpha})=U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*}).

(3.11)

By a direct computation, we have

W\Big(\frac{\pi}{2},\dots,\frac{\pi}{2}\Big)=-8i\Lambda\Big(\frac{\pi}{4}\Big)=-2\mathbf{i}\mathrm{Cov}(0,\dots,0);

and it suffices to prove that

\frac{\partial W(\bm{l})}{\partial l_{k}}=-2\mathbf{i}\frac{\partial\mathrm{Cov}(\bm{l})}{\partial l_{k}}=-\mathbf{i}\theta_{k}\quad\text{for }\bm{l}\in\mathcal{L}\cup\partial\mathcal{L},

(3.12)

and

\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}W(\bm{l})}{\partial l_{k}}=-2\frac{\partial\widetilde{\mathrm{Cov}}(\bm{l})}{\partial l_{k}}=-\widetilde{\theta}_{k}\quad\text{for }\bm{l}\in\mathbb{R}^{6}_{\geqslant 0}{\smallsetminus}(\mathcal{L}\cup\partial\mathcal{L}).

(3.13)

Without loss of generality, let us verify it for
k=1.
By the same algebraic computation and argument in the proof of
[
6
, Theorem 3.5]
, one has

\frac{\partial W(\bm{l})}{\partial l_{1}}=-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{G_{34}-\sqrt{G_{34}^{2}-G_{33}G_{44}}}{G_{34}+\sqrt{G_{34}^{2}-G_{33}G_{44}}},

(3.14)

where
G_{ij}
is the
ij
-th cofactor of the Gram matrix
\mathrm{Gram}(\bm{l}).
We have the following case by case discussion:

(1)

If
\bm{l}\in\mathcal{L},
then

\cos\theta_{1}=\frac{G_{34}}{\sqrt{G_{33}G_{44}}}

and

\frac{\partial W(\bm{l})}{\partial l_{1}}=-\frac{1}{2}\log e^{2\mathbf{i}\theta_{1}}=-\mathbf{i}\theta_{1}.

(2)

If
\bm{l}\in\partial\mathcal{L}=X_{1}\cup X_{2}\cup X_{3},

then

\theta_{1}=\widetilde{\theta}_{1}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccl}\pi&\text{if}&\bm{l}\in X_{1},\\
0&\text{if}&\bm{l}\in X_{2}\cup X_{3},\\
\end{array}\right.

and as
\bm{l}
is a limit point of
\mathcal{L}
and by the continuity of
\frac{\partial W}{\partial l_{1}}
and Case (1),

\frac{\partial W(\bm{l})}{\partial l_{1}}=-\mathbf{i}\theta_{1}=-\mathbf{i}\widetilde{\theta}_{1}.

(3)

If
\bm{l}\in\mathbb{R}^{6}_{\geqslant 0}{\smallsetminus}(\mathcal{L}\cup\partial\mathcal{L})=\Omega_{1}\cup\Omega_{2}\cup\Omega_{3},
then by Proposition
2.7
,

G_{34}^{2}-G_{33}G_{44}=\sinh^{2}l_{1}\det\mathrm{Gram}(\bm{l})>0,

and hence

\frac{G_{34}-\sqrt{G_{34}^{2}-G_{33}G_{44}}}{G_{34}+\sqrt{G_{34}^{2}-G_{33}G_{44}}}
is real. Then by (
3.14
), the continuity of
\frac{\partial W}{\partial l_{1}},
Case (2) and Proposition
2.6
,

\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}W(\bm{l})}{\partial l_{1}}=-\widetilde{\theta}_{1}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccl}-\pi&\text{if}&\bm{l}\in\Omega_{1},\\
0&\text{if}&\bm{l}\in\Omega_{2}\cup\Omega_{3}.\\
\end{array}\right.

Putting (1)(2)(3) together, we verified that

\frac{\partial W(\bm{l})}{\partial l_{1}}=-\mathbf{i}\theta_{1}\quad\text{for }\bm{l}\in\mathcal{L}\cup\partial\mathcal{L},

and

\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}W(\bm{l})}{\partial l_{1}}=-\widetilde{\theta}_{1}\quad\text{for }\bm{l}\in\mathbb{R}^{6}_{\geqslant 0}{\smallsetminus}(\mathcal{L}\cup\partial\mathcal{L}).

Moreover, define

W^{*}(\bm{\alpha})=U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{**}).

Then by the same algebraic computation and argument in the proof of
[
6
, Theorem 3.5]
, one has

\frac{\partial}{\partial l_{k}}\big(\mathrm{Re}W(\bm{\alpha})+\mathrm{Re}W^{*}(\bm{\alpha})\big)=0

for all
k\in\{1,\dots,6\}.
As a consequence,
\mathrm{Re}W(\bm{\alpha})+\mathrm{Re}W^{*}(\bm{\alpha})=c
for some constant
c
and all
\bm{\alpha}
in
(\frac{\pi}{2}+\mathbf{i}\mathbb{R})^{6};
and a direct computation at
\bm{\alpha}=(\frac{\pi}{2},\dots,\frac{\pi}{2})
shows that
c=0.
This implies that

\mathrm{Re}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})=-\mathrm{Re}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{**}).

Finally, we show that the critical points in Cases (1) and (3) are non-degenerate, and in Case (2) the critical point is degenerate with a nonzero the third derivative. By Proposition
2.7
and Proposition
3.2
below, we have:

(1)

For
\bm{l}\in\mathcal{L},

U^{\prime\prime}_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})=-16\exp\Big(\frac{\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})}{\pi\mathbf{i}}\Big)\sqrt{\det\mathrm{Gram}(\bm{l})}\neq 0,

and
\xi^{*}
is a non-degenerate critical point.

(2)

For
\bm{l}\in\mathcal{\partial}L,

U^{\prime\prime}_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})=-16\exp\Big(\frac{\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})}{\pi\mathbf{i}}\Big)\sqrt{\det\mathrm{Gram}(\bm{l})}=0,

and
\xi^{*}
is a degenerate critical point.

By a direct computation, one sees that

U^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})=8\mathbf{i}\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{4}\frac{e^{2\mathbf{i}(\xi^{*}-\tau_{i})}}{\big(1-e^{2\mathbf{i}(\xi^{*}-\tau_{i})}\big)^{2}}-\sum_{j=1}^{4}\frac{e^{2\mathbf{i}(\eta_{j}-\xi^{*})}}{\big(1-e^{2\mathbf{i}(\eta_{j}-\xi^{*})}\big)^{2}}\bigg).

We observe that all the summands in the parentheses are real when
\mathrm{Re}\xi^{*}=\frac{3\pi}{2}
or
2\pi.

If
\mathrm{Re}\xi^{*}=\frac{3\pi}{2},
then
\frac{e^{2\mathbf{i}(\xi^{*}-\tau_{i})}}{(1-e^{2\mathbf{i}(\xi^{*}-\tau_{i})})^{2}}>0
and
\frac{e^{2\mathbf{i}(\eta_{j}-\xi^{*})}}{(1-e^{2\mathbf{i}(\eta_{j}-\xi^{*})})^{2}}<0
for all
i,j\in\{1,2,3,4\}.
Hence
\mathrm{Im}U^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})>0
and
U^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})\neq 0.
Similarly, if
\mathrm{Re}\xi^{*}=2\pi,
then
\frac{e^{2\mathbf{i}(\xi^{*}-\tau_{i})}}{(1-e^{2\mathbf{i}(\xi^{*}-\tau_{i})})^{2}}<0
and
\frac{e^{2\mathbf{i}(\eta_{j}-\xi^{*})}}{(1-e^{2\mathbf{i}(\eta_{j}-\xi^{*})})^{2}}>0
for all
i,j\in\{1,2,3,4\},
and hence
\mathrm{Im}U^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})<0
and
U^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})\neq 0.

(3)

For
\bm{l}\in{\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}}^{6}{\smallsetminus}(\mathcal{L}\cup\partial\mathcal{L})
and
i=1,2,

U^{\prime\prime}_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi_{i}^{*})=-16\exp\Big(\frac{\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi_{i}^{*})}{\pi\mathbf{i}}\Big)\big(\pm\sqrt{\det\mathrm{Gram}(\bm{l})}\big)\neq 0,

and
\xi_{1}^{*}
and
\xi_{2}^{*}
are non-degenerate critical points.

This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 3.2
.

For
\bm{l}=(l_{1},\dots,l_{6})\in{\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}}^{6},
let
\bm{\alpha}=\big(\frac{\pi}{2}\pm\mathbf{i}\frac{l_{1}}{2},\dots,\frac{\pi}{2}\pm\mathbf{i}\frac{l_{1}}{2}\big).
When
\bm{l}\in\mathcal{L}\cup\partial\mathcal{L},
let
\xi^{*}
be the critical point of
U_{\bm{\alpha}}
in
\overline{D}=D\cup\partial D;
and when
\bm{l}\in{\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}}^{6}{\smallsetminus}(\mathcal{L}\cup\partial\mathcal{L}),
let
\xi^{*}
be the critical point
\xi^{*}_{1}
of
U_{\bm{\alpha}}
and let
{\xi^{**}}
be the other critical point
\xi^{*}_{1}
of
U_{\bm{\alpha}}
on
\partial D.

Then

\frac{-U^{\prime\prime}_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})}{\exp\big(\frac{\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})}{\pi\mathbf{i}}\big)}=16\sqrt{\det\mathrm{Gram}(\bm{l})},

and

\frac{-U^{\prime\prime}_{\bm{\alpha}}({\xi^{**}})}{\exp\big(\frac{\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}({\xi^{**}})}{\pi\mathbf{i}}\big)}=-16\sqrt{\det\mathrm{Gram}(\bm{l})}.

Proof.

The proof follows the argument adapted from that of
[
9
, Lemma 3]
. Namely, from
U^{\prime}_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})=0,

we have

\sum_{i=1}^{4}\log\big(1-e^{2\mathbf{i}(\xi^{*}-\tau_{i})}\big)=\sum_{j=1}^{4}\log\big(1-e^{2\mathbf{i}(\eta_{j}-\xi^{*})}\big)-8i\xi^{*}+4\mathbf{i}\sum_{k=1}^{6}\alpha_{k}+2\pi\mathbf{i};

and plugging in (
3.4
), we have

\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})=2\pi^{2}+2\pi\sum_{k=1}^{6}\alpha_{k}-4\pi\xi^{*}-\pi\mathbf{i}\sum_{i=1}^{4}\log\big(1-e^{2\mathbf{i}(\xi^{*}-\tau_{i})}\big).

For
k\in\{1,\dots,6\},
let
u_{k}=e^{2\mathbf{i}\alpha_{k}},
and let
z^{*}=e^{-2\mathbf{i}\xi^{*}}
be the root of the quadratic equation as in (
3.9
). Then

\begin{split}-U^{\prime\prime}_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})=4\bigg(&\frac{z^{*}}{1-z^{*}}+\frac{z^{*}u_{1}u_{2}u_{4}u_{5}}{1-z^{*}u_{1}u_{2}u_{4}u_{5}}+\frac{z^{*}u_{1}u_{3}u_{4}u_{6}}{1-z^{*}u_{1}u_{3}u_{4}u_{6}}+\frac{z^{*}u_{2}u_{3}u_{5}u_{6}}{1-z^{*}u_{2}u_{3}u_{5}u_{6}}\\
&-\frac{z^{*}u_{1}u_{2}u_{3}}{1-z^{*}u_{1}u_{2}u_{3}}-\frac{z^{*}u_{1}u_{5}u_{6}}{1-z^{*}u_{1}u_{5}u_{6}}-\frac{z^{*}u_{2}u_{4}u_{6}}{1-z^{*}u_{2}u_{4}u_{6}}-\frac{z^{*}u_{3}u_{4}u_{5}}{1-z^{*}u_{3}u_{4}u_{5}}\bigg).\end{split}

As a consequence,

\begin{split}\frac{-U^{\prime\prime}_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})}{\exp\big({\frac{\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})}{\pi\mathbf{i}}}\big)}=&\frac{4(1-z^{*}u_{1}u_{2}u_{3})(1-z^{*}u_{1}u_{5}u_{6})(1-z^{*}u_{2}u_{4}u_{6})(1-z^{*}u_{3}u_{4}u_{5})}{z^{*2}u_{1}u_{2}u_{3}u_{4}u_{5}u_{6}}\\
&\bigg(\frac{z^{*}}{1-z^{*}}+\frac{z^{*}u_{1}u_{2}u_{4}u_{5}}{1-z^{*}u_{1}u_{2}u_{4}u_{5}}+\frac{z^{*}u_{1}u_{3}u_{4}u_{6}}{1-z^{*}u_{1}u_{3}u_{4}u_{6}}+\frac{z^{*}u_{2}u_{3}u_{5}u_{6}}{1-z^{*}u_{2}u_{3}u_{5}u_{6}}\\
&\quad-\frac{z^{*}u_{1}u_{2}u_{3}}{1-z^{*}u_{1}u_{2}u_{3}}-\frac{z^{*}u_{1}u_{5}u_{6}}{1-z^{*}u_{1}u_{5}u_{6}}-\frac{z^{*}u_{2}u_{4}u_{6}}{1-z^{*}u_{2}u_{4}u_{6}}-\frac{z^{*}u_{3}u_{4}u_{5}}{1-z^{*}u_{3}u_{4}u_{5}}\bigg);\end{split}

and by a direct computation, this equals

4\bigg(3Az^{*}+2B+\frac{C}{z^{*}}\bigg)=4\bigg(Az^{*}-\frac{C}{z^{*}}\bigg),

which in turn equals,

4A(z^{*}-{z^{**}})=4\sqrt{B^{2}-4AC}=16\sqrt{\det\mathrm{Gram}(\bm{l})},

where
{z^{**}}
is the other root of the quadratic equation as in (
3.9
).

By a similar comutation, we have

\frac{-U^{\prime\prime}_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{**})}{\exp\big({\frac{\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}({\xi^{**}})}{\pi\mathbf{i}}}\big)}=4A({z^{**}}-z^{*})=-16\sqrt{\det\mathrm{Gram}(\bm{l})}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 3.3
.

(1)

In the domain
D,
the function
\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)
is concave down in
\mathrm{Im}\xi.

(2)

On
\partial D,
the function
\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)
is piecewise linear and concave down in
\mathrm{Im}\xi.

Proof.

By a direct computation, we have

\begin{split}\frac{\partial^{2}\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi^{2}}&\\
=-\sum_{i=1}^{4}&\frac{4\sin\big(2\mathrm{Re}(\xi-\tau_{i})\big)}{\cosh^{2}\mathrm{Im}(\xi-\tau_{i})-\cos^{2}\mathrm{Re}(\xi-\tau_{i})}-\sum_{j=1}^{4}\frac{4\sin\big(2\mathrm{Re}(\eta_{j}-\xi)\big)}{\cosh^{2}\mathrm{Im}(\eta_{j}-\xi)-\cos^{2}\mathrm{Re}(\eta_{j}-\xi)}.\end{split}

(3.15)

To see (1), for
\xi\in D,
we have
\mathrm{Re}(\xi-\tau_{i})\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
for
i\in\{1,\dots,4\},
and
\mathrm{Re}(\eta_{j}-\xi)\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
for
j\in\{1,\dots,4\}.
As a consequence,
\sin\big(2\mathrm{Re}(\xi-\tau_{i})\big)>0
for each
i,
and
\sin\big(2\mathrm{Re}(\eta_{j}-\xi)\big)>0
for each
j.
Then by (
3.15
),

\frac{\partial^{2}\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi^{2}}<0,\\

and
\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}
is concave down in
\mathrm{Im}\xi.

To see (2), for the concavity, we observe that
U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)
is a pointwise limit of a sequence of concave down functions, each of which is the restriction of
U_{\bm{\alpha}}
on the vertical lines
\{x_{n}+\mathbf{i}y\ |\ y\in\mathbb{R}\}
with
x_{n}\in(\frac{3\pi}{2},2\pi)
approaching
\frac{3\pi}{2}
or
2\pi.
For the piecewise linearity, we have
\mathrm{Re}(\xi-\tau_{i})\in\{0,\frac{\pi}{2}\}
for
i\in\{1,\dots,4\},
and
\mathrm{Re}(\eta_{j}-\xi)\in\{0,\frac{\pi}{2}\}
for
j\in\{1,\dots,4\}.
As a consequence,
\sin\big(2\mathrm{Re}(\xi-\tau_{i})\big)=0
for each
i,
and
\sin\big(2\mathrm{Re}(\eta_{j}-\xi)\big)=0
for each
j.
Then by (
3.15
),

\frac{\partial^{2}\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi^{2}}=0

unless
\xi=\tau_{i}
for some
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
or
\xi=\eta_{j}
for some
j\in\{1,2,3,4\},
which are the singular points.
∎

To understand the function
\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}},
in the following Propositions
3.4
,
3.6
,
3.7
and Corollaries
3.5
,
3.8
we study various behaviors of its partial derivatives. For
c\in[\frac{3\pi}{2},2\pi],
we consider the contour

\Gamma_{c}=\Big\{\xi\in\overline{D}\ \Big|\ \mathrm{Re}\xi=c\Big\}.

Proposition 3.4
.

On
\Gamma_{c},
we have

\lim_{\mathrm{Im}\xi\to+\infty}\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}=-4\pi\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{\mathrm{Im}\xi\to-\infty}\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}=4\pi.

Proof.

By a direct computation, we have

\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}L(\alpha+\mathbf{i}l)}{\partial l}=-2\arg\Big(1-e^{-2l+2\mathbf{i}\alpha}\Big)+2\alpha,

and

\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}L(\alpha-\mathbf{i}l)}{\partial l}=2\arg\Big(1-e^{2l+2\mathbf{i}\alpha}\Big)-2\alpha.

As a consequence, for a fixed
\alpha\in[0,\frac{\pi}{2}],
we have

\lim_{l\to+\infty}\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}L(\alpha+\mathbf{i}l)}{\partial l}=-2\arg(1)+2\alpha=2\alpha,

\lim_{l\to-\infty}\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}L(\alpha+\mathbf{i}l)}{\partial l}=-2\arg(-e^{2\mathbf{i}\alpha})+2\alpha=2\pi-2\alpha;

and

\lim_{l\to+\infty}\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}L(\alpha-\mathbf{i}l)}{\partial l}=2\arg(-e^{2\mathbf{i}\alpha})-2\alpha=2\alpha-2\pi,

\lim_{l\to-\infty}\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}L(\alpha-\mathbf{i}l)}{\partial l}=2\arg(1)-2\alpha=-2\alpha.

Then

\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}=\sum_{i=1}^{4}\frac{\partial L(\xi-\tau_{i})}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}+\sum_{j=1}^{4}\frac{\partial L(\eta_{j}-\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi},

with

\lim_{\mathrm{Im}\xi\to+\infty}\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}=\sum_{i=1}^{4}2(\xi-\tau_{i})+\sum_{j=1}^{4}\big(2(\eta_{j}-\xi)-2\pi\big)=-4\pi,

and

\lim_{\mathrm{Im}\xi\to-\infty}\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}=\sum_{i=1}^{4}\big(2\pi-2(\xi-\tau_{i}))+\sum_{j=1}^{4}\big(-2(\eta_{j}-\xi)\big)=4\pi.\qed

An immediate consequence of Propositions
3.3
and
3.4
is the following

Corollary 3.5
.

For each
c\in(\frac{3\pi}{2},2\pi),

\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)
has a unique critical point
\xi^{*}_{c}
on
\Gamma_{c},
which achieves the maximum.

In the rest of this section, we will re-index
\tau_{i}
’s so that
\mathrm{Im}\tau_{i_{1}}\leqslant\mathrm{Im}\tau_{i_{2}}\leqslant\mathrm{Im}\tau_{i_{3}}\leqslant\mathrm{Im}\tau_{i_{4}},
and re-index
\eta_{j}
’s so that
\mathrm{Im}\eta_{j_{1}}\leqslant\mathrm{Im}\eta_{j_{2}}\leqslant\mathrm{Im}\eta_{j_{3}}\leqslant\mathrm{Im}\eta_{j_{4}},
and as a notion let
\mathrm{Im}\tau_{i_{0}}=\mathrm{Im}\eta_{j_{0}}=-\infty
and
\mathrm{Im}\tau_{i_{5}}=\mathrm{Im}\eta_{j_{5}}=+\infty.

Proposition 3.6
.

For
k\in\{0,\dots,4\}
and
\xi\in\Gamma_{\frac{3\pi}{2}}
with
\mathrm{Im}\xi\in(\mathrm{Im}{\tau_{i_{k}}},\mathrm{Im}\tau_{i_{k+1}})
or
\xi\in\Gamma_{2\pi}
with
\mathrm{Im}\xi\in(\mathrm{Im}{\eta_{j_{k}}},\mathrm{Im}\eta_{j_{k+1}}),

\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}=4\pi-2k\pi.

In particular, for
k=2,

\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}=0.

Proof.

We have

\frac{\partial L(\xi-\tau_{i})}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}=-2\arg\Big(1-e^{-2\mathrm{Im}(\xi-\tau_{i})+2\mathbf{i}\mathrm{Re}(\xi-\tau_{i})}\Big)+2\mathrm{Re}(\xi-\tau_{i})

for
i\in\{1,2,3,4\},
and

\frac{\partial L(\eta_{j}-\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}=2\arg\Big(1-e^{-2\mathrm{Im}(\eta_{j}-\xi)+2\mathbf{i}\mathrm{Re}(\eta_{j}-\xi)}\Big)-2\mathrm{Re}(\eta_{j}-\xi)

for
j\in\{1,2,3,4\}.

If
\xi\in\Gamma_{\frac{3\pi}{2}},
then
\mathrm{Re}(\xi-\tau_{i})=0.
In addition, if
\mathrm{Im}\xi>\mathrm{Im}\tau_{i},
then

1-e^{-2\mathrm{Im}(\xi-\tau_{i})+2\mathbf{i}\mathrm{Re}(\xi-\tau_{i})}=1-e^{-2\mathrm{Im}(\xi-\tau_{i})}>0;

and if
\mathrm{Im}\xi<\mathrm{Im}\tau_{i},
then

1-e^{-2\mathrm{Im}(\xi-\tau_{i})+2\mathbf{i}\mathrm{Re}(\xi-\tau_{i})}=1-e^{-2\mathrm{Im}(\xi-\tau_{i})}<0.

As a consequence, for
\xi\in\Gamma_{\frac{3\pi}{2}}
with
\mathrm{Im}{\tau_{i_{k}}}<\mathrm{Im}\xi<\mathrm{Im}{\tau_{i_{k+1}}},
we have

\frac{\partial L(\xi-\tau_{i})}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}=0

(3.16)

if
\mathrm{Im}\tau_{i}\leqslant\mathrm{Im}\tau_{i_{k}},

and

\frac{\partial L(\xi-\tau_{i})}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}=2\pi

(3.17)

if
\mathrm{Im}\tau_{i}\geqslant\mathrm{Im}\tau_{i_{k+1}}.
In the latter case the argument of
1-e^{-2\mathrm{Im}(\xi-\tau_{i})+2\mathbf{i}\mathrm{Re}(\xi-\tau_{i})}
equals
-\pi
because as
\xi\in D
approaches
\Gamma_{\frac{3\pi}{2}},

\mathrm{Im}\xi
approaches
\frac{3\pi}{2}
from above. As a consequence,
1-e^{-2\mathrm{Im}(\xi-\tau_{i})+2\mathbf{i}\mathrm{Re}(\xi-\tau_{i})}
approaches the negative real axis from below and the argument approaches
-\pi.
On the other hand, we have
\mathrm{Re}(\eta_{j}-\xi)=\frac{\pi}{2}
and
1-e^{-2\mathrm{Im}(\eta_{j}-\xi)+2\mathbf{i}\mathrm{Re}(\eta_{j}-\xi)}>0,
which implies that

\frac{\partial L(\eta_{j}-\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}=-\pi

(3.18)

for each
j\in\{1,2,3,4\}.
Putting (
3.16
), (
3.17
) and (
3.18
) together, we have

\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}=(4-k)2\pi-4\pi=4\pi-2k\pi.

The case that
\xi\in\Gamma_{2\pi}
and
\mathrm{Im}\xi\in(\mathrm{Im}{\eta_{j_{k}}},\mathrm{Im}\eta_{j_{k+1}})
is very similar. Namely, we have
\mathrm{Re}(\xi-\tau_{i})=\frac{\pi}{2}
and
1-e^{-2\mathrm{Im}(\xi-\tau_{i})+2\mathbf{i}\mathrm{Re}(\xi-\tau_{i})}>0,
which implies

\frac{\partial L(\xi-\tau_{i})}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}=\pi.

(3.19)

One the other hand, we have
\mathrm{Re}(\eta_{j}-\xi)=0,

1-e^{-2\mathrm{Im}(\eta_{j}-\xi)+2\mathbf{i}\mathrm{Re}(\eta_{j}-\xi)}<0

when
\mathrm{Im}\xi>\mathrm{Im}\eta_{j},

and

1-e^{-2\mathrm{Im}(\eta_{j}-\xi)+2\mathbf{i}\mathrm{Re}(\eta_{j}-\xi)}>0

when
\mathrm{Im}\xi>\mathrm{Im}\eta_{j}.
As a consequence, we have

\frac{\partial L(\eta_{j}-\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}=-2\pi

(3.20)

if
\mathrm{Im}\eta_{j}\leqslant\mathrm{Im}\eta_{j_{k}},
where the argument of
1-e^{-2\mathrm{Im}(\eta_{j}-\xi)+2\mathbf{i}\mathrm{Re}(\eta_{j}-\xi)}
equals
-\pi
by a similar reason as in the previous case;
and

\frac{\partial L(\eta_{j}-\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}=0

(3.21)

if
\mathrm{Im}\eta_{j}\geqslant\mathrm{Im}\eta_{j_{k+1}}.

Putting (
3.19
), (
3.20
) and (
3.21
) together, we have

\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}=4\pi-2k\pi.\qed

Proposition 3.7
.

For all
i,j\in\{1,2,3,4\},
we have

\lim_{\xi\to\tau_{i}}\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Re}\xi}=-\infty\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{\xi\to\eta_{j}}\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Re}\xi}=+\infty.

Proof.

By a direct computation, we have

\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}L(\alpha+\mathbf{i}l)}{\partial\alpha}=2\log\Big|1-e^{2\mathbf{i}(\alpha+\mathbf{i}l)}\Big|+2l,

\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}L(-\alpha+\mathbf{i}l)}{\partial\alpha}=-2\log\Big|1-e^{2\mathbf{i}(-\alpha+\mathbf{i}l)}\Big|-2l,

and
\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}L(\pm\alpha+\mathbf{i}l)}{\partial\alpha}
converges to a finite value as
\pm\alpha+\mathbf{i}l
tend to any points other than
k\pi
for any integer
k.

As a consequence,

\lim_{\alpha+\mathbf{i}l\to 0}\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}L(\alpha+\mathbf{i}l)}{\partial\alpha}=-\infty\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{-\alpha+\mathbf{i}l\to 0}\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}L(-\alpha+\mathbf{i}l)}{\partial\alpha}=+\infty.

Then

\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Re}\xi}=\sum_{i=1}^{4}\frac{\partial L(\xi-\tau_{i})}{\partial\mathrm{Re}\xi}+\sum_{j=1}^{4}\frac{\partial L(\eta_{j}-\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Re}\xi},

which implies

\lim_{\xi\to\tau_{i}}\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Re}\xi}=-\infty\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{\xi\to\eta_{j}}\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Re}\xi}=+\infty

for all
i
and
j
in
\{1,2,3,4\}.

∎

By Proposition
3.7
and the continuity of
\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Re}\xi},
we have the following

Corollary 3.8
.

There exists an
\delta>0
such that for each
i\in\{1,2,3,4\},
if
\xi\in\overline{D}
lying in the
\delta
-square neighborhood of
\tau_{i},
then

\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Re}\xi}<0;

and for each
j\in\{1,2,3,4\},
if
\xi\in\overline{D}
lying in the
\delta
-square neighborhood of
\eta_{j},
then

\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Re}\xi}>0.

I.e., in the intersection of the
\overline{D}
with the
\delta
-square neighborhood of
\tau_{i}
’s and
\eta_{j}
’s the vector field

\mathbf{v}(\xi)=\bigg(-\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Re}\xi},0\bigg)

points inside the region
D.

For
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{C},
we denote by
\mathrm{I}_{z_{1}z_{2}}
the line segment connecting
z_{1}
and
z_{2}.
By Proposition
3.6
, if
\bm{l}\in\partial\mathcal{L}
and
\xi^{*}
is the unique critical point of
U_{\bm{\alpha}},
then either
\xi^{*}\in\mathrm{I}_{\tau_{i_{2}}\tau_{i_{3}}}
or
\xi^{*}\in\mathrm{I}_{\eta_{j_{2}}\eta_{j_{3}}};
and if
\bm{l}\in{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}^{6}{\smallsetminus}(\mathcal{L}\cup\partial\mathcal{L})
and
\xi^{*}_{1}
and
\xi^{*}_{2}
are the critical points of
U_{\bm{\alpha}},
then either
\{\xi^{*}_{1},\xi^{*}_{2}\}\subset\mathrm{I}_{\tau_{i_{2}}\tau_{i_{3}}}
or
\{\xi^{*}_{1},\xi^{*}_{2}\}\subset\mathrm{I}_{\eta_{j_{2}}\eta_{j_{3}}}.
Among
\mathrm{I}_{\tau_{i_{2}}\tau_{i_{3}}}
and
\mathrm{I}_{\eta_{j_{2}}\eta_{j_{3}}},
we denote by
\mathrm{I}
the one that contains the critical points; and by Proposition
3.7
, the critical points cannot be the end points of
\mathrm{I}.

Proposition 3.9
.

Consider the vector field

\mathbf{v}(\xi)=\bigg(-\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Re}\xi},0\bigg).

(1)

If
\bm{l}\in\partial\mathcal{L},
then
\mathbf{v}
is nowhere vanishing and pointing inside
D
on
\mathrm{I}{\smallsetminus}\{\xi^{*}\}.

(2)

If
\bm{l}\in{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}^{6}{\smallsetminus}(\mathcal{L}\cup\partial\mathcal{L}),
then
\mathbf{v}
is nowhere vanishing on
\mathrm{I}{\smallsetminus}\{\xi^{*}_{1},\xi^{*}_{2}\},
pointing inside
D
on
\mathrm{I}{\smallsetminus}\mathrm{I}_{\xi^{*}_{1}\xi^{*}_{2}}
and pointing outside
D
on
\mathrm{I}_{\xi^{*}_{1}\xi^{*}_{2}}.

Proof.

For (1), by Proposition
3.6
,
\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}
is a constant on
\mathrm{I},
hence
\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}\equiv 0
on
\mathrm{I};
and since
\xi^{*}
is the only critical point of
\mathrm{U}_{\bm{\alpha}},
we have
\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}}{\partial\mathrm{Re}\xi}\neq 0
on
\mathrm{I}{\smallsetminus}\{\xi^{*}\}.
By Proposition
3.8
,
\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}}{\partial\mathrm{Re}\xi}<0
near the end points of
\mathrm{I}
when
\xi^{*}\in\Gamma_{\frac{3\pi}{2}},
and
\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}}{\partial\mathrm{Re}\xi}>0
near the end points of
\mathrm{I}
when
\xi^{*}\in\Gamma_{2\pi}.
Therefore,
\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}}{\partial\mathrm{Re}\xi}<0
on
\mathrm{I}{\smallsetminus}\{\xi^{*}\}
when
\xi^{*}\in\Gamma_{\frac{3\pi}{2}},
and
\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}}{\partial\mathrm{Re}\xi}>0
on
\mathrm{I}{\smallsetminus}\{\xi^{*}\}
when
\xi^{*}\in\Gamma_{2\pi}.

For (2), by Proposition
3.6
,
\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}
is a constant on
\mathrm{I},
hence
\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}\equiv 0
on
\mathrm{I};
and since
\xi_{1}^{*}
and
\xi_{2}^{*}
are the only critical points of
\mathrm{U}_{\bm{\alpha}},
we have
\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}}{\partial\mathrm{Re}\xi}\neq 0
on
\mathrm{I}{\smallsetminus}\{\xi_{1}^{*},\xi_{2}^{*}\}.
By Proposition
3.8
,
\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}}{\partial\mathrm{Re}\xi}<0
near the end points of
\mathrm{I}
when
\xi^{*}\in\Gamma_{\frac{3\pi}{2}},
and
\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}}{\partial\mathrm{Re}\xi}>0
near the end points of
\mathrm{I}
when
\xi^{*}\in\Gamma_{2\pi}.
Therefore,
\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}}{\partial\mathrm{Re}\xi}<0
on
\mathrm{I}{\smallsetminus}\mathrm{I}_{\xi^{*}_{1}\xi^{*}_{2}}
when
\xi^{*}\in\Gamma_{\frac{3\pi}{2}},
and
\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}}{\partial\mathrm{Re}\xi}>0
on
\mathrm{I}{\smallsetminus}\mathrm{I}_{\xi^{*}_{1}\xi^{*}_{2}}
when
\xi^{*}\in\Gamma_{2\pi}.
On the other hand, since
\mathrm{I}
is a level set of
\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}
passing through non-degenerate critical points, the monotonicity of the function along the direction orthogonal to the level set (which is the
\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathrm{Re}\xi}
direction in our case) changes sign on the two sides of each of the critical points. As a consequence, we have
\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}}{\partial\mathrm{Re}\xi}>0
on the interior of
\mathrm{I}_{\xi^{*}_{1}\xi^{*}_{2}}
when
\xi^{*}\in\Gamma_{\frac{3\pi}{2}},
and
\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}}{\partial\mathrm{Re}\xi}<0
on the interior of
\mathrm{I}_{\xi^{*}_{1}\xi^{*}_{2}}
when
\xi^{*}\in\Gamma_{2\pi}.

∎

For the proof of Theorem
1.3
, we need to consider the following bigger region
D_{\delta,c}=D^{\bm{\alpha}}_{\delta,c},
where
\bm{\alpha}\in\big(\frac{\pi}{2}+\mathbf{i}\mathbb{R}\big)^{6},

\delta>0
is sufficiently small and
c\in(\delta,\frac{\pi}{2}-\delta),
consisting of
\xi\in\mathbb{C}
either with
\mathrm{Re}\xi\in[\frac{3\pi}{2}+\delta,2\pi-\delta],
or with
|\mathrm{Im}(\xi-\tau_{i})|\geqslant\delta,

|\mathrm{Im}(\eta_{j}-\xi)|\geqslant\delta
for all
i,j\in\{1,2,3,4\}
and
\mathrm{Re}\xi\in[\frac{3\pi}{2}-c,2\pi+c].
See Figure
5
. The significance of this region is that for all
\xi\in D_{\delta,c},
all
\xi-\tau_{i}
and
\eta_{j}-\xi,

i,j\in\{1,2,3,4\},
are in the region
H_{\delta,K}
in Proposition
2.1
for any
K>c.

Figure 5
:
Region
D_{\delta,c}

Proposition 3.10
.

For
\delta>0
sufficiently small and
c\in(\delta,\frac{\pi}{2}-\delta),
there exists a constant
K=K_{\delta,c}>0
such that

\Bigg|\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}\Bigg|<K

for all
\xi\in D_{\delta,c}.

Proof.

We first observe that for
x\in H_{\delta,K},
there exists a constant
C_{\delta,K}>0
depending on
\delta
and
K
such that

\bigg|\frac{\partial\log\big(1-e^{2\mathbf{i}x}\big)}{\partial\mathrm{Im}x}\bigg|<C_{\delta,K}.

Indeed, by a direct computation,

\bigg|\frac{\partial\log\big(1-e^{2\mathbf{i}x}\big)}{\partial\mathrm{Im}x}\bigg|=\frac{e^{-\mathrm{Im}x}}{\sqrt{\cosh^{2}\mathrm{Im}x-\cos^{2}\mathrm{Re}x}}

which converges to
0
as
\mathrm{Im}x
tends to
+\infty,
and converges to
1
as
\mathrm{Im}x
tends to
-\infty.
Also, when
\delta\leqslant\mathrm{Re}x\leqslant\pi-\delta
the denominator
\sqrt{\cosh^{2}\mathrm{Im}x-\cos^{2}\mathrm{Re}x}\geqslant\sqrt{1-\cos^{2}\delta},
which is bounded from below; and when
|\mathrm{Im}x|\geqslant\delta
the denominator
\sqrt{\cosh^{2}\mathrm{Im}x-\cos^{2}\mathrm{Re}x}\geqslant\sqrt{\cosh^{2}\delta-1},
which is also bounded from below.
As a consequence, the function is bounded from both sides by a constant
C_{\delta,K}
on
H_{\delta,K}.

Then by a direct computation,

\begin{split}\Bigg|\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}\Bigg|=&\Bigg|-28\pi-4\pi\sum_{i=1}^{4}\mathrm{Re}\bigg(\frac{\partial\log\big(1-e^{2\mathbf{i}(\xi-\tau_{i})}\big)}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}\bigg)+3\pi\sum_{j=1}^{4}\mathrm{Re}\bigg(\frac{\partial\log\big(1-e^{2\mathbf{i}(\eta_{j}-\xi}\big)}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}\bigg)\Bigg|\\
\leqslant&\,28\pi+4\pi\sum_{i=1}^{4}\bigg|\frac{\partial\log\big(1-e^{2\mathbf{i}(\xi-\tau_{i})}\big)}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}\bigg|+3\pi\sum_{j=1}^{4}\bigg|\frac{\partial\log\big(1-e^{2\mathbf{i}(\eta_{j}-\xi}\big)}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}\bigg|<K\end{split}

for all
\xi\in D_{\delta,c},
where
K=28\pi+4\pi\sum_{i=1}^{4}C_{\delta,c}+3\pi\sum_{j=1}^{4}C_{\delta,c}=28\pi(C_{\delta,c}-1).

∎

More generally, for
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{C}^{6},
we let
D^{\bm{\alpha}}_{\delta,c}
be the region consisting of
\xi\in\mathbb{C}
with
\mathrm{Re}(\xi-\tau_{i})\geqslant\delta,

|\mathrm{Im}(\xi-\tau_{i})|\geqslant\delta
for all
i\in\{1,2,3,4\},

\mathrm{Re}(\eta_{j}-\xi)\geqslant\delta,

|\mathrm{Im}(\eta_{j}-\xi)|\geqslant\delta
for all
j\in\{1,2,3,4\}
and
\mathrm{Re}\xi\in[\frac{3\pi}{2}-c,2\pi+c].
In the proof of Theorem
1.9
, we need to consider the following region

\mathbf{D}_{\delta,c}=\Big\{(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})\in\mathbb{C}^{6}\times\mathbb{C}\ \Big|\ \frac{\pi}{2}-\delta\leqslant\mathrm{Re}\alpha_{k}\leqslant\frac{\pi}{2}+\delta\text{ for all }k\text{ in }\{1,\dots,6\},\text{ and }\xi\in D^{\bm{\alpha}}_{\delta,c}\Big\}.

The significance of this region is that for all
(\bm{\alpha},\xi)\in\mathbf{D}_{\delta,c},
all
\xi-\tau_{i}
and
\eta_{j}-\xi,

i,j\in\{1,2,3,4\},
are in the region
H_{\delta,K}
in Proposition
2.1
for any
K>c.

Proposition 3.11
.

For
b>0
and
\delta>0
both sufficiently small and
c\in(\delta,\frac{\pi}{2}-\delta),
there exists a constant
N=N_{\delta,c}>0
independent of
b
such that

\mathrm{Im}\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)\leqslant\big|\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)\big|<N

for all
(\bm{\alpha},\xi)
in the region
\mathbf{D}_{\delta,c}.

Proof.

Recall (
3.5
) that
\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)=\frac{U_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)-\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}-U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)}{b^{4}}.

By (
1.3
) and (
3.6
),

\begin{split}U_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)=&-\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{4}\sum_{j=1}^{4}\log S_{b}(q_{j}-t_{i})+2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{4}\log S_{b}(u-t_{i})+2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{4}\log S_{b}(q_{j}-u).\end{split}

First, for
(\bm{\alpha},\xi)\in\mathbf{D}_{\delta,c}
with
\delta
small enough,
\mathrm{Re}(\eta_{j}-\tau_{i})\in[\epsilon,\pi-\epsilon]
for all
i,j\in\{1,2,3,4\}
and for some
\epsilon>0.
Hence all
\eta_{j}-\tau_{i}
’s are in
H_{\delta,K}
for any
K>c,
and Proposition
2.1
with the choice of
B
therein tells us

\Big|2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}\log S_{b}(q_{j}-t_{i})-L(\eta_{j}-\tau_{i})\Big|<Bb^{4}.

Next, since
(\bm{\alpha},\xi)\in\mathbf{D}_{\delta,c},
all
\xi-\tau_{i}
’s and
\eta_{j}-\xi
’s are in the region
H_{\delta,K}
and
K>c.
Then for
b
small enough, all
\xi-\tau_{i}-2\pi b^{2}
’s and
\eta_{j}-\xi+\frac{3\pi b^{2}}{2}
’s are in
H_{\delta,K},
and Proposition
2.1
with the choice of
B
therein gives

\Big|2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}\log S_{b}(u-t_{i})-L\big(\xi-\tau_{i}-2\pi b^{2}\big)\Big|<Bb^{4}

and

\bigg|2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}\log S_{b}(q_{j}-u)-L\Big(\eta_{j}-\xi+\frac{3\pi b^{2}}{2}\Big)\bigg|<Bb^{4}.

As a consequence, we have

\Big|U_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)-V_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)\Big|<16Bb^{4},

(3.22)

where

V_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{4}\sum_{j=1}^{4}L(\eta_{j}-\tau_{i})+\sum_{i=1}^{4}L\big(\xi-\tau_{i}-2\pi b^{2}\big)+\sum_{j=1}^{4}L\Big(\eta_{j}-\xi+\frac{3\pi b^{2}}{2}\Big).

We are left to estimate the difference between
V_{\bm{\alpha},b}
and
U_{\bm{\alpha}}.
To this end, we use the Mean Value Theorem, and we have

\begin{split}V_{\bm{\alpha},b}-U_{\bm{\alpha}}=&\sum_{i=1}^{4}\Big(L\big(\xi-\tau_{i}-2\pi b^{2}\big)-L\big(\xi-\tau_{i}\big)\Big)+\sum_{j=1}^{4}\bigg(L\Big(\eta_{j}-\xi+\frac{3\pi b^{2}}{2}\Big)-L\Big(\eta_{j}-\xi\Big)\bigg)\\
=&-2\pi b^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{4}L^{\prime}\big(\xi-\delta_{1}-\tau_{i}\big)+\frac{3\pi b^{2}}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{4}L^{\prime}\big(\eta_{j}-\xi+\delta_{2}\big)\end{split}

for some
\delta_{1}\in[0,2\pi b^{2}]
and
\delta_{2}\in\big[0,\frac{3\pi b^{2}}{2}\big];
and by the Mean Value Theorem again, we have

\begin{split}V_{\bm{\alpha},b}-U_{\bm{\alpha}}=&-2\pi b^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{4}L^{\prime}\big(\xi-\tau_{i}\big)+\frac{3\pi b^{2}}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{4}L^{\prime}\big(\eta_{j}-\xi\big)\\
&+2\pi b^{2}\delta_{1}^{\prime}\sum_{i=1}^{4}L^{\prime\prime}\big(\xi-\delta_{1}^{\prime}-\tau_{i}\big)+\frac{3\pi b^{2}}{2}\delta_{2}^{\prime}\sum_{j=1}^{4}L^{\prime\prime}\big(\eta_{j}-\xi+\delta_{2}^{\prime}\big)\\
=&\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}+2\pi b^{2}\delta_{1}\sum_{i=1}^{4}L^{\prime\prime}\big(\xi-\delta_{1}^{\prime}-\tau_{i}\big)+\frac{3\pi b^{2}}{2}\delta_{2}\sum_{j=1}^{4}L^{\prime\prime}\big(\eta_{j}-\xi+\delta_{2}^{\prime}\big)\end{split}

for some
\delta_{1}^{\prime}\in[0,\delta_{1}]
and
\delta_{2}^{\prime}\in[0,\delta_{2}],
where
\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}
is as defined in (
3.4
). By a direct computation,

L^{\prime\prime}(x)=2+\frac{4e^{2\mathbf{i}x}}{1-e^{2\mathbf{i}x}},

whose norm is bounded from above for any
\delta>0
and
K>0
on the region
H_{\delta,K}.
As a consequence,

\big|L^{\prime\prime}\big(\xi-\tau_{i}\big)\big|
and
\big|L^{\prime\prime}\big(\eta_{j}-\xi\big)\big|
for all
i,j\in\{1,2,3,4\}
are bounded from above on the region
\mathbf{D}_{\delta,c}
for any sufficiently small
\delta>0.
For
b
sufficiently small so that
\xi-2\pi b^{2}
and
\xi+\frac{3\pi b^{2}}{2}
are in
D^{\bm{\alpha}}_{\delta,c},
we let
C
be the upper bound of
|L^{\prime\prime}|
on
\mathbf{D}_{\delta,c}.
Then

\Big|2\pi b^{2}\delta_{1}^{\prime}\sum_{i=1}^{4}L^{\prime\prime}\big(\xi-\delta_{1}^{\prime}-\tau_{i}\big)+\frac{3\pi b^{2}}{2}\delta_{2}^{\prime}\sum_{j=1}^{4}L^{\prime\prime}\big(\eta_{j}-\xi+\delta_{2}^{\prime}\big)\Big|<8\pi b^{2}\delta_{1}^{\prime}C+6\pi b^{2}\delta_{2}^{\prime}C\leqslant 25\pi^{2}Cb^{4},

where the last inequality come from that
\delta_{1}^{\prime}\leqslant\delta_{1}\leqslant 2\pi b^{2}
and
\delta_{2}^{\prime}\leqslant\delta_{2}\leqslant\frac{3\pi b^{2}}{2}.

As a consequence,

\Big|V_{\alpha}(\xi)-\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}-U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)\Big|<25\pi^{2}Cb^{4}.

(3.23)

Putting (
3.22
) and (
3.23
) together and letting
N=16B+25\pi^{2}C,
we have

\Big|U_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)-\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}-U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)\Big|<Nb^{4}

(3.24)

for all
(\bm{\alpha},\xi)
in
\mathbf{D}_{\delta,c},
and the result follows from (
3.5
).
∎

3.2
Proof of Theorem
1.2

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Recall the domain
D=\Big\{\xi\in\mathbb{C}\ \Big|\ \frac{3\pi}{2}<\mathrm{Re}(\xi)<2\pi\Big\}
. Let
d>0
be sufficiently small so that the region

B_{d}=\Big\{\xi\in\mathbb{C}\ \Big|\ |\mathrm{Re}\xi-\mathrm{Re}\xi^{*}|\leqslant d\text{ and }|\mathrm{Im}\xi-\mathrm{Im}\xi^{*}|\leqslant d\Big\}

lies in
D.
By Proposition
3.3
(1),
\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}
is strictly decreasing as
\xi
moves along
\Gamma^{*}
from
\xi^{*}
with
\mathrm{Im}\xi
approaching
+\infty,
and is strictly increasing as
\xi
moves along
\Gamma^{*}
from
\xi^{*}
with
\mathrm{Im}\xi
approaching
-\infty.

Therefore, there is an
\epsilon>0
such that

\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*}\pm\mathbf{i}l)<-2\mathrm{Col}(\bm{l})-4\epsilon

(3.25)

and for
l>d;
and by Propositions
3.3
(1) and
3.4
, there is an
L>d
such that

\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}(\xi^{*}+il)<-2\pi\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}(\xi^{*}-il)>2\pi.

(3.26)

for
l>L.

Let

\Gamma^{*}_{d}=\Gamma^{*}\cap B_{d}=\Big\{\xi\in\Gamma^{*}\ \Big|\ |\mathrm{Im}\xi-\mathrm{Im}\xi^{*}|<d\Big\}

and let

\Gamma^{*}_{L}=\Big\{\xi\in\Gamma^{*}\ \Big|\ |\mathrm{Im}\xi-\mathrm{Im}\xi^{*}|\leqslant L\Big\}.

We will show that, as
b\to 0,

(I)

\frac{1}{\pi b}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{d}}\exp\bigg(\frac{U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)+\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}+\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)b^{4}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\bigg)d\xi=\frac{e^{\frac{-\mathrm{Cov}(\bm{l})}{\pi b^{2}}}}{\sqrt[4]{-\det\mathrm{Gram}(\bm{l})}}\Big(1+O\big(b^{2}\big)\Big),

(II)

\bigg|\frac{1}{\pi b}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{L}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{d}}\exp\bigg(\frac{U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)+\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}+\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)b^{4}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\bigg)d\xi\bigg|<O\Big(e^{\frac{-\mathrm{Cov}(\bm{l})-\epsilon_{1}}{\pi b^{2}}}\Big),

and

(III)

\bigg|\frac{1}{\pi b}\int_{\Gamma^{*}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{L}}\exp\bigg(\frac{U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)+\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}+\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)b^{4}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\bigg)d\xi\bigg|<O\Big(e^{\frac{-\mathrm{Cov}(\bm{l})-\epsilon_{1}}{\pi b^{2}}}\Big)

for some
\epsilon_{1}>0,
from which the result follows.

For (I), we claim that all the conditions of Proposition
2.22
are satisfied by letting
\hbar=b^{2},

D=B_{d},

f=\frac{U_{\bm{\alpha}}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}},

g=\exp\big(\frac{\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\big),

f_{\hbar}=\frac{U_{\bm{\alpha}}+\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}b^{4}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}},

\upsilon_{h}=\frac{\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}},

S=\Gamma^{*}_{d}
and
c=\xi^{*}.

Indeed, by Proposition
3.1
(1),
\xi^{*}
is a critical point of
f=\frac{U_{\bm{\alpha}}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}
in
B_{d},
hence condition (i) is satisfied.

By Propositions
3.1
(1) and
3.3
(1),
\xi^{*}
is the unique maximum point of
\mathrm{Re}f=\frac{\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}}{2\pi}
on
\Gamma^{*}_{d},
hence condition (ii) is satisfied.

For conditions (iii) and (iv), since
\xi^{*}\neq\tau_{i}
and
\xi^{*}\neq\eta_{j}
for any
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
and
j\in\{1,2,3\},

\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})
is a finite value. As a consequence,
g(\xi^{*})=\exp\big(\frac{\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\big)\neq 0,
and condition (iv) is satisfied. Also by this, Proposition
2.7
and Proposition
3.2
,
U^{\prime\prime}_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})=-16{\exp\big(\frac{\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})}{\pi\mathbf{i}}\big)}\sqrt{\det\mathrm{Gram}(\bm{l})}\neq 0,
and condition (iii) is satisfied.

For condition (v), by Proposition
3.11
,
|\upsilon_{\hbar}(\xi)|=\big|\frac{\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\big|<\frac{N}{2\pi}
on
B_{d}.

For condition (vi), since
\Gamma^{*}
is a straight line, it is a smooth embedding near
\xi^{*}.

Finally, by Proposition
2.22
, Proposition
3.1
(1) and Proposition
3.2
, we have as
b\to 0,

\begin{split}\frac{1}{\pi b}\int_{\Gamma^{*}}\exp\bigg(\frac{U_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\bigg)d\xi=&\frac{(2\pi b^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\pi b}\frac{\exp\big(\frac{\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\big)}{\sqrt{-\mathrm{Hess}\frac{U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}}}e^{\frac{-2\mathbf{i}\mathrm{Cov}(\bm{l})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}}\Big(1+O\big(b^{2}\big)\Big)\\
=&\frac{e^{\frac{-\mathrm{Cov}(\bm{l})}{\pi b^{2}}}}{\sqrt[4]{-\det\mathrm{Gram}(\bm{l})}}\Big(1+O\big(b^{2}\big)\Big).\end{split}

This completes the proof of (I).

For (II) and (III), we have

\begin{split}&\bigg|\frac{1}{\pi b}\int_{\Gamma^{*}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{L}}\exp\bigg(\frac{U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)+\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}+\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)b^{4}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\bigg)d\xi\bigg|\\
\leqslant&\frac{1}{\pi b}\int_{\Gamma^{*}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{L}}\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)+\mathrm{Im}\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}+\mathrm{Im}\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)b^{4}}{2\pi b^{2}}\bigg)|d\xi|.\end{split}

For (II), by Proposition
3.11
, there is a
b_{1}>0
such that

\mathrm{Im}\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)b^{4}<Nb^{4}<\epsilon

(3.27)

for all
b<b_{1}
and for all
\xi\in\Gamma^{*};
and together with (
3.25
), we have

\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)+\mathrm{Im}\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)b^{4}<-2\mathrm{Cov}(\bm{l})-4\epsilon+\epsilon<-2\mathrm{Cov}(\bm{l})-2\epsilon

(3.28)

for all
b<b_{0}
and
\xi\in\Gamma^{*}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{d}.
By the compactness of
{\Gamma^{*}_{L}}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{d},
there exists an
M>0
such that

\mathrm{Im}\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)<M

(3.29)

for all
\xi\in\Gamma^{*}_{L}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{d}.
As a consequence of (
3.28
) and (
3.29
), we have

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{\pi b}\int_{\Gamma^{*}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{L}}\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)+\mathrm{Im}\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}+\mathrm{Im}\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)b^{4}}{2\pi b^{2}}\bigg)|d\xi|\\
<&\frac{2(L-d)e^{\frac{M}{2\pi}}}{\pi b}\exp\bigg(\frac{-\mathrm{Cov}(\bm{l})-\epsilon}{\pi b^{2}}\bigg)<O\Big(e^{\frac{-\mathrm{Cov}(\bm{l})-\epsilon_{1}}{\pi b^{2}}}\Big)\end{split}

for any
\epsilon_{1}<\epsilon.
This completes the proof of (II).

For (III), there is a
b_{0}\in(0,b_{1})
such that for all
b<b_{0},

\mathrm{Im}\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*}\pm\mathbf{i}L)b^{2}<\epsilon,

(3.30)

Kb^{2}<\epsilon
and
Nb^{2}<\epsilon,
where
K
and
N
are respectively the constants in Propositions
3.10
and
3.11
. We claim that, for
\xi\in\Gamma^{*}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{L}
and
b<b_{0},

\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)+\mathrm{Im}\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}+\mathrm{Im}\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)b^{4}<-2\big(\mathrm{Cov}(\bm{l})+\epsilon\big)-(2\pi-\epsilon)\big(|\xi-\xi^{*}|-L\big),

(3.31)

as a consequence of which, we have,

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{\pi b}\int_{\Gamma^{*}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{L}}\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)+\mathrm{Im}\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}+\mathrm{Im}\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)b^{4}}{2\pi b^{2}}\bigg)|d\xi|\\
<&\frac{1}{\pi b}\exp\bigg(\frac{-\mathrm{Cov}(\bm{l})-\epsilon}{\pi b^{2}}\bigg)\int_{\Gamma^{*}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{L}}\exp\bigg(\frac{-(2\pi-\epsilon)\big(|\xi-\xi^{*}|-L\big)}{2\pi}\bigg)|d\xi|\\
<&O\Big(e^{\frac{-\mathrm{Cov}(\bm{l})-\epsilon_{1}}{\pi b^{2}}}\Big)\end{split}

(3.32)

for any
\epsilon_{1}<\epsilon.

For the proof of the claim, by (
3.26
) and the choice of
b_{0},
for
l>L,
we have

\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}\Big(\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*}+\mathbf{i}l)+\mathrm{Im}\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*}+\mathbf{i}l)b^{2}\Big)<-2\pi+\epsilon,

and

\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}\Big(\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*}-\mathbf{i}l)+\mathrm{Im}\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*}-\mathbf{i}l)b^{2}\Big)>2\pi-\epsilon.

Together with the Mean Value Theorem, (
3.25
) and (
3.30
), we have

\begin{split}\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)+\mathrm{Im}\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}<&\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*}\pm\mathbf{i}L)+\mathrm{Im}\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*}\pm\mathbf{i}L)b^{2}-(2\pi-\epsilon)\big|\xi-(\xi^{*}\pm\mathbf{i}L)\big|\\
<&-2\mathrm{Cov}(\bm{l})-3\epsilon-(2\pi-\epsilon)\big(|\xi-\xi^{*}|-L\big)\end{split}

(3.33)

for all
\xi\in\Gamma^{*}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{L}.
Finally, putting (
3.33
) and (
3.27
) together, we have (
3.31
) and the first inequality in (
3.32
); and since

|\xi-\xi^{*}|-L\to+\infty

as
\xi\in\Gamma^{*}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{L}
approaches
\infty,
we have the second inequality in (
3.32
). This completes the proof of (III).

Putting (I), (II) and (III) together, we have as
b\to 0,

\bigg\{\begin{matrix}a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{3}\\
a_{4}&a_{5}&a_{6}\end{matrix}\bigg\}_{b}=\frac{e^{\frac{-\mathrm{Cov}(\bm{l})}{\pi b^{2}}}}{\sqrt[4]{-\det\mathrm{Gram}(\bm{l})}}\Big(1+O\big(b^{2}\big)\Big).\qed

3.3
Proof of Theorem
1.3

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

The proof is similar to the proof of Theorem
1.2
, namely, we will carefully choose an integral contour and use the Saddle Point Approximation. A seemingly natural choice of the contour is
\Gamma_{2\pi}
that passes through the unique critical point
\xi^{*}
of
U_{\bm{\alpha}}
in Case (a) and critical points
\xi^{*}_{1}
and
\xi^{*}_{2}
of
U_{\bm{\alpha}}
in Case (b) respectively given by Proposition
3.1
(2) and (3). However, there are issues with this choice as:

(a)

\Gamma_{2\pi}
pass through the points
\eta_{j}
’s where
U_{\bm{\alpha}}
is only continuous but not holomorphic, and

(b)

the critical points are not the unique maximum of
\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}
on the contour, hence condition (ii) of Proposition
2.22
is not satisfied.

We will resolve these issues as follows.

By Proposition
3.1
(2) and (3),
U_{\bm{\alpha}}
either has a unique critical point
\xi^{*}
or two critical points
\xi^{*}_{1}
and
\xi^{*}_{2}
lying on
\Gamma_{2\pi},
which pass through the non-holomorphic points
\eta_{j}
’s of
U_{\bm{\alpha}}.
To avoid these non-holomorphic points, we let
\delta>0
be sufficiently small so that Corollary
3.8
holds for all
\xi
in the
\delta
-square neighborhood of each
\eta_{j},
and consider the region
D_{\delta,c}
as depicted in Figure
5
and defined there-around. We observe that
\Gamma_{2\pi}
intersects the complement of
D_{\delta,c},
and we deform
\Gamma_{2\pi}
to the new contour
\Gamma^{\prime}
by pushing the parts out of
D_{\delta,c}
into the boundary of
D_{\delta,c}.
See Figure
6
.

Figure 6
:
Contour
\Gamma^{\prime}

By abuse of notation let
\xi^{*}
denote the unique critical point in Case (a) or either of the two critical points in Case (b).
By Propositions
3.6
, we see that
\mathrm{Im}\xi^{*}\in[\mathrm{Im}\eta_{j_{2}},\mathrm{Im}\eta_{j_{3}}];
and by Proposition
3.7
,
\xi^{*}
cannot be the end points of the intervals. Therefore,
\mathrm{Im}\xi^{*}\in(\mathrm{Im}\eta_{j_{2}},\mathrm{Im}\eta_{j_{3}}).

By Proposition
3.6
,
\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}
is a constant function on the line segment connecting
\eta_{j_{2}}
and
\eta_{j_{3}},
hence
\xi^{*}
is not the unique maximum point of
\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}
on
\Gamma^{\prime}
and we need to further deform it. We will do it case by case as follows.

For Case (a), we choose an
L>0
large enough so that

[\mathrm{Im}\eta_{i_{1}}-\delta,\mathrm{Im}\eta_{i_{4}}+\delta]\subset(\mathrm{Im}\xi^{*}-L+\delta,\mathrm{Im}\xi^{*}+L-\delta),

where
\delta>0
is as above, and consider the following smooth vector filed
\psi\mathbf{v}
on

D_{\delta,c},

where

\psi
is a

C^{\infty}
-smooth bump function on
D_{\delta,c}
satisfying

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\psi(\xi)=1&\text{if}&|\mathrm{Im}\xi-\mathrm{Im}\xi^{*}|\leqslant L-\delta\\
0<\psi(\xi)<1&\text{if}&L-\delta<|\mathrm{Im}\xi-\mathrm{Im}\xi^{*}|<L\\
\psi(\xi)=0&\text{if}&|\mathrm{Im}\xi-\mathrm{Im}\xi^{*}|\geqslant L\end{array}\right.

with
\delta>0
as above, and
\mathbf{v}
is the vector field on
D_{\delta,c}
defined by

\mathbf{v}=\bigg(-\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}}{\partial\mathrm{Re}\xi},0\bigg).

Now let
\Gamma^{*}
be the contour obtained from
\Gamma^{\prime}
by following the flow lines of
\psi\mathbf{v}
for a short time
t>0
.
See Figure
7
.
Then by Corollary
3.8
and the choice of
\delta,

\Gamma^{*}
stays inside
D_{\delta,c},
and by Proposition
3.9
(1),

\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)<\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})

(3.34)

for all
\xi\in\Gamma^{*}{\smallsetminus}\{\xi^{*}\}.

Figure 7
:
Contour
\Gamma^{*}
in Case (2)

Now in the computation of the
b
-
6j
symbol (
3.6
), we choose
\Gamma=\Gamma^{*}
as the contour of integral. Then we have

\bigg\{\begin{matrix}a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{3}\\
a_{4}&a_{5}&a_{6}\end{matrix}\bigg\}_{b}=\frac{1}{\pi b}\int_{\Gamma^{*}}\exp\bigg(\frac{U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)+\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}+\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)b^{4}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\bigg)d\xi.

Let
d>0
be sufficiently small so that the region

B_{d}=\Big\{\xi\in\mathbb{C}\ \Big|\ |\mathrm{Re}\xi-\mathrm{Re}\xi^{*}|<d,|\mathrm{Im}\xi-\mathrm{Im}\xi^{*}|<d\Big\}

is in
D_{\delta,c}.

Let

\Gamma^{*}_{d}=\Gamma^{*}\cap B_{d}=\Big\{\xi\in\Gamma^{*}\ \Big|\ |\mathrm{Im}\xi-\mathrm{Im}\xi^{*}|<d\Big\}

and let

\Gamma^{*}_{L}=\Big\{\xi\in\Gamma^{*}\ \Big|\ |\mathrm{Im}\xi-\mathrm{Im}\xi^{*}|\leqslant L\Big\}.

We will show that, as
b\to 0,
there exists an
\epsilon_{2}>0
such that

(I)

\frac{1}{\pi b}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{d}}\exp\bigg(\frac{U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)+\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}+\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)b^{4}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\bigg)d\xi=C(2\pi b^{2})^{\frac{1}{3}}\frac{\exp\big(\frac{\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\big)}{\sqrt[3]{-\frac{U^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}}}e^{\frac{-\widetilde{\mathrm{Cov}}(\bm{l})}{\pi b^{2}}}\Big(1+O\big(b^{2}\big)\Big)

for some nonzero constant
C,

(II)

\bigg|\frac{1}{\pi b}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{L}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{d}}\exp\bigg(\frac{U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)+\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}+\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)b^{4}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\bigg)d\xi\bigg|<O\Big(e^{\frac{-\widetilde{\mathrm{Cov}}(\bm{l})-\epsilon_{2}}{\pi b^{2}}}\Big),

and

(III)

\bigg|\frac{1}{\pi b}\int_{\Gamma^{*}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{L}}\exp\bigg(\frac{U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)+\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}+\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)b^{4}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\bigg)d\xi\bigg|<O\Big(e^{\frac{-\widetilde{\mathrm{Cov}}(\bm{l})-\epsilon_{2}}{\pi b^{2}}}\Big),

from which the result follows.

For (I), we claim that by letting
\hbar=b^{2},

D=B_{d},

f=\frac{U_{\bm{\alpha}}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}},

g=\exp\big(\frac{\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\big),

f_{\hbar}=\frac{U_{\bm{\alpha}}+\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}b^{4}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}},

\upsilon_{\hbar}=\frac{\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}},

S=\Gamma^{*}_{d}
and
c=\xi^{*},
all the conditions in Proposition
2.24
are satisfied.

Indeed, by Proposition
3.1
(2),
\xi^{*}
is a critical point of
f=\frac{U_{\bm{\alpha}}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}
in
B_{d},
hence condition (i) is satisfied.

By (
3.34
),
\xi^{*}
is the unique maximum point of
\mathrm{Re}f=\frac{\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}}{2\pi}
on
\Gamma^{*},
hence condition (ii) is satisfied.

For conditions (iii), (iv) and (v), since
\xi^{*}_{i}\in D_{\epsilon,c},

\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})
is a finite value. As a consequence,
g(\xi^{*})=\exp\big(\frac{\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\big)\neq 0,
and condition (v) is satisfied; and by Proposition
3.1
(3), conditions (iii) and (iv) are satisfied.

For condition (vi), by Proposition
3.11
,
|\upsilon_{\hbar}(\xi)|=\big|\frac{\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\big|<\frac{N}{2\pi}
on
B_{d}.

For condition (vii), since near
\xi^{*},

\Gamma^{*}
is obtained from a straight line segment
\Gamma^{\prime}
by moving along the flow lines of a smooth vector field
\mathbf{v},

\Gamma_{d}
is a smooth embedding around
\xi^{*}.

Finally, by Proposition
2.24
and Proposition
3.1
(2), we have as
b\to 0,

\frac{1}{\pi b}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{d}}\exp\bigg(\frac{U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)+\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}+\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)b^{4}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\bigg)d\xi=C(2\pi b^{2})^{\frac{1}{3}}\frac{\exp\big(\frac{\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\big)}{\sqrt[3]{-\frac{U^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}}}e^{\frac{-\widetilde{\mathrm{Cov}}(\bm{l})}{\pi b^{2}}}\Big(1+O\big(b^{2}\big)\Big)

for a nonzero constant
C.
This completes the proof of (I).

For (II) and (III), we have

\begin{split}&\bigg|\frac{1}{\pi b}\int_{\Gamma^{*}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{L}}\exp\bigg(\frac{U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)+\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}+\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)b^{4}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\bigg)d\xi\bigg|\\
\leqslant&\frac{1}{\pi b}\int_{\Gamma^{*}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{L}}\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)+\mathrm{Im}\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}+\mathrm{Im}\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)b^{4}}{2\pi b^{2}}\bigg)|d\xi|.\end{split}

For (II), by (
3.34
) and Proposition
3.1
(2),
\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)<-2\mathrm{Cov}(\bm{l})
for all
\xi\in\Gamma^{*}{\smallsetminus}\{\xi^{*}\};
and by the compactness of
{\Gamma^{*}_{L}}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{d},
there exists an
\epsilon>0
such that

\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)<-2\mathrm{Col}(\bm{l})-4\epsilon

(3.35)

for all
\xi\in{\Gamma^{*}_{L}}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{d}.
Also by the compactness, there is an
M>0
such that

\mathrm{Im}\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)<M

(3.36)

for all
\xi\in\Gamma^{*}_{L}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{d}.
By Proposition
3.11
, there is a
b_{1}>0
such that

\mathrm{Im}\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)b^{4}<Nb^{4}<\epsilon

(3.37)

for all
b<b_{0}
and
\xi\in\Gamma^{*};
and together with (
3.35
), we have

\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)+\mathrm{Im}\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)b^{4}<-2\mathrm{Cov}(\bm{l})-4\epsilon+\epsilon<-2\mathrm{Cov}(\bm{l})-2\epsilon

(3.38)

for all
b<b_{1}
and
\xi\in\Gamma^{*}_{L}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{d}.
Putting (
3.36
) and (
3.38
) together, we have

\begin{split}&\bigg|\frac{1}{\pi b}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{L}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{d}}\exp\bigg(\frac{U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)+\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}+\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)b^{4}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\bigg)d\xi\bigg|\\
<&\frac{2(L-d)e^{\frac{M}{2\pi}}}{\pi b}\exp\bigg(\frac{-\mathrm{Cov}(\bm{l})-\epsilon}{\pi b^{2}}\bigg)\\
<&O\Big(e^{\frac{-\mathrm{Cov}(\bm{l})-\epsilon_{2}}{\pi b^{2}}}\Big)\end{split}

for any
\epsilon_{2}<\epsilon.
This completes the proof of (II).

For (III), there is a
b_{0}\in(0,b_{1})
such that for all
b<b_{0},

\mathrm{Im}\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*}\pm\mathbf{i}L)b^{2}<\epsilon,

(3.39)

Kb^{2}<\epsilon
and
Nb^{2}<\epsilon,
where
K
and
N
are respectively the constants in Propositions
3.10
and
3.11
. We claim that, for
\xi\in\Gamma^{*}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{L}
and
b<b_{0},

\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)+\mathrm{Im}\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}+\mathrm{Im}\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)b^{4}<-2\big(\mathrm{Cov}(\bm{l})+\epsilon\big)-(4\pi-\epsilon)\big(|\xi-\xi^{*}|-L\big),

(3.40)

as a consequence of which, we have,

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{\pi b}\int_{\Gamma^{*}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{L}}\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)+\mathrm{Im}\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}+\mathrm{Im}\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)b^{4}}{2\pi b^{2}}\bigg)|d\xi|\\
<&\frac{1}{\pi b}\exp\bigg(\frac{-\mathrm{Cov}(\bm{l})-\epsilon}{\pi b^{2}}\bigg)\int_{\Gamma^{*}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{L}}\exp\bigg(\frac{-(4\pi-\epsilon)\big(|\xi-\xi^{*}|-L\big)}{2\pi}\bigg)|d\xi|\\
<&O\Big(e^{\frac{-\mathrm{Cov}(\bm{l})-\epsilon_{2}}{\pi b^{2}}}\Big)\end{split}

(3.41)

for any
\epsilon_{2}<\epsilon.

For the proof of the claim, by Proposition
3.6
and (
3.35
), for
l>L,
i.e.,
\xi^{*}\pm\mathbf{i}l\in\Gamma^{*}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{L},
we have

\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*}\pm\mathbf{i}l)\leqslant\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*}\pm\mathbf{i}L)<-2\mathrm{Cov}(\bm{l})-4\epsilon;

(3.42)

and by Proposition
3.6
again and the choice of
L
and
b_{0},
we have

\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}\Big(\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*}+\mathbf{i}l)+\mathrm{Im}\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*}+\mathbf{i}l)b^{2}\Big)<-4\pi+\epsilon,

and

\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathrm{Im}\xi}\Big(\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*}-\mathbf{i}l)+\mathrm{Im}\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*}-\mathbf{i}l)b^{2}\Big)>4\pi-\epsilon.

Together with the Mean Value Theorem, (
3.42
) and (
3.39
), we have

\begin{split}\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)+\mathrm{Im}\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}<&\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*}\pm\mathbf{i}L)+\mathrm{Im}\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*}\pm\mathbf{i}L)b^{2}-(4\pi-\epsilon)\big|\xi-(\xi^{*}\pm\mathbf{i}L)\big|\\
<&-2\mathrm{Cov}(\bm{l})-3\epsilon-(4\pi-\epsilon)\big(|\xi-\xi^{*}|-L\big)\end{split}

(3.43)

for all
\xi\in\Gamma^{*}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{L}.
Finally, putting (
3.43
) and (
3.37
) together, we have (
3.40
) and the first inequality in (
3.41
); and since

|\xi-\xi^{*}|-L\to+\infty

as
\xi\in\Gamma^{*}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{L}
approaches
\infty,
we have the second inequality in (
3.41
). This completes the proof of (III).

Putting (I), (II) and (III) together, we have

\lim_{b\to 0}\pi b^{2}\log\bigg\{\begin{matrix}a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{3}\\
a_{4}&a_{5}&a_{6}\end{matrix}\bigg\}_{b}=-\widetilde{\mathrm{Cov}}(\bm{l}).

in Case (a).

For Case (b), let
\xi_{\text{mid}}=\frac{1}{2}(\xi^{*}_{1}+\xi^{*}_{2})
be the mid-point of
\xi^{*}_{1}
and
\xi^{*}_{2},
and we choose an
L>0
large enough so that

[\mathrm{Im}\eta_{i_{1}}-\delta,\mathrm{Im}\eta_{i_{4}}+\delta]\subset(\mathrm{Im}\xi_{\text{mid}}-L+\delta,\mathrm{Im}\xi_{\text{mid}}+L-\delta),

where
\delta>0
is as above. Similar to Case (2), we consider the following smooth vector filed
\psi\mathbf{v}
on

D_{\delta,c},

where

\psi
is a

C^{\infty}
-smooth bump function on
D_{\delta,c}
satisfying

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\psi(\xi)=1&\text{if}&|\mathrm{Im}\xi-\mathrm{Im}\xi_{\text{mid}}|\leqslant L-\delta\\
0<\psi(\xi)<1&\text{if}&L-\delta<|\mathrm{Im}\xi-\mathrm{Im}\xi_{\text{mid}}|<L\\
\psi(\xi)=0&\text{if}&|\mathrm{Im}\xi-\mathrm{Im}\xi_{\text{mid}}|\geqslant L\end{array}\right.

with
\delta>0
as above, and
\mathbf{v}
is the vector field on
D_{\delta,c}
defined by

\mathbf{v}=\bigg(-\frac{\partial\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}}{\partial\mathrm{Re}\xi},0\bigg).

Now let
\Gamma^{*}
be the contour obtained from
\Gamma^{\prime}
by following the flow lines of
\psi\mathbf{v}
for a short time
t>0.
See Figure
8
.
Then by Corollary
3.8
and the choice of
\delta,

\Gamma^{*}
stays inside
D_{\delta,c},
and by Proposition
3.9
(2),

\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)<\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*}_{1})=\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi^{*}_{2})

(3.44)

for all
\xi\in\Gamma^{*}{\smallsetminus}\{\xi^{*}_{1},\xi^{*}_{2}\}.

Figure 8
:
Contour
\Gamma^{*}
in Case (3)

Now in the computation of the
b
-
6j
symbol (
3.6
), we choose
\Gamma=\Gamma^{*}
as the contour of integral. Then we have

\bigg\{\begin{matrix}a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{3}\\
a_{4}&a_{5}&a_{6}\end{matrix}\bigg\}_{b}=\frac{1}{\pi b}\int_{\Gamma^{*}}\exp\bigg(\frac{U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)+\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}+\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)b^{4}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\bigg)d\xi.

Let
d>0
be sufficiently small so that both of the regions

B_{i,d}=\Big\{\xi\in\mathbb{C}\ \Big|\ |\mathrm{Re}\xi-\mathrm{Re}\xi^{*}_{i}|<d,|\mathrm{Im}\xi-\mathrm{Im}\xi^{*}_{i}|<d\Big\},

i=1,2,
are in
D_{\delta,c}.

For
i=1,2
let

\Gamma^{*}_{i,d}=\Gamma^{*}\cap B_{i,d}=\Big\{\xi\in\Gamma^{*}\ \Big|\ |\mathrm{Im}\xi-\mathrm{Im}\xi_{i}^{*}|<d\Big\},

let

\Gamma^{*}_{d}=\Gamma^{*}_{1,d}\cup\Gamma^{*}_{2,d}

and let

\Gamma^{*}_{L}=\Big\{\xi\in\Gamma^{*}\ \Big|\ |\mathrm{Im}\xi-\mathrm{Im}\xi_{\text{mid}}|\leqslant L\Big\}.

We will show that, as
b\to 0,
there exists an
\epsilon_{3}>0
such that:

(I)

For
i=1,2,

\frac{1}{\pi b}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{1,d}}\exp\bigg(\frac{U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)+\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}+\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)b^{4}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\bigg)d\xi=\frac{e^{-\big(\frac{\mathrm{Re}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi_{1}^{*})}{2\pi b^{2}}+\frac{\pi}{4}\big)\mathbf{i}}}{\sqrt[4]{\det\mathrm{Gram}(\bm{l})}}e^{\frac{-\widetilde{\mathrm{Cov}}(\bm{l})}{\pi b^{2}}}\Big(1+O\big(b^{2}\big)\Big)

and

\frac{1}{\pi b}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{2,d}}\exp\bigg(\frac{U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)+\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}+\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)b^{4}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\bigg)d\xi=\frac{e^{\big(\frac{\mathrm{Re}U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi_{1}^{*})}{2\pi\bm{b}^{2}}+\frac{\pi}{4}\big)\mathbf{i}}}{\sqrt[4]{\det\mathrm{Gram}(\bm{l})}}e^{\frac{-\widetilde{\mathrm{Cov}}(\bm{l})}{\pi b^{2}}}\Big(1+O\big(b^{2}\big)\Big),

(II)

\bigg|\frac{1}{\pi b}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{L}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{d}}\exp\bigg(\frac{U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)+\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}+\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)b^{4}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\bigg)d\xi\bigg|<O\Big(e^{\frac{-\widetilde{\mathrm{Cov}}(\bm{l})-\epsilon_{3}}{\pi b^{2}}}\Big),

and

(III)

\bigg|\frac{1}{\pi b}\int_{\Gamma^{*}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{L}}\exp\bigg(\frac{U_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)+\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}(\xi)b^{2}+\nu_{\bm{\alpha},b}(\xi)b^{4}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\bigg)d\xi\bigg|<O\Big(e^{\frac{-\widetilde{\mathrm{Cov}}(\bm{l})-\epsilon_{3}}{\pi b^{2}}}\Big),

from which the result follows.

For (I), we claim that by letting
\hbar=b^{2},

D_{i}=B_{i,d}
for
i=1,2,

f=\frac{U_{\bm{\alpha}}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}},

g=\exp\big(\frac{\kappa_{\bm{\alpha}}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\big),
