Now let
\Gamma^{*}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta}}
be the contour obtained from
\Gamma^{\prime}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta}}
by following the flow lines of
\psi_{\Delta}\mathbf{v}_{\Delta}
for a short time
t>0.
See Figure
10
.
Then by Corollary
3.8
and the choice of
\delta,

\Gamma^{*}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta}}
stays inside
D_{\delta,c}^{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta}},
and by Proposition
3.9
,

\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta}}(\xi_{\Delta})<\mathrm{Im}U_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta}}\big(\xi^{*}_{\Delta}(\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta})\big)

(4.50)

for all
\xi_{\Delta}\in\Gamma^{*}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta}}{\smallsetminus}\{\xi_{\Delta}^{*}(\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta})\}.

Figure 10
:
Contour
\Gamma^{*}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta}}
when: (1)
\bm{l}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta}}\in\mathcal{L}
and
\Gamma_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta}}
does not intersect the complement of
D^{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta}}_{\delta,c},
(2)
\bm{l}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta}}\in\mathcal{L}
and
\Gamma_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta}}
intersects the complement of
D^{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta}}_{\delta,c}
or
\bm{l}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta}}\in\partial\mathcal{L},
and (3)
\bm{l}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta}}\in{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}^{6}{\smallsetminus}(\mathcal{L}\cup\partial\mathcal{L}).

Let

\Gamma^{*}_{\text{in}}=\bigg\{(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})\in B(\bm{\alpha}^{*}_{t_{0}},L_{0})\times\mathbb{C}^{T}\ \bigg|\ \xi_{\Delta}\in\Gamma^{*}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta}}\text{ for all }\Delta\text{ in }T\bigg\}.

Then
\Gamma^{*}_{\text{in}}
is obtained from
\Gamma^{\prime}
by following the flow lines of the smooth vector field

\mathbf{v}=\bigg((0)_{e\in E},\big(\psi_{\Delta}\mathbf{v}_{\Delta}\big)_{\Delta\in T}\bigg)

on the region
\mathcal{D}_{\delta,c}=\big\{(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})\in\big(\frac{\pi}{2}+\mathbf{i}{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\big)^{E}\times\mathbb{C}^{T}\ \big|\ \xi_{\Delta}\in D^{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta}}_{\delta,c}\text{ for all }\Delta\in T\big\}
defined before Proposition
4.7
for a short time
t>0,
hence is a connected domain, and is smoothly embedded in
\mathcal{D}_{\delta,c}
near the critical point
(\bm{\alpha}^{*},\bm{\xi}^{*})
of
\mathcal{W};
and by (
4.50
), Proposition
3.1
(1) (2) (3) and Proposition
2.15
, we have

\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{W}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})\leqslant\sum_{e\in E}2\pi l_{e}+\sum_{\Delta\in T}U\big(\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta},\xi^{*}_{\Delta}(\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta})\big)=-2\widetilde{\mathrm{Cov}}(\bm{l})<-2\mathrm{Vol}(M)

(4.51)

for all
(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})\in\Gamma^{*}_{\text{in}}{\smallsetminus}\{(\bm{\alpha}^{*},\bm{\xi}^{*})\}.

By (
4.41
), we have

\mathrm{I}_{\text{in}}=\frac{(-\mathbf{i})^{|E|}}{(\pi b)^{|E|+|T|}}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{\text{in}}}\bigg(\prod_{e\in E}4\sinh l_{e}\sinh\frac{l_{e}}{b^{2}}\bigg)\exp\Bigg(\frac{\sum_{\Delta\in T}U_{b}(\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta},\xi_{\Delta})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\Bigg)d\bm{\alpha}d\bm{\xi}.

(4.52)

To estimate this integral, we let
\delta>0
be as above, and let
d_{0}>0
be sufficiently small so that for any
\bm{\alpha}\in\Big(\Big[\frac{\pi}{2}-\delta,\frac{\pi}{2}+\delta\Big]+\mathbf{i}{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\Big)^{E}
with
|\bm{\alpha}-\bm{\alpha}^{*}|<d_{0},

\big[\mathrm{Im}\xi^{*}_{\Delta}-d_{0},\mathrm{Im}\xi^{*}_{\Delta}+d_{0}\big]\subset\big(\mathrm{Im}\eta_{j_{2}}(\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta})+\delta,\mathrm{Im}\eta_{j_{3}}(\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta})-\delta\big)

for all
\Delta
in
T.
Let
\bm{D}_{\delta,c}=\big\{(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})\in\big([\frac{\pi}{2}-\delta,\frac{\pi}{2}+\delta]+\mathbf{i}{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}\big)^{E}\times D^{T}\ \big|\ \xi_{\Delta}\in D^{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta}}_{\delta,c}\text{ for all }\Delta\in T\big\}
be the region defined before Proposition
4.8
, let

\bm{B}_{d_{0}}=\bigg\{(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})\in\bm{D}_{\delta,c}\ \bigg|\ |\bm{\alpha}-\bm{\alpha}^{*}|<d_{0},|\mathrm{Re}\xi_{\Delta}-\mathrm{Re}\xi^{*}_{\Delta}|<d_{0}\text{ and }|\mathrm{Im}\xi_{\Delta}-\mathrm{Im}\xi^{*}_{\Delta}|<d_{0}\text{ for all }\Delta\text{ in }T\bigg\},

and let

\Gamma^{*}_{d_{0}}=\Gamma^{*}_{\text{in}}\cap\bm{B}_{d_{0}}.

We claim that

(i)

\begin{split}&\frac{(-\mathbf{i})^{|E|}}{(\pi b)^{|E|+|T|}}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{d_{0}}}\bigg(\prod_{e\in E}4\sinh l_{e}\sinh\frac{l_{e}}{b^{2}}\bigg)\exp\Bigg(\frac{\sum_{\Delta\in T}U_{b}(\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta},\xi_{\Delta})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\Bigg)d\bm{\alpha}d\bm{\xi}\\
=&(2\mathbf{i})^{\frac{\chi(M)}{2}}\frac{e^{\frac{-\mathrm{Vol}(M)}{\pi b^{2}}}}{\sqrt{\pm\mathrm{Tor}(DM,\mathrm{Ad}_{\rho_{DM}})}}\Big(1+O\big(b^{2}\big)\Big),\end{split}

(4.53)

and

(ii)

\begin{split}\frac{(-\mathbf{i})^{|E|}}{(\pi b)^{|E|+|T|}}&\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{\text{in}}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{d_{0}}}\bigg(\prod_{e\in E}4\sinh l_{e}\sinh\frac{l_{e}}{b^{2}}\bigg)\exp\Bigg(\frac{\sum_{\Delta\in T}U_{b}(\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta},\xi_{\Delta})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\Bigg)d\bm{\alpha}d\bm{\xi}\\
<&O\bigg(e^{\frac{-\mathrm{Vol}(M)-\epsilon_{2}}{\pi b^{2}}}\bigg)\end{split}

(4.54)

for some
\epsilon_{2}>0.

Then putting (
4.52
), (i) and (ii) together, we have (II).

To see (i), we have

\begin{split}&\frac{(-\mathbf{i})^{|E|}}{(\pi b)^{|E|+|T|}}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{d_{0}}}\bigg(\prod_{e\in E}4\sinh l_{e}\sinh\frac{l_{e}}{b^{2}}\bigg)\exp\Bigg(\frac{\sum_{\Delta\in T}U_{b}(\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta},\xi_{\Delta})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\Bigg)d\bm{\alpha}d\bm{\xi}\\
=&\sum_{\bm{\lambda}\in\{-1,1\}^{E}}\frac{(-2\mathbf{i})^{|E|}}{(\pi b)^{|E|+|T|}}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{d_{0}}}\bigg(\prod_{e\in E}\lambda_{e}\sinh l_{e}\bigg)\exp\Bigg(\frac{\mathcal{W}^{\bm{\lambda}}_{b}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\Bigg)d\bm{\alpha}d\bm{\xi}.\end{split}

(4.55)

For
\bm{\lambda}=(1,\dots,1),
we claim that all the conditions of Proposition
2.22
are satisfied by letting
\hbar=b^{2},

D=\bm{B}_{d_{0}},

f=\frac{\mathcal{W}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}},

g
be defined by
g(\bm{z})=\prod_{e\in E}\sinh l_{e}\exp\big(\frac{\kappa(\bm{z})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\big),

f_{\hbar}=\frac{\mathcal{W}+\nu_{b}b^{4}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}},

\upsilon_{\hbar}=\frac{\nu_{b}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}},

S=\Gamma^{*}_{d_{0}}
and
c=\bm{z}^{*}=(\bm{\alpha}^{*},\bm{\xi}^{*}).

Indeed, by Proposition
4.1
,
\bm{z}^{*}
is a critical point of
f=\frac{\mathcal{W}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}
in
\bm{B}_{d_{0}},
hence condition (i) is satisfied.

By Proposition
4.1
and (
4.51
),
\bm{z}^{*}
is the unique maximum point of
\mathrm{Re}f=\frac{\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{W}}{2\pi}
on
\Gamma^{*}_{d_{0}},
hence condition (ii) is satisfied.

By Proposition
4.3
,
\det(-\mathrm{Hess}\mathcal{W}(\bm{z}^{*}))\neq 0,
and condition (iii) is satisfied.

Since
\xi_{\Delta}^{*}\in D_{\delta,c}^{\bm{\alpha}^{*}_{\Delta}}
for any
\Delta\in T,

\kappa(\bm{z}^{*})
is a finite value; and since
l^{*}_{e}>0
for each
e\in E,

g(\bm{z}^{*})=\prod_{e\in E}\sinh l^{*}_{e}\exp\big(\frac{\kappa(\bm{z}^{*})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\big)\neq 0,
and condition (iv) is satisfied.

For condition (v), by Proposition
4.8
,
|\upsilon_{\hbar}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})|=\big|\frac{\nu_{b}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\big|<\frac{N}{2\pi}
on
\bm{B}_{d_{0}}.

For condition (vi), since
\Gamma^{*}_{\text{in}}
is smoothly embedded around
\bm{z}^{*},

\Gamma^{*}_{d_{0}}=\Gamma^{*}_{\text{in}}\cap\bm{B}_{d_{0}}
is smoothly embedded around
\bm{z}^{*}.

Finally, by Proposition
2.22
, Proposition
4.1
, Proposition
4.3
and the fact that
\chi(M)=-|E|+|T|,
we have as
b\to 0,

\begin{split}&\frac{(-2\mathbf{i})^{|E|}}{(\pi b)^{|E|+|T|}}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{d_{0}}}\bigg(\prod_{e\in E}\sinh l_{e}\bigg)\exp\Bigg(\frac{\mathcal{W}_{b}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\Bigg)d\bm{\alpha}d\bm{\xi}\\
=&\frac{(-2\mathbf{i})^{|E|}}{(\pi b)^{|E|+|T|}}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{d_{0}}}\bigg(\prod_{e\in E}\sinh l_{e}\bigg)\exp\bigg(\frac{\kappa(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\bigg)\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathcal{W}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})+\nu_{b}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})b^{4}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\bigg)d\bm{\alpha}d\bm{\xi}\\
=&\frac{(-2\mathbf{i})^{|E|}(2\pi b^{2})^{\frac{|E|+|T|}{2}}}{(\pi b)^{|E|+|T|}}\frac{\exp\big(\frac{\kappa(\bm{z}^{*})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\big)\prod_{e\in E}\sinh l_{e}}{\sqrt{\det\Big(-\mathrm{Hess}\frac{\mathcal{W}(\bm{z}^{*})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\Big)}}e^{\frac{\mathcal{W}(\bm{z}^{*})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}}\Big(1+O\big(b^{2}\big)\Big)\\
=&(2\mathbf{i})^{\frac{\chi(M)}{2}}\frac{e^{\frac{-\mathrm{Vol}(M)}{\pi b^{2}}}}{\sqrt{\pm\mathrm{Tor}(DM,\mathrm{Ad}_{\rho_{DM}})}}\Big(1+O\big(b^{2}\big)\Big).\end{split}

(4.56)

For any
\bm{\lambda}\neq(1,\dots,1),
we have

\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{W}_{b}^{\bm{\lambda}}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})-\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{W}_{b}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})=-2\pi\sum_{e\in E}\big(1-\lambda_{e}\big)l_{e}<0

for all for
(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})
in
\Gamma^{*}_{d_{0}};
and by the compactness of
\Gamma^{*}_{d_{0}},
there is an
\epsilon>0
such that

\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{W}_{b}^{\bm{\lambda}}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})<\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{W}_{b}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})-2\epsilon

for all for
(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})
in
\Gamma^{*}_{d_{0}}.
Then by (
4.56
), for any
\bm{\lambda}\neq(1,\dots,1),

we have

\begin{split}&\Bigg|\frac{(-2\mathbf{i})^{|E|}}{(\pi b)^{|E|+|T|}}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{d_{0}}}\bigg(\prod_{e\in E}\lambda_{e}\sinh l_{e}\bigg)\exp\Bigg(\frac{\mathcal{W}^{\bm{\lambda}}_{b}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\Bigg)d\bm{\alpha}d\bm{\xi}\Bigg|\\
\leqslant&\frac{2^{|E|}}{(\pi b)^{|E|+|T|}}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{d_{0}}}\bigg(\prod_{e\in E}\sinh l_{e}\bigg)\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{W}^{\bm{\lambda}}_{b}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})}{2\pi b^{2}}\bigg)\big|d\bm{\alpha}d\bm{\xi}\big|\\
<&\frac{2^{|E|}}{(\pi b)^{|E|+|T|}}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{d_{0}}}\bigg(\prod_{e\in E}\sinh l_{e}\bigg)\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{W}_{b}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})-2\epsilon}{2\pi b^{2}}\bigg)\big|d\bm{\alpha}d\bm{\xi}\big|\\
<&O\bigg(e^{\frac{-\mathrm{Vol}(M)-\epsilon}{\pi b^{2}}}\bigg).\end{split}

(4.57)

Putting (
4.55
), (
4.56
) and (
4.57
) together, we have (
4.53
), completing the proof of (i).

To see (ii), for each
\bm{\alpha}\in B(\bm{\alpha}^{*}_{t_{0}},L_{0})
and
\Delta\in T,
we let

\Gamma^{*}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta},L_{1}}=\Big\{\xi_{\Delta}\in\Gamma^{*}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta}}\ \Big|\ |\mathrm{Im}\xi_{\Delta}-\mathrm{Im}\xi^{*}_{t_{0},\Delta}|\leqslant L_{1}\Big\}.

Then
\Gamma^{*}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta}}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta},L_{1}}
is the disjoint union of two rays.
Let

\begin{split}\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T}=&\bigg\{(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})\in\Gamma^{*}_{\text{in}}\ \bigg|\ \bm{\xi}\in\prod_{\Delta\in T}\Gamma^{*}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta},L_{1}}\bigg\}\\
=&\bigg\{(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})\in\Gamma^{*}_{\text{in}}\ \bigg|\ \xi_{\Delta}\in\Gamma^{*}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta},L_{1}}\text{ for all }\Delta\text{ in }T\bigg\},\end{split}

and for any proper subset
T_{0}
of
T,
we let

\begin{split}\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T_{0}}=&\bigg\{(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})\in\Gamma^{*}_{\text{in}}\ \bigg|\ \bm{\xi}\in\prod_{\Delta\in T_{0}}\Gamma^{*}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta},L_{1}}\times\prod_{\Delta\notin T_{0}}\big(\Gamma^{*}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta}}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta},L_{1}}\big)\bigg\}\\
=&\bigg\{(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})\in\Gamma^{*}_{\text{in}}\ \bigg|\ \xi_{\Delta}\in\Gamma^{*}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta},L_{1}}\text{ for all }\Delta\text{ in }T_{0},\text{ and }\xi_{\Delta}\notin\Gamma^{*}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta},L_{1}}\text{ for all }\Delta\text{ not in }T_{0}\bigg\}.\end{split}

Then we have the following partition

\Gamma^{*}_{\text{in}}=\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T}\cup\bigg(\bigcup_{T_{0}\subset T}\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T_{0}}\bigg)

of
\Gamma^{*}_{\text{in}}.

We will show that:

(
ii
.1)

\begin{split}\frac{(-\mathbf{i})^{|E|}}{(\pi b)^{|E|+|T|}}&\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{d_{0}}}\bigg(\prod_{e\in E}4\sinh l_{e}\sinh\frac{l_{e}}{b^{2}}\bigg)\exp\Bigg(\frac{\sum_{\Delta\in T}U_{b}(\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta},\xi_{\Delta})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\Bigg)d\bm{\alpha}d\bm{\xi}\\
<&O\bigg(e^{\frac{-\mathrm{Vol}(M)-\epsilon_{3}}{\pi b^{2}}}\bigg)\end{split}

(4.58)

for some
\epsilon_{3}>0.

(
ii
.2)

For any proper subset
T_{0}
of
T,

\begin{split}\frac{(-\mathbf{i})^{|E|}}{(\pi b)^{|E|+|T|}}&\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T_{0}}}\bigg(\prod_{e\in E}4\sinh l_{e}\sinh\frac{l_{e}}{b^{2}}\bigg)\exp\Bigg(\frac{\sum_{\Delta\in T}U_{b}(\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta},\xi_{\Delta})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\Bigg)d\bm{\alpha}d\bm{\xi}\\
<&O\bigg(e^{\frac{-\mathrm{Vol}(M)-\epsilon_{4}}{\pi b^{2}}}\bigg)\end{split}

(4.59)

for some
\epsilon_{4}>0.

Then (ii) follows from (ii.1) and (ii.2) with
\epsilon_{2}=\min\{\epsilon_{3},\epsilon_{4}\}.

To see (ii,1), we have

\begin{split}&\frac{(-\mathbf{i})^{|E|}}{(\pi b)^{|E|+|T|}}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{d_{0}}}\bigg(\prod_{e\in E}4\sinh l_{e}\sinh\frac{l_{e}}{b^{2}}\bigg)\exp\Bigg(\frac{\sum_{\Delta\in T}U_{b}(\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta},\xi_{\Delta})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\Bigg)d\bm{\alpha}d\bm{\xi}\\
=&\sum_{\bm{\lambda}\in\{-1,1\}^{E}}\frac{(-2\mathbf{i})^{|E|}}{(\pi b)^{|E|+|T|}}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{d_{0}}}\bigg(\prod_{e\in E}\lambda_{e}\sinh l_{e}\bigg)\exp\Bigg(\frac{\mathcal{W}^{\bm{\lambda}}_{b}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\Bigg)d\bm{\alpha}d\bm{\xi}.\end{split}

(4.60)

For
\bm{\lambda}=(1,\dots,1),
by (
4.51
) and the compactness of
\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{d_{0}},
there is an
\epsilon_{3}>0,
an
M>0
and an
S>0
such that

\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{W}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})<-2\mathrm{Vol}(M)-5\epsilon_{3}

(4.61)

and

\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{\kappa}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})<M\quad\text{and}\quad\prod_{e\in E}\sinh l_{e}<S

(4.62)

for all
(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})\in\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{d_{0}};
and by Proposition
4.8
, there is a
b_{0}>0
such that

\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{\nu}_{b}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})b^{4}<Nb^{4}<\epsilon_{3}

(4.63)

for all
b<b_{0}
and for all
(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})\in\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{d_{0}}.
As a consequence of (
4.61
), (
4.62
) and (
4.63
),
we have

\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{W}_{b}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})=\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{W}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})+\mathrm{Im}\kappa(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})b^{2}+\mathrm{Im}\nu_{b}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})b^{4}<-2\mathrm{Vol}(M)-4\epsilon_{3}+Mb^{2};

and letting
\mathrm{V}
be the volume of
\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{d_{0}},
we have

\begin{split}&\Bigg|\frac{(-2\mathbf{i})^{|E|}}{(\pi b)^{|E|+|T|}}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{d_{0}}}\bigg(\prod_{e\in E}\sinh l_{e}\bigg)\exp\Bigg(\frac{\mathcal{W}_{b}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\Bigg)d\bm{\alpha}d\bm{\xi}\Bigg|\\
\leqslant&\frac{2^{|E|}}{(\pi b)^{|E|+|T|}}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{d_{0}}}\bigg(\prod_{e\in E}\sinh l_{e}\bigg)\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{W}_{b}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})}{2\pi b^{2}}\bigg)\big|d\bm{\alpha}d\bm{\xi}\big|\\
<&\frac{2^{|E|}S\mathrm{V}e^{\frac{M}{2\pi}}}{(\pi b)^{|E|+|T|}}O\bigg(e^{\frac{-\mathrm{Vol}(M)-2\epsilon_{3}}{\pi b^{2}}}\bigg)\\
<&O\bigg(e^{\frac{-\mathrm{Vol}(M)-\epsilon_{3}}{\pi b^{2}}}\bigg).\end{split}

(4.64)

For any
\bm{\lambda}\neq(1,\dots,1),
we have

\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{W}_{b}^{\bm{\lambda}}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})-\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{W}_{b}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})=-2\pi\sum_{e\in E}\big(1-\lambda_{e}\big)l_{e}\leqslant 0

for all for
(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})
in
\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{d_{0}}.
Then by (
4.64
),
we have

\begin{split}&\Bigg|\frac{(-2\mathbf{i})^{|E|}}{(\pi b)^{|E|+|T|}}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{d_{0}}}\bigg(\prod_{e\in E}\lambda_{e}\sinh l_{e}\bigg)\exp\Bigg(\frac{\mathcal{W}^{\bm{\lambda}}_{b}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\Bigg)d\bm{\alpha}d\bm{\xi}\Bigg|\\
\leqslant&\frac{2^{|E|}}{(\pi b)^{|E|+|T|}}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{d_{0}}}\bigg(\prod_{e\in E}\sinh l_{e}\bigg)\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{W}^{\bm{\lambda}}_{b}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})}{2\pi b^{2}}\bigg)\big|d\bm{\alpha}d\bm{\xi}\big|\\
\leqslant&\frac{2^{|E|}}{(\pi b)^{|E|+|T|}}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{d_{0}}}\bigg(\prod_{e\in E}\sinh l_{e}\bigg)\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{W}_{b}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})}{2\pi b^{2}}\bigg)\big|d\bm{\alpha}d\bm{\xi}\big|\\
<&O\bigg(e^{\frac{-\mathrm{Vol}(M)-\epsilon_{3}}{\pi b^{2}}}\bigg).\end{split}

(4.65)

Putting (
4.60
), (
4.64
) and (
4.65
) together, we have (
4.58
), completing the proof of (II.1).

To see (ii.2), for each proper subset
T_{0}
of
T,
we have

\begin{split}&\frac{(-\mathbf{i})^{|E|}}{(\pi b)^{|E|+|T|}}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T_{0}}}\bigg(\prod_{e\in E}4\sinh l_{e}\sinh\frac{l_{e}}{b^{2}}\bigg)\exp\Bigg(\frac{\sum_{\Delta\in T}U_{b}(\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta},\xi_{\Delta})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\Bigg)d\bm{\alpha}d\bm{\xi}\\
=&\frac{(-\mathbf{i})^{|E|}}{(\pi b)^{|E|+|T|}}\sum_{\bm{\mu}\in\{-1,1\}^{E}}\sum_{\bm{\lambda}\in\{-1,1\}^{E}}\bigg(\prod_{e\in E}\mu_{e}\lambda_{e}\bigg)\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T_{0}}}\exp\Bigg(\frac{\mathcal{V}_{b}^{\bm{\mu}\bm{\lambda}}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\Bigg)d\bm{\alpha}d\bm{\xi}.\end{split}

(4.66)

For
\bm{\mu}=\bm{\lambda}=(1,\dots,1),
we have

\mathcal{V}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})=\mathcal{W}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})+2\pi b^{2}\sum_{e\in E}(2\alpha_{e}-\pi),

and hence

\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{V}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})=\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{W}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})+2\pi b^{2}\sum_{e\in E}l_{e}

(4.67)

for all
(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})\in\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T_{0}}.

Next, by (
4.46
), (
4.50
) and Proposition
2.15
and letting
\epsilon
be as in (
4.46
), we have

\begin{split}\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{W}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})\leqslant&\sum_{e\in E}2\pi l_{e}+\sum_{\Delta\in T}U\big(\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta},\xi^{*}_{\Delta}(\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta})\big)\\
<&-2\widetilde{\mathrm{Cov}}(\bm{l})-4\big(|T|-|T_{0}|\big)\epsilon<-2\mathrm{Vol}(M)-4\epsilon\end{split}

(4.68)

for all
(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})\in\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T_{0}},
where the last inequality comes from that
T_{0}
is a proper subset of
T
hence
|T|-|T_{0}|\geqslant 1.
By (
4.47
) and (
4.48
), for any
(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})\in\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T_{0}}
and any unit vector
\mathbf{u}=\big((u_{e})_{e\in E},(u_{\Delta})_{\Delta\in T}\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{|E|+|T|}
with
u_{e}=0
for all
e\in E
and
u_{\Delta}=0
for all
\Delta\in T_{0}
and so that the ray
\{(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})+l\mathbf{u}\ |\ l\in(0,+\infty)\}
stays in
\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T_{0}},
we have that the directional derivative

D_{\mathbf{u}}\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{W}((\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})+l\mathbf{u})<-2\pi.

(4.69)

As a notation, let

\partial\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T_{0}}\doteq\bigg\{(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})\in\Gamma^{*}_{\text{in}}\ \bigg|\ \bm{\xi}\in\prod_{\Delta\in T_{0}}\Gamma^{*}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta},L_{1}}\times\prod_{\Delta\notin T_{0}}\partial\Gamma^{*}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta},L_{1}}\bigg\},

which is a compact set. Then by the compactness of
\partial\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T_{0}}
and
B(\bm{\alpha}^{*}_{t_{0}},L_{0}),
there is a
b_{0}>0
such that for all
b<b_{0},

\mathrm{Im}\kappa(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})b^{2}<\epsilon

(4.70)

for all
(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})\in\partial\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T_{0}},

2\pi b^{2}\sum_{e\in E}l_{e}<\epsilon

(4.71)

for all
\bm{\alpha}\in B(\bm{\alpha}^{*}_{t_{0}},L_{0}),

K|T|b^{2}<\epsilon
and
Nb^{4}<\epsilon,
where
K
and
N
are respectively the constants in Proposition
3.10
and Proposition
4.8
. For
\bm{\xi}\in\prod_{\Delta\in T_{0}}\Gamma^{*}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta},L_{1}}\times\prod_{\Delta\notin T_{0}}\big(\Gamma^{*}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta}}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta},L_{1}}\big),
we let
\bm{\xi}_{\text{proj}}=(\xi_{\text{proj},\Delta})_{\Delta\in T}\in\prod_{\Delta\in T_{0}}\Gamma^{*}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta},L_{1}}\times\prod_{\Delta\notin T_{0}}\partial\big(\Gamma^{*}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta}}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta},L_{1}}\big)
be the projection of
\bm{\xi},
i.e., for
\Delta\in T_{0},

\xi_{\text{proj},\Delta}=\xi_{\Delta},
and for
\Delta\notin T_{0},

\xi_{\text{proj},\Delta}\in\partial\big(\Gamma^{*}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta}}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma^{*}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta},L_{1}}\big)
is the starting point of the ray containing
\xi_{\Delta}.

Then we claim that, for all
(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})\in\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T_{0}}
and
b<b_{0},

\begin{split}\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{V}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})+&\mathrm{Im}\kappa(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})b^{2}+\mathrm{Im}\nu_{b}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})b^{4}\\
<&-2\Big(\mathrm{Vol}(M)+\epsilon\Big)-(2\pi-\epsilon)\big|\bm{\xi}-\bm{\xi}^{*}_{\text{proj}}\big|.\end{split}

(4.72)

As a consequence of the claim and by letting

\mathrm{I}_{T_{0}}=2^{|T|-|T_{0}|}\int_{{\mathbb{R}_{>0}}^{|T|-|T_{0}|}}e^{-(2\pi-\epsilon)|\bm{x}|}d\bm{x}<+\infty

and
\mathrm{V}_{T_{0}}
be the volume of the compact set
\Big\{(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})\in B(\bm{\alpha}^{*}_{t_{0}},L_{0})\times\mathbb{C}^{T_{0}}\ \Big|\ \bm{\xi}\in\prod_{\Delta\in T_{0}}\Gamma^{*}_{\bm{\alpha}_{\Delta},L_{1}}\Big\},

we have

\begin{split}&\Bigg|\frac{(-\mathbf{i})^{|E|}}{(\pi b)^{|E|+|T|}}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T_{0}}}\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathcal{V}_{b}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\bigg)d\bm{\alpha}d\bm{\xi}\Bigg|\\
<&\frac{1}{(\pi b)^{|E|+|T|}}\exp\bigg(\frac{-\mathrm{Vol}(M)-\epsilon}{\pi b^{2}}\bigg)\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T_{0}}}\exp\Bigg(\frac{-(2\pi-\epsilon)\big|\bm{\xi}-\bm{\xi}^{*}_{\text{proj}}\big|}{2\pi b^{2}}\Bigg)|d\bm{\alpha}d\bm{\xi}|\\
=&\frac{\mathrm{I}_{T_{0}}\mathrm{V}_{T_{0}}}{(\pi b)^{|E|+|T|}}\exp\bigg(\frac{-\mathrm{Vol}(M)-\epsilon}{\pi b^{2}}\bigg)\\
<&\ O\bigg(e^{\frac{-\mathrm{Vol}(M)-\epsilon_{4}}{\pi b^{2}}}\bigg)\end{split}

(4.73)

for any
\epsilon_{4}<\epsilon.

To prove the claim (
4.72
), by (
4.67
) and (
4.71
), we first have

\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{V}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})<\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{W}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})+\epsilon

(4.74)

for all
b<b_{0}
and for all
(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})\in\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T_{0}}.

Let
\mathbf{u}=\frac{(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})-(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi}_{\text{proj}})}{|(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})-(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi}_{\text{proj}})|}.
Then by (
4.69
) and the choice of
b_{0},
for
l>0,
we have

D_{\mathbf{u}}\Big(\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{W}\big((\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi}_{\text{proj}})+l\mathbf{u}\big)+\mathrm{Im}\kappa\big((\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi}_{\text{proj}})+l\mathbf{u}\big)b^{2}\Big)<-2\pi+K(|T|-|T_{0}|)b^{2}<-2\pi+\epsilon

for all
b<b_{0}.
Together with the Mean Value Theorem, (
4.68
), (
4.70
), we have

\begin{split}\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{W}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})+\mathrm{Im}\kappa(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})b^{2}<&\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{W}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi}_{\text{proj}})+\mathrm{Im}\kappa(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi}_{\text{proj}})b^{2}-(2\pi-\epsilon)\big|\bm{\xi}-\bm{\xi}^{*}_{\text{proj}}\big|\\
<&-2\mathrm{Vol}(M)-3\epsilon-(2\pi-\epsilon)\big|\bm{\xi}-\bm{\xi}^{*}_{\text{proj}}\big|\end{split}

(4.75)

for all for all
(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})\in\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T_{0}}.
Finally, by Proposition
4.8
and the choice of
b_{0}
, we have

\mathrm{Im}\nu_{b}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})b^{4}<Nb^{4}<\epsilon

(4.76)

for all
b<b_{0}
and for all
(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})\in\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T_{0}}.

Putting (
4.74
), (
4.75
) and (
4.76
) together, we have the first inequality in (
4.73
).

For
\bm{\mu}\neq(1,\dots,1)
or
\bm{\lambda}\neq(1,\dots,1),
we observe that

\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{V}_{b}^{\bm{\mu}\bm{\lambda}}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})-\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{V}_{b}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})=-2\pi\sum_{e\in E}\big((1-\mu_{e})b^{2}+(1-\lambda_{e})\big)l_{e}\leqslant 0

for all for
(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})
in
\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T_{0}}.
Then by (
4.73
),

\begin{split}&\Bigg|\frac{(-\mathbf{i})^{|E|}}{(\pi b)^{|E|+|T|}}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T_{0}}}\bigg(\prod_{e\in E}\mu_{e}\lambda_{e}\bigg)\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathcal{V}_{b}^{\bm{\mu}\bm{\lambda}}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}\bigg)d\bm{\alpha}d\bm{\xi}\Bigg|\\
\leqslant&\frac{1}{(\pi b)^{|E|+|T|}}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T_{0}}}\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{V}^{\bm{\mu}\bm{\lambda}}_{b}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})}{2\pi b^{2}}\bigg)|d\bm{\alpha}d\bm{\xi}|\\
\leqslant&\frac{1}{(\pi b)^{|E|+|T|}}\int_{\Gamma^{*}_{L_{1},T_{0}}}\exp\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Im}\mathcal{V}_{b}(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\xi})}{2\pi b^{2}}\bigg)|d\bm{\alpha}d\bm{\xi}|\\
<&\ O\bigg(e^{\frac{-\mathrm{Vol}(M)-\epsilon_{4}}{\pi b^{2}}}\bigg).\end{split}

Together with (
4.73
) and (
4.66
), this completes the proof of (ii.2).
∎

5
Topological Invariance

The goal of this section is to prove Theorem
1.7
. The idea is similar to that of the original Turaev-Viro invariants
[
64
]
, namely, the Orthogonality and the Biedenharn-Elliot Identity (Pentagon Identity) satisfied by the quantum
6j
-symbols there correspond to the invariance under the
0
-
2
and
2
-
3
Pachner Moves that connect the different ideal triangulations. In our setting, the corresponding identities still hold as distributions. However, as how our invariants are defined, what we really need are the identities between the integrals of certain functions, which do not always hold as the integrand may not be absolutely integrable and Fubini’s Theorem does not apply. For instance, due to the appearance of the Dirac delta function, the integrand fails to be absolutely integrable when a
0
-
2
Pachner Move is performed. To resolve the issue, we restrict ourselves to the class of the Kojima ideal triangulations and introduce a
4
-
4
move illustrated in Figure
11
to avoid the appearance of Dirac delta functions. Then we show that two Kojima ideal triangulations can be connected by a sequence of
2
-
3
,
3
-
2
and
4
-
4
moves such that each intermediate ideal triangulation supports angle structures so that the corresponding integral absolutely converges (see Theorem
4.9
) and Fubini’s Theorem applies to prove the desired identity.

Figure 11
:
The 4-4 move

For the invariance under the
2
-
3
,
3
-
2
and
4
-
4
moves, we need the following identities for the
b
-
6j
symbols, where the Pentagon Identity was proved in
[
50
]
, and the
4
-
4
Move Identity is new and whose proof is deferred to Section
5.1
.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
\mathbb{S}\doteq\frac{Q}{2}+\mathbf{i}\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

(1)

The following
Pentagon Identity

\begin{split}&\begin{Bmatrix}a_{5}&a_{6}&a_{10}\\
a_{3}&a_{4}&a_{7}\end{Bmatrix}_{b}\begin{Bmatrix}a_{10}&a_{5}&a_{6}\\
a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{9}\end{Bmatrix}_{b}\\
=&\int_{\mathbb{S}}|S_{b}(2a_{8})|^{2}\begin{Bmatrix}a_{3}&a_{7}&a_{6}\\
a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{8}\end{Bmatrix}_{b}\begin{Bmatrix}a_{4}&a_{5}&a_{7}\\
a_{1}&a_{8}&a_{9}\end{Bmatrix}_{b}\begin{Bmatrix}a_{4}&a_{9}&a_{8}\\
a_{2}&a_{3}&a_{10}\end{Bmatrix}_{b}d\mathrm{Im}a_{8}\end{split}

(5.1)

holds as continuous functions in
(a_{1},\dots,a_{10})\in{\mathbb{S}}^{10}
.

(2)

The following
4-4 Move Identity

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{S}}|S_{b}(2a_{13})|^{2}\begin{Bmatrix}a_{13}&a_{6}&a_{1}\\
a_{10}&a_{11}&a_{5}\end{Bmatrix}_{b}\begin{Bmatrix}a_{13}&a_{12}&a_{8}\\
a_{4}&a_{5}&a_{11}\end{Bmatrix}_{b}\begin{Bmatrix}a_{8}&a_{13}&a_{12}\\
a_{2}&a_{3}&a_{9}\end{Bmatrix}_{b}\begin{Bmatrix}a_{9}&a_{6}&a_{7}\\
a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{13}\end{Bmatrix}_{b}d\mathrm{Im}a_{13}\\
=&\int_{\mathbb{{\mathbb{S}}}}|S_{b}(2a_{14})|^{2}\begin{Bmatrix}a_{2}&a_{11}&a_{14}\\
a_{4}&a_{3}&a_{12}\end{Bmatrix}_{b}\begin{Bmatrix}a_{7}&a_{10}&a_{14}\\
a_{11}&a_{2}&a_{1}\end{Bmatrix}_{b}\begin{Bmatrix}a_{7}&a_{9}&a_{6}\\
a_{5}&a_{10}&a_{14}\end{Bmatrix}_{b}\begin{Bmatrix}a_{5}&a_{9}&a_{14}\\
a_{3}&a_{4}&a_{8}\end{Bmatrix}_{b}d\mathrm{Im}a_{14}\end{split}

(5.2)

holds as continuous functions in
(a_{1},\dots,a_{14})\in\mathbb{S}^{14}
.

On the topological side, let
\{P_{i}\}_{i\in\mathrm{I}}
be the set of hyperideal polyhedra in the Kojima polyhedral decomposition of
M
. Then as described in Section
2.8
, a Kojima ideal triangulation depends on the choice of a tip
p_{i}
for each convex polyhedron
P_{i}
and the choice of a cone point
p_{ij}
for each face
F_{ij}
of the polyhedron
P_{i}
not adjacent to
p_{i}
; and we will denote by
\mathcal{T}(\{p_{i}\},\{p_{ij}\})
the Kojima ideal triangulation determined by this choice. The following Propositions
5.2
and
5.3
, which will be proved in Section
5.2
, imply that any two different Kojima ideal triangulations can be connected by a sequence of
2
-
3
,
3
-
2
and
4
-
4
moves such that each of the intermediate triangulations supports angle structures.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
P_{u}
be a hyperideal polyhedron in the Kojima polyhedral decomoposition
M
and let
F_{uv}
be a face of
P_{u}
that is not adjacent to
p_{u}
. Suppose
\mathcal{T}(\{p_{i}\},\{p_{ij}\})
and
\mathcal{T}(\{p_{i}^{\prime}\},\{p_{ij}^{\prime}\})
are two Kojima ideal triangulation of
M
such that

(1)

the tips
p_{i}=p_{i}^{\prime}
for all
i\in\mathrm{I}
, and

(2)

the cone points
p_{ij}=p_{ij}^{\prime}
for all
(i,j)\neq(u,v)
.

Then
\mathcal{T}(\{p_{i}\},\{p_{ij}\})
and
\mathcal{T}(\{p_{i}^{\prime}\},\{p_{ij}^{\prime}\})
can be connected by a sequence of
2
-
3
and
3
-
2
moves such that each of the intermediate triangulations supports angle structures.

Figure 12
:
The figure above illustrates the difference between the two triangulations
\mathcal{T}(\{p_{i}\},\{p_{ij}\})
on the left and
\mathcal{T}(\{p_{i}^{\prime}\},\{p_{ij}^{\prime}\})
on the right described in Proposition
5.3
over the polygonal faces in the neighbourhood of
p_{u}
and
p_{u}^{\prime}
; and all the other faces are triangulated identically. The edges of the polygonal faces are colored in black and the edges coming from the subdivision are colored in blue.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
P_{u}
be a hyperideal polyhedron in the Kojima polyhedral decomposition
M=\sqcup_{i\in\mathrm{I}}P_{i}
. Suppose
\mathcal{T}(\{p_{i}\},\{p_{ij}\})
and
\mathcal{T}(\{p_{i}^{\prime}\},\{p_{ij}^{\prime}\})
are two Kojima ideal triangulation of
M
such that

(1)

the tips
p_{i}=p_{i}^{\prime}
for all
i\neq u
, and
p_{u}
and
p_{u}^{\prime}
are adjacent in
P_{u}
,

(2)

the cone points
p_{ij}=p_{ij}^{\prime}
for all faces
F_{ij}
not in
P_{u},
and the cone points
p_{uj}=p_{uj}^{\prime}
for all faces
F_{uj}
of
P_{u}
that is adjacent to neither
p_{u}
nor
p_{u}^{\prime}
, and

(3)

the cone point
p_{uj}=p_{u}^{\prime}
for all faces
F_{uj}
adjacent to
p_{u}^{\prime}
but not adjacent to
p_{u}
, and the cone point
p_{uj}^{\prime}=p_{u}
for all faces
F_{uj}
adjacent to
p_{u}
. (See Figure
12
)

Then
\mathcal{T}(\{p_{i}\},\{p_{ij}\})
and
\mathcal{T}(\{p_{i}^{\prime}\},\{p_{ij}^{\prime}\})
can be connected by a sequence of
2
-
3
,
3
-
2
and
4
-
4
moves such that each of the intermediate triangulations supports angle structures.

Combining these ingredients, we can now prove Theorem
1.7
.

Proof of Theorem
1.7
.

By Theorem
4.9
, if an ideal triangulation
\mathcal{T}
admits angle structures, then the associated state integral
\mathrm{TV}_{b}(M,\mathcal{T})
converges absolutely for a sufficiently small
b
. If two ideal triangulations
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\mathcal{T}_{2}
both admit angle structure and differ from each other by a single
2
-
3
,
3
-
2
or
4
-
4
move, then Fubini’s Theorem together with Proposition
5.1
implies that the two state integrals
\mathrm{TV}_{b}(M,\mathcal{T}_{1})=\mathrm{TV}_{b}(M,\mathcal{T}_{2})
.
Therefore, to prove Theorem
1.7
, it suffices to prove that any two Kojima ideal triangulations can be connected by a sequence of
2
-
3
,
3
-
2
and
4
-
4
moves such that each of the intermediate triangulations supports angle structures.

By Proposition
5.2
, any two Kojima ideal triangulations determined by the same set of tips can be connected by a sequence of
2
-
3
and
3
-
2
moves such that each of the intermediate ideal triangulations supports angle structure. Now for two Kojima ideal triangulation
\mathcal{T}(\{p_{i}\},\{p_{ij}\})
and
\mathcal{T}(\{p_{i}^{\prime}\},\{p_{ij}^{\prime}\})
, one can find a sequence of Kojima ideal triangulations
\mathcal{T}(\{p_{i}^{(k)}\},\{p_{ij}^{(k)}\})
such that for each
k
there exist a
u_{k}\in\mathrm{I}
such that

(1)

for each pair of consecutive Kojima ideal triangulations
\mathcal{T}(\{p_{i}^{(k)}\},\{p_{ij}^{(k)}\})
and
\mathcal{T}(\{p_{i}^{(k+1)}\},\{p_{ij}^{(k+1)}\})
in the sequence, the tips
p_{u_{k}}^{(k)}
and
p_{u_{k}}^{(k+1)}
are adjacent in the polyhedron
P_{u_{k}}
, and

(2)

the tips

p_{i}^{(k)}=p_{i}^{(k+1)}
in all the other polyhedra
P_{i}
’s.

Then by Propositions
5.2
and
5.3
, consecutive Kojima ideal triangulations in the sequence, and hence
\mathcal{T}(\{p_{i}\},\{p_{ij}\})
and
\mathcal{T}(\{p_{i}^{\prime}\},\{p_{ij}^{\prime}\})
, can be connected by a sequence of
2
-
3
,
3
-
2
and
4
-
4
moves such that each of the intermediate ideal triangulations supports angle structures.
∎

5.1
Positive representations and proof of Proposition
5.1

For
b\in(0,1)
such that
q=e^{\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}
that is not a root of unity, the identities in Proposition
5.1
are established by Ponsot-Teschner
[
50
]
via the positive representations of the modular double of
\mathrm{U}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}(2;\mathbb{R})
.
In this section, we will show that both sides of the identities in Proposition
5.1
depend continuously on
b
, and it suffices to prove these identities for those
b
with
q=e^{\pi\mathbf{i}b^{2}}
not a root of unity. We now briefly recall the definition and basic properties of
\mathrm{U}_{q\tilde{q}}\mathfrak{sl}(2;\mathbb{R})
,
the modular double of
\mathrm{U}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}(2;\mathbb{R})
, and its positive representations. Recall that for
q\notin\{0,\pm 1\}
the quantum group
\mathrm{U}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}(2;\mathbb{R})
is the
*
-Hopf algebra generated by
E,F,K,K^{-1}
subject to the relations:

KE=qEK,\quad KF=q^{-1}FK,\quad[E,F]=\frac{K^{2}-K^{-2}}{q-q^{-1}},

and equipped with the following co-product:

\Delta(E)=E\otimes K+K^{-1}\otimes E,\quad\Delta(F)=F\otimes K+K^{-1}\otimes F,\quad\Delta(K)=K\otimes K.

The star structure is
E^{*}=E
,
F^{*}=F
and
K^{*}=K
. Let
\tilde{q}=e^{\pi\mathbf{i}b^{-2}}
. Then the
modular double
of
\mathrm{U}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}(2;\mathbb{R})
is defined as

\mathrm{U}_{q\tilde{q}}\mathfrak{sl}(2;\mathbb{R})=\mathrm{U}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}(2;\mathbb{R})\otimes\mathrm{U}_{\tilde{q}}\mathfrak{sl}(2;\mathbb{R}).

We denote the corresponding generators of
\mathrm{U}_{\tilde{q}}\mathfrak{sl}(2;\mathbb{R})
by
\tilde{E},\tilde{F},\tilde{K}
and
\tilde{K}^{-1}
.

For each
a=\frac{Q}{2}+\mathbf{i}\lambda
with
\lambda>0
, there is an irreducible representation
P_{a}
of
\mathrm{U}_{q\tilde{q}}\mathfrak{sl}(2;\mathbb{R})
, called the
positive representation
. It is defined using unbounded essentially self-adjoint operators over the dense domain

\mathcal{W}\doteq\Big\{\sum_{k=1}^{N}e^{-{r_{k}}x^{2}+{s_{k}}x+c_{k}}x^{i_{k}}\Big|r_{k}>0,s_{k},c_{k}\in\mathbb{C},i_{k}\in\mathbb{N},N\in\mathbb{N}\Big\}

in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R},dx)
(c.f
[
28
, Section 2.5]
). The action of the generators
E,F,K^{\pm 1},\tilde{E},\tilde{F},\tilde{K}^{\pm 1}
of
\mathrm{U}_{q\tilde{q}}\mathfrak{sl}(2;\mathbb{R})
under
P_{a}
is given by

(1)

P_{a}(E)(u(x))=\frac{e^{2\pi bx}}{2\sin\pi b^{2}}[e^{\mathbf{i}\pi b(\frac{b}{2}-\mathbf{i}\lambda)}u(x+\frac{\mathbf{i}b}{2})+e^{\mathbf{i}\pi b(\mathbf{i}\lambda-\frac{b}{2})}u(x-\frac{\mathbf{i}b}{2})]
,

(2)

P_{a}(F)(u(x))=\frac{e^{-2\pi bx}}{2\sin\pi b^{2}}[e^{\mathbf{i}\pi b(\mathbf{i}\lambda-\frac{b}{2})}u(x+\frac{\mathbf{i}b}{2})+e^{\mathbf{i}\pi b(\frac{b}{2}-\mathbf{i}\lambda)}u(x-\frac{\mathbf{i}b}{2})]
,

(3)

P_{a}(K)(u(x))=u(x+\frac{\mathbf{i}b}{2})
, and

(4)

\tilde{E},\tilde{F}
and
\tilde{K}
are sent respectively to the operators given by replacing the
b
in
E,

F
and
K
by
b^{-1}.

We first follow
[
50
]
and recall the definition of the Clebsch-Gordan maps, which are unitary intertwining maps decomposing a tensor product
P_{a_{2}}\otimes P_{a_{1}}
into a direct integral
\int_{\mathbb{S}}^{\oplus}P_{a}d\rho(a)
.

Theorem 5.4

(
[
50
]
, Theorem 2)
.

Equip
\mathbb{S}=\frac{Q}{2}+\mathbf{i}\mathbb{R}_{>0}
with the
Plancherel measure

d\rho(a)\doteq\left|S_{b}(2a)\right|^{2}d\operatorname{Im}a.

Then the tensor product of positive representations of
\mathrm{U}_{q\tilde{q}}\mathfrak{sl}(2;\mathbb{R})
decomposes into a direct integral of irreducible positive representations:

P_{a_{2}}\otimes P_{a_{1}}\simeq\int_{\mathbb{S}}^{\oplus}P_{a}\,d\rho(a).

Moreover, the isomorphism
\simeq
is realized by a unitary intertwiner
(\mathcal{C}_{2,1}^{3})^{\rm PT}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R})\to L^{2}\left(\mathbb{S}\times\mathbb{R},d\rho(a_{3})dx_{3}\right)

defined by

\displaystyle(\mathcal{C}_{2,1}^{3})^{\rm PT}(f)(a_{3},x_{3})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}a_{3}\\
x_{3}\end{array}\Bigg|\begin{array}[]{cc}a_{2}&a_{1}\\
x_{2}&x_{1}\end{array}\right]^{\rm PT}f(x_{2},x_{1})\,dx_{2}dx_{1},

where the kernel is the
Clebsch–Gordan coefficient

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{c}a_{3}\\
x_{3}\end{array}\Bigg|\begin{array}[]{cc}a_{2}&a_{1}\\
x_{2}&x_{1}\end{array}\right]^{\rm PT}

\displaystyle\doteq e^{-\frac{\pi\mathbf{i}}{2}\left(a_{3}(Q-a_{3})-a_{1}(Q-a_{1})-a_{2}(Q-a_{2})\right)}\times\frac{S_{b}\left(\frac{Q}{2}-\mathbf{i}(x_{2}-x_{1})-\frac{a_{1}+a_{2}+2a_{3}-Q}{2}\right)}{S_{b}\left(\frac{Q}{2}-\mathbf{i}(x_{2}-x_{1})+\frac{a_{1}+a_{2}-Q}{2}\right)}

\displaystyle\times\frac{S_{b}\left(\frac{Q}{2}-\mathbf{i}(x_{2}-x_{3})-\frac{Q+a_{2}-a_{3}}{2}\right)}{S_{b}\left(\frac{Q}{2}-\mathbf{i}(x_{2}-x_{3})+\frac{Q+a_{2}-a_{3}-2a_{1}}{2}\right)}\times\frac{S_{b}\left(\frac{Q}{2}-\mathbf{i}(x_{3}-x_{1})-\frac{Q+a_{1}-a_{3}}{2}\right)}{S_{b}\left(\frac{Q}{2}-\mathbf{i}(x_{3}-x_{1})+\frac{Q+a_{1}-a_{3}-2a_{2}}{2}\right)}.

Remark 5.5
.

The original proof in
[
50
]
was based on studying the eigenfunctions of the Casimir operator acting on
P_{a_{2}}\otimes P_{a_{1}}
. The treatment of the unitarity of the Clebsch–Gordan map defined above was later simplified in
[
14
,
44
]
, using the connection between the Casimir operator and the Kashaev length operator introduced in
[
30
]
. The orthogonality and completeness of the eigenfunctions of the Kashaev length operator were proven in
[
60
]
, and
[
14
,
44
]
demonstrated how the Clebsch–Gordan coefficients - viewed as eigenfunctions of the Casimir operator - can be explicitly constructed from these eigenfunctions. Similar construction for the Fourier transform of the above representation was considered in
[
29
]
.

In
[
50
]
, a variant of the
b
-
6j
symbols in Definition
1.1
was obtained from the Clebsch–Gordan map
(\mathcal{C}_{2,1}^{3})^{\rm PT}
, which differs from our
b
-
6j
symbols by a factor. To obtain our
b
-
6j
symbols, as the convention taken in
[
63
]
, we need to renormalize the Clebsch–Gordan maps as

\displaystyle(\mathcal{C}_{2,1}^{3})(f)(a_{3},x_{3})\doteq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}a_{3}\\
x_{3}\end{array}\Bigg|\begin{array}[]{cc}a_{2}&a_{1}\\
x_{2}&x_{1}\end{array}\right]^{\rm TV}f(x_{2},x_{1})\,dx_{2}dx_{1},

where

\left[\begin{array}[]{c}a_{3}\\
x_{3}\end{array}\Bigg|\begin{array}[]{cc}a_{2}&a_{1}\\
x_{2}&x_{1}\end{array}\right]^{\rm TV}\doteq M(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\left[\begin{array}[]{c}a_{3}\\
x_{3}\end{array}\Bigg|\begin{array}[]{cc}a_{2}&a_{1}\\
x_{2}&x_{1}\end{array}\right]^{\mathrm{PT}},

and

M(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})=\left[S_{b}(2Q-a_{1}-a_{2}-a_{3})S_{b}(Q-a_{1}-a_{2}+a_{3})S_{b}(a_{1}+a_{3}-a_{2})S_{b}(a_{2}+a_{3}-a_{1})\right]^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Now we explain the relation between
\mathcal{C}_{2,1}^{3}
and our
b
-
6j
symbols. It is in analog to the case of finite dimensional representations of quantum groups with the summation replaced by an integral. See e.g.
[
65
, Chapter VI]
for reference in that case.
We shall use subscripts with parentheses, such as
(t)
, to indicate that an integral operator acts on the component
L^{2}(\mathbb{S},d\rho(a_{t}))
, and subscripts without parentheses, such as
t
, to indicate that it acts on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R},dx_{t})
. The superscripts, such as
(s)
and
s
, will indicate that the codomain is respectively
L^{2}(\mathbb{S},d\rho(a_{s}))
and
L^{2}(\mathbb{R},dx_{s})
. Consider the following compositions of the Clebsch–Gordan maps, both of which are unitary operators:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{4}_{3,s}\circ(\mathrm{Id}_{3}\otimes\mathcal{C}^{s}_{2,1})

\displaystyle:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},dx_{1}dx_{2}dx_{3})\longrightarrow L^{2}\left(\mathbb{S}\times\mathbb{S}\times\mathbb{R},d\rho(a_{4})\,d\rho(a_{s})\,dx_{4}\right),

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{4}_{t,1}\circ(\mathcal{C}^{t}_{3,2}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{1})

\displaystyle:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},dx_{1}dx_{2}dx_{3})\longrightarrow L^{2}\left(\mathbb{S}\times\mathbb{S}\times\mathbb{R},d\rho(a_{4})\,d\rho(a_{t})\,dx_{4}\right).

Given
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}\in\mathbb{S}
, define
\mathcal{F}_{(t)}^{(s)}:L^{2}(\mathbb{S},d\rho(a_{t}))\to L^{2}(\mathbb{S},d\rho(a_{s}))
by

(\mathcal{F}_{(t)}^{(s)}f)(a_{s})=\int_{\mathbb{S}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{s}\\
a_{3}&a_{4}&a_{t}\end{array}\right\}_{b}f(a_{t})d\rho(a_{t}).

The map
\mathcal{F}_{(t)}^{(s)}
is called the
fusion transformation operator
and relates to the Clebsch–Gordan maps by

\mathcal{F}_{(t)}^{(s)}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{4}=(\mathcal{C}^{4}_{3,s}\circ(\mathrm{Id}_{3}\otimes\mathcal{C}^{s}_{2,1}))\circ(\mathcal{C}^{4}_{t,1}\circ(\mathcal{C}^{t}_{3,2}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{1}))^{-1}\quad\textrm{on }L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2}\times\mathbb{R},d\rho(a_{4})d\rho(a_{t})dx_{4}),

(5.11)

as illustrated in the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},dx_{1}dx_{2}dx_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2}\times\mathbb{R},d\rho(a_{4})d\rho(a_{t})dx_{4})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2}\times\mathbb{R},d\rho(a_{4})d\rho(a_{s})dx_{4}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{4}_{t,1}\circ(\mathcal{C}^{t}_{3,2}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{4}_{3,s}\circ(\mathrm{Id}_{3}\otimes\mathcal{C}^{s}_{2,1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{(t)}^{(s)}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{4}}

The relation (
5.11
) has a variant for
(\mathcal{C}_{2,1}^{3})^{\rm PT}

which is established in
[
50
]
. Based on this, the relation (
5.11
)
was obtained in
[
63
, Equations (2.23), (2.24), and (2.27)]
after changing the normalization as in (
5.1
). The finite dimensional counterpart of (
5.11
) between can be found in
[
33
, Section 5]
.

The following proposition gives the orthogonality of the
b
-
6j
symbols.

Proposition 5.6
.

\mathcal{F}_{(t)}^{(s)}:L^{2}(\mathbb{S},d\rho(a_{t}))\to L^{2}(\mathbb{S},d\rho(a_{s}))
is a unitary operator.

Proof.

This is because both
\mathcal{C}^{4}_{3,s}\circ(\mathrm{Id}_{3}\otimes\mathcal{C}^{s}_{2,1})
and
\mathcal{C}^{4}_{t,1}\circ(\mathcal{C}^{t}_{3,2}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{1})
are unitary.
∎

We are ready to prove Proposition
5.1
. To simplify the presentation, we use the
diagrammatic representation of the Clebsch–Gordan maps. Each map can be represented using a binary tree with three univalent vertices. The composition of two maps then corresponds to stacking such trees in different associative patterns. See Figure
13
.

5.1
.

Considier the following two unitary maps from

to

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}}\begin{Bmatrix}a_{10}&a_{5}&a_{6}\\

\displaystyle F(a_{5},a_{9},a_{10},x_{5})d\rho(a_{9})d\rho(a_{10}),

\displaystyle V_{2}:F(a_{5},a_{9},a_{10},x_{5})\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{S}}\begin{Bmatrix}a_{3}&a_{7}&a_{6}\\
a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{8}\end{Bmatrix}_{b}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}}\begin{Bmatrix}a_{4}&a_{5}&a_{7}\\
a_{1}&a_{8}&a_{9}\end{Bmatrix}_{b}\int_{\mathbb{S}}\begin{Bmatrix}a_{4}&a_{9}&a_{8}\\

\displaystyle F(a_{5},a_{9},a_{10},x_{5})d\rho(a_{10})d\rho(a_{9})d\rho(a_{8}).

), we have

.

U_{1}=\mathcal{C}_{9,1}^{5}\circ(\mathcal{C}_{10,2}^{9}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{1})\circ(\mathcal{C}_{4,3}^{10}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{2}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{1})
from

By Proposition
2.5
for the continuity of the
b
-
6j
symbols,
and Proposition
5.7
(1) right below, the kernels of
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
are both continuous functions in the variables
\alpha_{i}
’s. Therefore, the fact that
V_{1}=V_{2}
as unitary maps from
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{3}\times\mathbb{R},d\rho(a_{5})d\rho(a_{9})d\rho(a_{10})dx_{5})
to
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{3}\times\mathbb{R},d\rho(a_{5})d\rho(a_{6})d\rho(a_{7})dx_{5})

) for

not a root of unity. By the continuity in
b
of the relevant funcions given in Proposition

(2), the identity holds for all

. The equation (
5.2
) for the 4-4 move follows from
W_{1}=W_{2}
in the same way.∎

Proposition 5.7
.

(1)

The integrals

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}}|S_{b}(2a_{8})|^{2}\begin{Bmatrix}a_{3}&a_{7}&a_{6}\\
a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{8}\end{Bmatrix}_{b}\begin{Bmatrix}a_{4}&a_{5}&a_{7}\\
a_{1}&a_{8}&a_{9}\end{Bmatrix}_{b}\begin{Bmatrix}a_{4}&a_{9}&a_{8}\\
a_{2}&a_{3}&a_{10}\end{Bmatrix}_{b}d\mathrm{Im}a_{8},

(5.12)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}}|S_{b}(2a_{13})|^{2}\begin{Bmatrix}a_{13}&a_{6}&a_{1}\\
a_{10}&a_{11}&a_{5}\end{Bmatrix}_{b}\begin{Bmatrix}a_{13}&a_{12}&a_{8}\\
a_{4}&a_{5}&a_{11}\end{Bmatrix}_{b}\begin{Bmatrix}a_{8}&a_{13}&a_{12}\\
a_{2}&a_{3}&a_{9}\end{Bmatrix}_{b}\begin{Bmatrix}a_{9}&a_{6}&a_{7}\\
a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{13}\end{Bmatrix}_{b}d\mathrm{Im}a_{13},

(5.13)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}}|S_{b}(2a_{14})|^{2}\begin{Bmatrix}a_{2}&a_{11}&a_{14}\\
a_{4}&a_{3}&a_{12}\end{Bmatrix}_{b}\begin{Bmatrix}a_{7}&a_{10}&a_{14}\\
a_{11}&a_{2}&a_{1}\end{Bmatrix}_{b}\begin{Bmatrix}a_{7}&a_{9}&a_{6}\\
a_{5}&a_{10}&a_{14}\end{Bmatrix}_{b}\begin{Bmatrix}a_{5}&a_{9}&a_{14}\\
a_{3}&a_{4}&a_{8}\end{Bmatrix}_{b}d\mathrm{Im}a_{14}.

(5.14)

converge and provide the kernels of the compositions of the fusion transformation operators
V_{2},W_{1},W_{2}
, which are continuous in
\alpha_{i}
’s in
\mathbb{S}
.

(2)

The
b
-
6j
symbols, as well as the integrals above, are continuous in
b\in(0,1)
.

Proof.

Recall the estimate (
2.21
), which implies that for
\epsilon\in(0,\frac{Q}{2})
and for
b
in a compact interval
[b_{\min},b_{\max}]\subset(0,1)
, over the vertical line
\Gamma\doteq\{u\ |\ \mathrm{Re}u=2Q-\epsilon\}
we have a uniform estimate:

\displaystyle\Bigg|\prod_{i=1}^{4}S_{b}(u-t_{i})\prod_{j=1}^{4}S_{b}(q_{j}-u)\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leqslant(C^{8}e^{-\pi\sum_{i}|\text{Im}u-\mathrm{Im}t_{i}|\epsilon})e^{-\pi\sum_{j}|\text{Im}u-\mathrm{Im}q_{j}|(\frac{Q}{2}-\epsilon)}

\displaystyle\leqslant C(\epsilon,b_{\min},b_{\max})e^{-\pi\sum_{j}|\text{Im}u-\mathrm{Im}q_{j}|(\frac{b_{\min}}{2}-\epsilon)}.

Here
C=C(\epsilon,b)
is as in (
2.21
), which is a continuous function in
b
, and

C(\epsilon,b_{\min},b_{\max})=\max_{b\in[b_{\min},b_{\max}]}C(\epsilon,b).

Thus for
\epsilon
small enough, the Dominated Convergence Theorem gives that the
b
-
6j
symbols are continuous in
b
.

Next, we show that for each
k\in\{1,\dots,6\}
, the
b
-
6j
symbol is dominated by a function that exponentially decays in
\mathrm{Im}a_{k}
. By the tetrahedral symmetry, it suffices to consider the case that
k=1.
Let

\displaystyle\tilde{q}_{1}\doteq q_{1}-a_{1}=a_{2}+a_{4}+a_{5}\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{q}_{2}\doteq q_{2}-a_{2}=a_{3}+a_{4}+a_{6}.

Then over the vertical line
\Gamma
the estimate (
2.20
) results in the following:

\displaystyle\Bigg|\int_{\Gamma}\prod_{i=1}^{4}S_{b}(u-t_{i})\prod_{j=1}^{4}S_{b}(q_{j}-u)d\mathrm{Im}u\Bigg|\leqslant C^{8}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-(\frac{Q}{2}-\epsilon)\pi(\sum_{j}|y-\mathrm{Im}q_{j}|)}dy

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle C^{8}\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{(\frac{Q}{2}-\epsilon)\pi(4y-\mathrm{Im}(q_{3}+\tilde{q}_{2}+\tilde{q}_{1})-2\mathrm{Im}a_{1})}dy+C^{8}\int^{\infty}_{2\mathrm{Im}a_{1}}e^{-(\frac{Q}{2}-\epsilon)\pi(4y-\mathrm{Im}(q_{3}+\tilde{q}_{2}+\tilde{q}_{1})-2\mathrm{Im}a_{1})}dy

\displaystyle+C^{8}(2\mathrm{Im}a_{1}+\mathrm{Im}(q_{3}+\tilde{q}_{2}+\tilde{q}_{1}))e^{-(\frac{Q}{2}-\epsilon)\pi(2\mathrm{Im}a_{1}-\mathrm{Im}(\tilde{q}_{1}+\tilde{q}_{2}+q_{3}))}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle C(Q,\epsilon,\mathrm{Im}\tilde{q}_{1},\mathrm{Im}\tilde{q}_{2},\mathrm{Im}q_{3})(1+\mathrm{Im}a_{1})e^{-(Q-2\epsilon)\pi\mathrm{Im}a_{1}}.

Here
C(Q,\epsilon,\mathrm{Im}\tilde{q}_{1},\mathrm{Im}\tilde{q}_{2},\mathrm{Im}q_{3})
can be chosen as a continuous function in its inputs. By the estimate (
2.20
), we have that over
\mathbb{S}
the density of the Plancherel measure satisfies
|S_{b}(2a)|^{2}\leqslant e^{2Q\pi\mathrm{Im}a}
. Therefore, for any compact interval
[b_{\min},b_{\max}]\subset(0,1)
, the integrand in (
5.12
), which involves a product of three
b
-
6j
symbols, is controlled by

\displaystyle C_{1}(b_{\min},b_{\max},\epsilon,\{a_{1},\dots,a_{10}\}{\smallsetminus}\{a_{8}\})(1+\mathrm{Im}a_{8})^{3}e^{-(b_{\min}-6\epsilon)\pi\mathrm{Im}a_{8}}

for some continuous function
C_{1}(b_{\min},b_{\max},\epsilon,\{a_{1},\dots,a_{10}\}{\smallsetminus}\{a_{8}\})
in its inputs; and the integrands (
5.13
), (
5.14
), which involves a product of four
b
-
6j
symbols, are controlled by

\displaystyle C_{2}(b_{\min},b_{\max},\epsilon,\{a_{1},\dots,a_{12}\})(1+\mathrm{Im}a_{13})^{4}e^{-(2b_{\min}-8\epsilon)\pi\mathrm{Im}a_{13}}.

for some continuous function
C_{2}(b_{\min},b_{\max},\epsilon,\{a_{1},\dots,a_{12}\})
in its inputs. Now for a sufficiently small
\epsilon
, these two bounds are integrable functions, respectively in
\mathrm{Im}a_{8}
and
\mathrm{Im}a_{13}
. Then the Dominated Convergence Theorem ensures that these integrals continuously depend on the parameters
\{a_{i}\}
and on
b
.
∎

5.2
Proof of Proposition
5.2
and Proposition
5.3

Proof of Proposition
5.2
.

We orient face
F_{uv}
using the normal vector pointing into polyhedron
P_{u}
, and label its vertices in cyclic order
B_{1}\ldots B_{n}
such that
B_{1}=p_{uv}
and
B_{t}
be the cone point of the polygon being glued to
P_{u}
along
F_{uv}
. Notice that all edges in the interior of
F_{uv}
except
B_{1}B_{t}
is adjacent to exactly
3
tetrahedra, two hyperideal and one flat.

If
B_{1}\neq B_{t}
, we subsequently perform
3
-
2
move with respect
B_{1}B_{t+1},B_{1}B_{t+2},\ldots,B_{1}B_{n};B_{1}B_{t-1},

B_{1}B_{t-2},\ldots B_{1}B_{2}
, skipping boundary edges. Each step of such
3
-
2
move involves two hyperideal tetrahedra and one flat tetrahedron (See Figure
17
). By doing so, all the flat tetrahedra are removed. Also observe that all intermediate triangulations are deformable since each edge is adjacent to at least one hyperideal tetrahedron. (See proof of Proposition
2.16
.) On the other hand, the relative position of
B_{1}
and
B_{t}
could be arbitrary. Hence, reversing this process (i.e., performing a sequence of
2
-
3
moves, see Figure
18
) with
B_{1}=p_{uv}
replaced by
p_{uv}^{\prime}
will result in
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
. See Figure
16
.
∎

Figure 16
:
Change of triangulations on
F_{uv}
: First apply
3
-
2
moves to remove
p_{uv}B_{4},p_{uv}p_{uv}^{\prime}
,
p_{uv}B_{6}
, and
p_{uv}B_{7}
in order, it removes all the layered flat tetrahedra in between
F_{uv}
and the polygon been glued to it. Then apply
2
-
3
moves to add
p_{uv}^{\prime}p_{uv}

p_{uv}^{\prime}B_{8},p_{uv}^{\prime}B_{7}
, and
p_{uv}^{\prime}B_{6}
in order to obtain
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
to insert layered flat tetrahedra.

Figure 18
:
Performing
2
-
3
move adding edge
p_{uv}p_{uv}^{\prime}
, turning hyperideal tetrahedra
p_{u}p_{uv}B_{t}B_{2}
,
p_{u}p_{uv}^{\prime}B_{t}B_{2}
on the left to hyperideal tetrahedra
p_{u}p_{uv}p_{uv}^{\prime}B_{2}
,
p_{u}p_{uv}p_{uv}^{\prime}B_{t}
and flat tetrahedron
p_{uv}p_{uv}^{\prime}B_{2}B_{t}
on the right.

In the rest of this subsection, we prove Proposition
5.3
by connecting
\mathcal{T}\doteq\mathcal{T}(\{p_{i}\},\{p_{ij}\})
and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\doteq(\{p_{i}^{\prime}\},\{p_{ij}^{\prime}\})
described in Proposition
5.3
using a sequence of
2
-
3
,
3
-
2
and
4
-
4
moves such the intermediate triangulations support angle structure. All the Pachner moves will take place among tetrahedra coming from subdividing
P_{u}
and flat tetrahedra inserted between faces of
P_{u}
and the faces identified with them. Without loss of generality, we embed the convex hyperideal polyhedron
P_{u}
into the hyperbolic space
\mathbb{H}^{3}
. To simplify the notation, we denote the adjacent vertices
p_{u}
and
p_{u}^{\prime}
described in Proposition
5.3
by
A
and
B
respectively in this subsection, and further denote the two faces adjacent to
AB
by
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
.
Let
\mathbf{B}
be the subcomplex consisting of all faces of
P_{u}
that are not adjacent to vertices
A
nor
B
(see the base part of Figure
19
),
and equipped it with the triangulation inherited from
\mathcal{T}
. By convexity of
\mathbf{P}
, the base
\mathbf{B}
is homeomorphic to a disk. Also observe that the restriction of triangulation
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
described in Proposition
5.3
over
\mathbf{B}
are identical. We remark that the triangulation on
\mathbf{B}
will not be changed during the process.

Then we layout some terminology which is standard in topology, which will be used to describe triangulation of
P_{u}
. Given two simplices
\Delta
and
\Delta^{\prime}
, the
join
of
\Delta
and
\Delta^{\prime}
is a simplex whose vertices are the union of vertices of
\Delta
and
\Delta^{\prime}
. The join of two simplicial complex
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{X}^{\prime}
is a simplicial complex consists simplices equals to the join of a simplex in
\mathbf{X}
with a simplex in
\mathbf{X}^{\prime}
. For instance, the join of a vertex with a complex
\mathbf{X}
is equivalent to the cone
C\textbf{X}
.

Given a two dimensional subcomplex
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
of the base
\mathbf{B}
, we denote the join of
\{A\}
(resp.
\{B\}
) and
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
by
A\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
(resp.
B\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
), which consists tetrahedra spanned by vertex
A
and triangles in
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
. In the same manner, given a one-dimensional subcomplex
\mathbf{S}
of
\mathbf{B}
, we denote the join of
\{A\}
(resp.
\{B\}
) and
\mathbf{S}
by
A\mathbf{S}
(resp.
B\mathbf{S}
) and the join of
\{AB\}
and
\mathbf{S}
by
AB\mathbf{S}
. Given a complex
\mathbf{X}
whose vertices belongs to the set of vertices of
P_{u}
, by
straightening
of
X
, we mean the complex that is isotopic to
X
, with its edges, faces and tetrahedra represented by geodesic lines, hyperideal triangles and hyperideal or flat tetrahedra respectively in
P_{u}
. Unless stated otherwise, we always realize such complexes as straightened ones.
We will be interested in two types of
1
-dimensional sub-complex
\mathbf{S}
that we explain below, whose join with
\{AB\}
contributes hyperideal tetrahedra and layered flat tetrahedra respectively after straightening.

Definition 5.8
.

A one-dimensional subcomplex
\mathbf{S}\subset\mathbf{B}
is said to be a separating path if
\mathbf{S}\cap F_{1}
is a singleton

\{S_{0}\}
and
\mathbf{S}\cap F_{2}
is a singleton
\{S_{k}\}
, and furthermore,
\mathbf{S}
is homeomorphic to an arc with
\partial S=\{S_{0},S_{k}\}
.

Observe that
\partial\mathbf{B}{\smallsetminus}(\partial F_{1}\cup\partial F_{2})
consists of two connected components, each component is a separating path. We denote the component with its vertices connected with vertex
A=p_{u}
in
\partial P_{u}
by
\mathbf{S}_{min}
and the other component by
\mathbf{S}_{max}
, see Figure
19
. A separating path
\mathbf{S}
may separate
\mathbf{B}
into disconnected components, we denote the union of connected components in between
\mathbf{S}
and
\mathbf{S}_{min}
by
\mathbf{B}_{L}^{\mathbf{S}}
, and denote the union of components in between
\mathbf{S}
and
\mathbf{S}_{max}
by
\mathbf{B}_{R}^{\mathbf{S}}
.

Definition 5.9
.

A separating path
\mathbf{S}\subset\mathbf{B}
is said to be admissible if

A\mathbf{B}_{L}^{\mathbf{S}}\cup AB\mathbf{S}\cup B\mathbf{B}_{R}^{\mathbf{S}}
form a hyperideal triangulation of
P_{u}
after straightening.

Another frequently used type of
1
-dimensional sub-complex is where it consists of consecutive edges of
F_{1}
or
F_{2}
.
In this case the join
AB\mathbf{S}
consists of the layered flat tetrahedra.

We prove Proposition
5.3
in three steps.

Step 1.

To each admissible path
\mathbf{S}
, we associate it with one or two triangulations of
M
, which are denoted by
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}
respectively, which differ by a single step of
2
-
3
or
3
-
2
move.

Step 2.

We show that each of the triangulations
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}
supports an angle structure using the same strategy as Proposition
2.16
.

Step 3.

We define a partial order
\prec
over the set of admissible paths such that
\mathbf{S}_{max}
and
\mathbf{S}_{min}
are the maximal and the minimal ones respectively, and show for each non-minimal admissible path
\mathbf{S}
, there exist another admissible path
\mathbf{S}^{*}\prec\mathbf{S}
such that there is a pair of triangulations assigned to
\mathbf{S}
and
\mathbf{S}^{*}
respectively that differ by a single step of
2
-
3
,
3
-
2
or
4
-
4
Move.

Proposition
5.3
then follows from the finiteness of admissible paths. We now carry out the three steps out in Sections
5.2.1
-
5.2.3
respectively.

5.2.1
Step 1: Describe the intermediate triangulations
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}
.

Let
\mathbf{S}\subset\mathbf{B}
be an admissible path passing through vertices
S_{0}\in F_{1},S_{1},\ldots S_{k}\in F_{2}
. We now define an ideal triangulation
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}
by further decomposing Kojima’s polyhedral decomposition.

We first subdivide all
P_{i}\neq P_{u}
in the same way as
\mathcal{T}
and subdivide
P_{u}
to
A\mathbf{B}_{L}^{\mathbf{S}}\cup AB\mathbf{S}\cup B\mathbf{B}_{R}^{\mathbf{S}}
. By doing so, the triangulations on the polygon faces have been identified together might be different. If on each of the two polygon faces that are identified together, the triangles of each polygon share a common vertex, we insert layered flat tetrahedra in the same way as we described in the definition of Kojima triangulation (See section
2.8
). Observe that all polygons faces except
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
in
P_{u}
are triangulated this way. It remains to describe how to insert layered flat tetrahedra between
F_{1}
(resp.
F_{2}
) and the polygon face identified with it.

Now we describe the triangulations of
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
that are inherit from the subdivision of
P_{u}
according to the admissible path
\mathbf{S}=\{S_{0},\ldots,S_{k}\}
. To be precise, the triangulation over
F_{1}
(resp.
F_{2}
) could be described as follow: First connect vertex
A
and
S_{0}
(resp.
A
and
S_{k}
) to separate
F_{1}
(resp.
S_{k}
) into two components, connecting vertex
B
with all other vertices in the same component, and connecting the rest vertices with
A
, see Figure
20
. We are ready to describe
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}
.

A
B
S_{0}

B
A
S_{k}

Figure 20
:
Triangulation over polygon
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
, viewing from the interior of
P_{u}.

•

Case 1: Suppose
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are not identified with each other. Without loss of generality, we only describe how the flat tetrahedra been inserted between
F_{1}
and the face
F
identified with it. For the other pair of polygons, one simply replace
S_{0}
by
S_{k}
.

We label the vertices of the polygon in cyclic order as
B_{1},B_{2},\ldots,B_{n}
such that
A=B_{1},B=B_{2},S_{0}=B_{s}
and the cone point
\bar{A}
of
F
is identified to
B_{t}
. We call the face
F_{1}
the
front side
(which is because we always make
F_{1}
facing us in the illustrative diagram) and the other face the
back side
. Now on the back side polygon, all triangles share the same vertex
\bar{A}=B_{t}
and on the front side,
B=B_{2}
is connected to
B_{i}
for all
3\leqslant i\leqslant s
and
A=B_{1}
is connected to
B_{j}
for all
s+1\leqslant j\leqslant n
.

(1a)

s\leqslant t
. Denote the paths
\mathbf{S}_{1}=\{B_{3},B_{4}\ldots B_{s}=S_{0}\}
,
\mathbf{S}_{2}=\{B_{s},B_{s+2}\ldots B_{t-1}\}
and
\mathbf{S}_{3}=\{B_{t+1},B_{t+2}\ldots B_{n}\}
which could be empty. We insert flat tetrahedra
B\bar{A}\mathbf{S}_{1}
,
ABS_{0}\bar{A}
,
A\bar{A}\mathbf{S}_{2}
, and
A\bar{A}\mathbf{S}_{3}
, layered in order from the bottom to the top.

(1b)

s>t
, Denote the paths
\mathbf{S}_{1}=\{B_{3},B_{4}\ldots B_{t-1}\}
,
\mathbf{S}_{2}=\{B_{t+1},B_{s+2}\ldots B_{s}\}
and
\mathbf{S}_{3}=\{B_{s},B_{s+1}\ldots B_{n}\}
which could be empty. We insert flat tetrahedra
A\bar{A}\mathbf{S}_{3}
,
ABS_{0}\bar{A}
,
B\bar{A}\mathbf{S}_{2}
and
B\bar{A}\mathbf{S}_{1}
, layered in order from the bottom to the top.

Remark 5.10
.

Notice that some tetrahedra in the list could be degenerate. For instance, if the vertex
S_{0}
is identified with vertex
\bar{A}
, then the tetrahedra
ABS_{0}\bar{A}
becomes degenerate, if this happen, we simply remove it from our list. We will follow this convention in the rest of the section, to reduce the number of situations without introducing ambiguity.

\text{Case (1a)}&\text{Case (1b)}\\
\end{array}

•

Case 2: Suppose
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are identified together by an orientation reversing homeomorphism. We label the vertices of
F_{1}
in cyclic order as
B_{1},\ldots B_{n}
. The way that the layered flat tetrahedra been inserted depends on the cyclic order of
6
distinguished vertices, namely,
S_{0},S_{k}
and
A,B
from
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
respectively. To distinguish the vertices
A,B
from
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
, we denote the vertices
A
and
B
from
F_{2}
by
\bar{A}
and
\bar{B}
respectively. Without loss of generality, we assume
A=B_{1}
and
B=B_{2}
and we always identify
F_{1}
as the front side and
F_{2}
as the back side. Suppose
\bar{A}=B_{t},S_{0}=B_{s}
and
S_{k}=B_{s^{\prime}}
. The orientation-reversing assumption means
\bar{B}=B_{t+1}
.

(2a)

2\leqslant s\leqslant t<s^{\prime}
, i.e.,
AB<S_{0}\leqslant\bar{A}\bar{B}<S_{k}
. Denote the paths
\mathbf{S}_{1}=\{B_{3},\ldots,B_{s}\},\mathbf{S}_{2}=\{B_{s},\ldots,B_{t-1}\},\mathbf{S}_{3}=\{B_{t+2},\ldots,B_{s^{\prime}}\}
, and
\mathbf{S}_{4}=\{B_{s^{\prime}},\ldots,B_{n}\}
. We insert flat tetrahedra
\bar{A}B\mathbf{S}_{1},\bar{A}ABS_{0},\bar{A}A\mathbf{S}_{2},\bar{A}A\mathbf{S}_{4},

S_{k}A\bar{A}\bar{B}
, and

\bar{B}A\mathbf{S}_{3}
, layered from the back to the front.

(2b)

2\leqslant s^{\prime}\leqslant t<s
, i.e.,
AB\leqslant S_{k}<\bar{A}\bar{B}\leqslant S_{0}
. Denote the paths
\mathbf{S}_{1}=\{B_{3},\ldots,B_{s^{\prime}}\},\mathbf{S}_{2}=\{B_{s^{\prime}},\ldots,B_{t-1}\},\mathbf{S}_{3}=\{B_{t+2},\ldots,B_{s}\}
, and
\mathbf{S}_{4}=\{B_{s},\ldots,B_{n}\}
. We insert flat tetrahedra
\bar{B}B\mathbf{S}_{1},S_{k}B\bar{A}\bar{B},\bar{A}B\mathbf{S}_{2},\bar{B}A\mathbf{S}_{4},\bar{B}ABS_{0},
and
\bar{B}B\mathbf{S}_{3}
, layered from the back to the front.

\text{Case (2a)}&\text{Case (2b)}\end{array}

(2c)

2\leqslant t<s^{\prime}\leqslant s
, i.e.,
AB\leqslant\bar{A}\bar{B}<S_{k}\leqslant S_{0}
. Denote the paths
\mathbf{S}_{1}=\{B_{3},\ldots,B_{t-1}\},\mathbf{S}_{2}=\{B_{t+2},\ldots,B_{s^{\prime}}\},\mathbf{S}_{3}=\{B_{s^{\prime}},\ldots,B_{s}\}
, and
\mathbf{S}_{4}=\{B_{s},\ldots,B_{n}\}
. We insert flat tetrahedra
\bar{A}B\mathbf{S}_{1},\bar{A}A\mathbf{S}_{4},S_{0}AB\bar{A},

\bar{A}B\mathbf{S}_{3},S_{k}B\bar{A}\bar{B},
and

\bar{B}B\mathbf{S}_{2}
, layered from the back to the front.

(2d)

2\leqslant s\leqslant s^{\prime}\leqslant t
, i.e.,
AB\leqslant S_{0}\leqslant S_{k}<\bar{A}\bar{B}
. Denote the paths
\mathbf{S}_{1}=\{B_{3},\ldots,B_{s}\},\mathbf{S}_{2}=\{B_{s},\ldots,B_{s^{\prime}}\},\mathbf{S}_{3}=\{B_{s^{\prime}},\ldots,B_{t-1}\}
, and
\mathbf{S}_{4}=\{B_{t+2},\ldots,B_{n}\}
. We insert flat tetrahedra
\bar{A}B\mathbf{S}_{4},\bar{A}A\mathbf{S}_{1},S_{0}AB\bar{A},\bar{A}B\mathbf{S}_{2},S_{k}B\bar{A}\bar{B}
, and
\bar{B}B\mathbf{S}_{3}
, layered from the back to the front.

\text{Case (2c)}&\text{Case (2d)}\end{array}

(2e)

2\leqslant t\leqslant s<s^{\prime}
, i.e.,
AB\leqslant\bar{A}\bar{B}\leqslant S_{0}<S_{k}
: Denote the paths
\mathbf{S}_{1}=\{B_{3},\ldots,B_{t-1}\},\mathbf{S}_{2}=\{B_{t+2},\ldots,B_{s}\},\mathbf{S}_{3}=\{B_{s},\ldots,B_{s^{\prime}}\}
, and
\mathbf{S}_{4}=\{B_{s^{\prime}},\ldots,B_{n}\}
. We add an extra edge connecting
A\bar{B}
and insert flat tetrahedra

\bar{A}B\mathbf{S}_{1},\bar{A}A\mathbf{S}_{4},S_{k}A\bar{A}\bar{B},\bar{B}A\mathbf{S}_{3},\bar{A}ABS_{0},AB\bar{A}\bar{B}
and
\bar{B}B\mathbf{S}_{2}
, layered from the back to the front. We remark that in this case, if
S_{0}=\bar{B}
, there is no flat tetrahedron on the top of the orange edge
A\bar{B}
. In this case, the edge
A\bar{B}
belongs to
F_{1}
and no extra edge is added. The remark applies to cases (2f), (3e) and (3f) where the orange edge appear generically.

(2f)

2\leqslant s^{\prime}<s\leqslant t
, i.e.,
AB\leqslant S_{k}<S_{0}\leqslant\bar{A}\bar{B}
: Denote the paths
\mathbf{S}_{1}=\{B_{3},\ldots,B_{s^{\prime}}\},\mathbf{S}_{2}=\{B_{s^{\prime}},\ldots,B_{s}\},\mathbf{S}_{3}=\{B_{s},\ldots,B_{t-1}\}
, and
\mathbf{S}_{4}=\{B_{t+2},\ldots,B_{n}\}
. We add an extra edge connecting
A\bar{B}
and insert flat tetrahedra
\bar{B}A\mathbf{S}_{4},\bar{B}B\mathbf{S}_{1},\bar{A}ABS_{0},\bar{A}B\mathbf{S}_{2},S_{k}B\bar{A}\bar{B},AB\bar{A}\bar{B}
and
\bar{A}A\mathbf{S}_{3}
, layered from the back to the front.

Before moving to Case (3), we explain the coloring convention of the illustrating figures for the layered tetrahedra been inserted in between polygon faces.
The solid edges comes from the triangulations of the polygon on the front side and the dashed edges comes from the triangulations of the polygon on the back side.
Here the front and back sides are relative to an observer inside the polygon
P_{u}
.
The red edges are shared by polygons on both side and the orange edges do not belong to neither polygons.

\text{Case (2e)}&\text{Case (2f)}\end{array}

•

Case 3: If the
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are identified by an orientation preserving homeomorphism. The way that the layered flat tetrahedra been inserted is almost identical to the previous case, one simply interchange
\bar{A}
and
\bar{B}
to each sub-cases in the Case (2). Nevertheless, we list the triangulations below for completeness. We adopt the same convention as in the Case (2): We label the vertices of
F_{1}
in cyclic order as
B_{1},\ldots B_{n}
, and denote the vertices
A
and
B
from
F_{2}
by
\bar{A}
and
\bar{B}
respectively. Set
A=B_{1},\bar{A}=B_{t},S_{0}=B_{s}
and
S_{k}=B_{s^{\prime}}
.
The orientation-preserving assumption means
\bar{B}=B_{t-1}
.

(3a)

2<s<t<s^{\prime}
, i.e.,
AB<S_{0}\leqslant\bar{B}\bar{A}<S_{k}
: Denote the paths
\mathbf{S}_{1}=\{B_{3},\ldots,B_{s}\},\mathbf{S}_{2}=\{B_{s},\ldots,B_{t-2}\},\mathbf{S}_{3}=\{B_{t+1},\ldots,B_{s^{\prime}}\}
, and
\mathbf{S}_{4}=\{B_{s^{\prime}},\ldots,B_{n}\}
. We insert flat tetrahedra
\bar{B}B\mathbf{S}_{1},\bar{B}ABS_{0},\bar{B}A\mathbf{S}_{2},\bar{B}A\mathbf{S}_{4},

S_{k}A\bar{B}\bar{A}
, and

\bar{A}A\mathbf{S}_{3}
, layered from the back to the front.

(3b)

2\leqslant s^{\prime}<t\leqslant s
, i.e.,
AB\leqslant S_{k}<\bar{B}\bar{A}\leqslant S_{0}
, Denote the paths
\mathbf{S}_{1}=\{B_{3},\ldots,B_{s^{\prime}}\},\mathbf{S}_{2}=\{B_{s^{\prime}},\ldots,B_{t-2}\},\mathbf{S}_{3}=\{B_{t+1},\ldots,B_{s}\}
, and
\mathbf{S}_{4}=\{B_{s},\ldots,B_{n}\}
. We insert flat tetrahedra
\bar{A}B\mathbf{S}_{1},S_{k}B\bar{B}\bar{A},\bar{B}B\mathbf{S}_{2},\bar{A}A\mathbf{S}_{4},\bar{A}ABS_{0},
and
\bar{A}B\mathbf{S}_{3}
, layered from the back to the front.

\text{Case (3a)}&\text{Case (3b)}\end{array}

(3c)

2<t<s^{\prime}\leqslant s
, i.e.,
AB\leqslant\bar{B}\bar{A}<S_{k}\leqslant S_{0}
: Denote the paths
\mathbf{S}_{1}=\{B_{3},\ldots,B_{t-2}\},\mathbf{S}_{2}=\{B_{t+1},\ldots,B_{s^{\prime}}\},\mathbf{S}_{3}=\{B_{s^{\prime}},\ldots,B_{s}\}
, and
\mathbf{S}_{4}=\{B_{s},\ldots,B_{n}\}
. The inserted tetrahedra are
\bar{B}B\mathbf{S}_{1},\bar{B}A\mathbf{S}_{4},S_{0}AB\bar{B},

\bar{B}B\mathbf{S}_{3},S_{k}B\bar{B}\bar{A},
and

\bar{A}B\mathbf{S}_{2}
, layered from the back to the front.

(3d)

2<s\leqslant s^{\prime}<t
, i.e.,
AB\leqslant S_{0}\leqslant S_{k}<\bar{B}\bar{A}
: Denote the paths
\mathbf{S}_{1}=\{B_{3},\ldots,B_{s}\},\mathbf{S}_{2}=\{B_{s},\ldots,B_{s^{\prime}}\},\mathbf{S}_{3}=\{B_{s^{\prime}},\ldots,B_{t-2}\}
, and
\mathbf{S}_{4}=\{B_{t+1},\ldots,B_{n}\}
. The inserted tetrahedra are
\bar{B}B\mathbf{S}_{4},\bar{B}A\mathbf{S}_{1},S_{0}AB\bar{B},\bar{B}B\mathbf{S}_{2},S_{k}B\bar{B}\bar{A}
, and
\bar{A}B\mathbf{S}_{3}
, layered from the back to the front.

\text{Case (3c)}&\text{Case (3d)}\end{array}

(3e)

2<t<s<s^{\prime}
, i.e.,
AB\leqslant\bar{B}\bar{A}\leqslant S_{0}<S_{k}
: Denote the paths
\mathbf{S}_{1}=\{B_{3},\ldots,B_{t-2}\},\mathbf{S}_{2}=\{B_{t+1},\ldots,B_{s}\},\mathbf{S}_{3}=\{B_{s},\ldots,B_{s^{\prime}}\}
, and
\mathbf{S}_{4}=\{B_{s^{\prime}},\ldots,B_{n}\}
. We add an extra edge connecting
A\bar{A}
and insert flat tetrahedra

\bar{B}B\mathbf{S}_{1},\bar{B}A\mathbf{S}_{4},S_{k}A\bar{B}\bar{A},\bar{A}A\mathbf{S}_{3},\bar{B}ABS_{0},AB\bar{B}\bar{A}
and
\bar{A}B\mathbf{S}_{2}
, layered from the back to the front.

(3f)

2\leqslant s^{\prime}<s<t
, i.e.,
AB\leqslant S_{k}<S_{0}\leqslant\bar{B}\bar{A}
: Denote the paths
\mathbf{S}_{1}=\{B_{3},\ldots,B_{s^{\prime}}\},\mathbf{S}_{2}=\{B_{s^{\prime}},\ldots,B_{s}\},\mathbf{S}_{3}=\{B_{s},\ldots,B_{t-2}\}
, and
\mathbf{S}_{4}=\{B_{t+1},\ldots,B_{n}\}
. we add an extra edge connecting
A\bar{A}
and insert flat tetrahedra
\bar{A}A\mathbf{S}_{4},\bar{A}B\mathbf{S}_{1},\bar{B}ABS_{0},\bar{B}B\mathbf{S}_{2},S_{k}B\bar{B}\bar{A},AB\bar{B}\bar{A}
and
\bar{B}A\mathbf{S}_{3}
, layered from the back to the front.

\text{Case (3e)}&\text{Case (3f)}\end{array}

Fact 5.11
.

Observe that for each edge
B_{i}B_{i+1}
of the polygon that is not adjacent to
AB
and
\bar{A}\bar{B}
(resp.
\bar{A}
in Case
2
) there is exactly one flat tetrahedra
X\bar{X}B_{i}B_{i+1}
adjacent to it, where
X\in\{A,B\}
and
\bar{X}\in\{\bar{A},\bar{B}\}
is chosen such that
XB_{i}B_{i+1}
form a triangle in
F_{1}
and
\bar{X}B_{i}B_{i+1}
form a triangle in
F_{2}
. We call these flat tetrahedra edge and the rest flat tetrahedra central. Notice there are at most
3
central tetrahedra for each
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}
listed above.

Suppose
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are identified with each other in the Kojima’s polyhedral decomposition, we now assign another triangulation
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}
to a subset of admissible paths
\mathbf{S}
, we call such admissible paths
special
if it satisfies one of the two conditions:

(A)

the cyclic order of the six distinguished vertices are the same as Cases (2c), (2d), (3c) or (3d). In addition assume (i) edges
AB
and
\bar{A}\bar{B}
are disconnected and (ii)
S_{0}=S_{k}
.

(B)

the cyclic order of the six distinguished vertices are the same as cases (2e), (2f), (3e) or (3f). In addition assume (i)
S_{0}
and
S_{k}
are adjacent; (ii)
AB
and
\bar{A}\bar{B}
are adjacent; and (iii)
S_{k}\notin AB,S_{0}\notin\bar{A}\bar{B}
.

When
\mathbf{S}
is special, we define
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}
as follows. Under Condition (A),
the two central flat tetrahedra in
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}
shares a triangle. We define
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}
to be the triangulation obtained from
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}
by performing
2
-
3
move with respect to the two central flat tetrahedra. By doing so, an extra edge is been added, notice the new edge is adjacent to exactly
3
flat tetrahedra, which are all central in the sense of Remark
5.11
(See Figure
21
). Under Condition (B), the orange edge (i.e., the edge does not belong to the triangulation of neither
F_{1}
nor
F_{2}
) is adjacent to exactly
3
flat tetrahedra which contains two central ones. We define
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}
to be the triangulation obtained from
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}
by performing
3
-
2
move with respect to the three tetrahedra. Notice that the two tetrahedra created in the Pachner Move are both edge in the sense of Remark
5.11
, and both adjacent to
S_{0}S_{k}
(See Figure
22
).

Figure 21
:
Performing
2
-
3
move to
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}
(left) in Case (2c) to obtain
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}
(right): The two central flat tetrahedra in
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}
are
AB\bar{A}S_{0}
and
\bar{A}\bar{B}BS_{0}
, they share a common face
BS_{0}\bar{A}
. Performing a
2
-
3
Move to the two tetrahedra, one obtain
3
new tetrahedra
AB\bar{A}\bar{B},A\bar{B}S_{0}A
and
A\bar{B}\bar{A}S_{0}
, which are all central in the sense of Remark
5.11
.
