Figure 22
:
Performing
3
-
2
move to
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}
(left) in Case (2e) to obtain
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}
(right): The three flat tetrahedra in
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}

A\bar{B}
adjacent to
A\bar{B}
are
A\bar{B}S_{0}B,A\bar{B}S_{k}B
and
A\bar{B}S_{0}S_{k}
. Performing a
3
-
2
move to the three tetrahedra, one obtain two new tetrahedra
ABS_{0}S_{k}
and
B\bar{B}S_{0}S_{k}
.

5.2.2
Step 2:
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}
support angle structures

Proposition 5.12
.

For all admissible path
\mathbf{S}\subset\mathbf{B}
, the triangulation
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}
support an angle structure.

Proof.

For triangulations
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}
from Case (1), (2a)-(2d), (3a)-(3d) and
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}
from Case (2e), (2f), (3e) and (3f), all edges are adjacent to at least one hyperideal tetrahedra after straightening. Therefore, these triangulations all support angle structure by Proposition
2.17
.

Now we show that the triangulation
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}
of Case (2e) supports an angle structure. The proof of the rest cases are identical, the essential condition is that the orange edge is adjacent to at least one zero dihedral angle.
Let
\mathcal{T}^{0}_{A\bar{B}}\doteq\{A\bar{B}\mathbf{S}_{3},A\bar{A}B\bar{B}\}
(resp.
\mathcal{T}^{\pi}_{A\bar{B}}\doteq\{A\bar{B}BS_{0},A\bar{B}S_{k}\bar{A}\}
) be the set consists of all flat tetrahedra that are adjacent to
A\bar{B}
at an edge of dihedral angle
0
(resp.
\pi
) after straightening. This set is nonempty (resp. contains exactly
2
-tetrahedra).

Let
m(e)
,
n(e)
and
k(e)
respectively be the number of
0
-dihedral angles,
\pi
-dihedral angles, and dihedral angles in
(0,\pi)
around
e
that neither belongs to
\mathcal{T}^{0}_{A\bar{B}}
nor
\mathcal{T}^{\pi}_{A\bar{B}}
.
Let
m^{\prime}(e)
and
n^{\prime}(e)
respectively be the number of
0
-dihedral angles and
\pi
-dihedral angles around
e
that also belongs to some tetrahedra in
\mathcal{T}^{0}_{A\bar{B}}
. Let
m^{\prime\prime}(e)
and
n^{\prime\prime}(e)
respectively be the number of
0
-dihedral angles and
\pi
-dihedral angles around
e
that also belongs to some tetrahedra in
\mathcal{T}^{\pi}_{A\bar{B}}
. Now we provide an angle structure by deforming these dihedral angles into
(0,\pi)
while remaining the cone angles unchanged.

(1)

For each
\pi
-dihedral angle (resp.
0
-dihedral angle) belonging to tetrahedra in
\mathcal{T}^{\pi}_{A\bar{B}}
, we deform it to
\pi-3\epsilon_{1}
(resp.
\epsilon_{1}
) for some small enough
\epsilon_{1}
. It makes tetrahedra in
\mathcal{T}^{\pi}_{A\bar{B}}
hyperideal.

(2)

For each
\pi
-dihedral angles (resp.
0
-dihedral angles) belonging to tetrahedra in
\mathcal{T}^{0}_{A\bar{B}}
, we deform it to
\pi-\frac{18\epsilon_{1}}{|\mathcal{T}^{0}_{A\bar{B}}|}
(resp.
\frac{6\epsilon_{1}}{|\mathcal{T}^{0}_{A\bar{B}}|}
) for some small enough
\epsilon_{1}
. It makes tetrahedra in
\mathcal{T}^{0}_{A\bar{B}}
hyperideal, and the cone angle of edge
A\bar{B}
is
2\pi
.

(3)

For the remaining
0
-dihedral angles (resp.
\pi
-dihedral angles), we deform it to
\pi-3\epsilon
(resp.
\epsilon
) for small enough
\epsilon
. This makes the remaining flat tetrahedra deformed to hyperideal tetrahedra.

(4)

For each dihedral angles with value
\alpha\in(0,\pi)
around
e
, we deform it to

\alpha+\frac{1}{k(e)}\left[3\epsilon n(e)-\epsilon m(e)+3\epsilon_{1}n^{\prime}(e)-3\epsilon_{1}m^{\prime}(e)+\frac{18\epsilon_{1}n^{\prime\prime}(e)}{|\mathcal{T}_{A\bar{B}}^{0}|}-\frac{6\epsilon_{1}m^{\prime\prime}(e)}{|\mathcal{T}_{A\bar{B}}^{0}|}\right].

This makes the cone angles around each edge equal
2\pi
.

For small enough
\epsilon
and
\epsilon_{1}
, after the deformation, the hyperideal tetrahedra remain hyperideal. Therefore,
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}
supports an angle structure.
∎

5.2.3
Step 3: connecting
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}

We now define a partial order
\prec
on the set of all admissible paths. Let
\mathbf{S}
and
\mathbf{S}^{*}
be two admissible paths, we say
\mathbf{S}^{*}\prec\mathbf{S}
if

\mathbf{B}_{L}^{\mathbf{S}^{*}}\subset\mathbf{B}_{L}^{\mathbf{S}}
. Note that there is a unique maximal element
\mathbf{S}_{max}
and a unique minimal element
\mathbf{S}_{min}
, where
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}_{max}}=\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}_{min}}=\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
.

Proposition 5.13
.

For an admissible path
\mathbf{S}
such that
\mathbf{S}\neq\mathbf{S}_{min}
, there exists an
\mathbf{S}^{*}
satisfying

(1)

\mathbf{S}^{*}\prec\mathbf{S}
,

(2)

One can choose a triangulation from each set of the triangulations assigned to
\mathbf{S}
and
\mathbf{S}^{*}
respectively, such that they are related by a single step of
2
-
3
,
3
-
2
or
4
-
4
move.

In order to prove the existence of such
\mathbf{S}^{*}
, we need following lemmas.

Lemma 5.14
.

Let
[n]\doteq\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
and
f:[n]\to[n]
be a map such that there is no
i\in[n]
satisfying
f(i)\geqslant i+1
and
f(i+1)\leqslant i
, then
f
has a fixed point. Moreover, if in addition there is some
f(k)<k
, then there is a fixed point
j
of
f
such that
j<k
.

Proof.

The second statement implies the first statement. The claim could be seen as following: If
f(n)=n
, then
n
is a fixed point, otherwise, the second statement guarantees a fixed point in
[0,n-1]
. Therefore, We only need to prove the second statement.

By way of contradiction, assume that there is no fixed point in between
0
and
k-1
. Then
f(k)\leqslant k-1
implies that
f(k-1)\leqslant k-1
due to the assumption of the lemma. Since
f(k-1)\neq k-1
by assumption, we have
f(k-1)<k-1
. Apply this reasoning inductively, one obtains
f(j)<j
for all
0\leqslant j\leqslant k
. In particular,
f(0)<0
, which is a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
\mathbf{S}=\{S_{m},S_{m+1},\ldots,S_{n}\}
be a path in
\mathbf{B}
satisfies the following conditions:

1.

A\mathbf{S}
divides
P_{u}
into two or more disconnected components;

2.

AB\mathbf{S}
consists of hyperideal tetrahedra that does not overlap with each other.

Suppose
B^{\prime}
is a vertex in
\mathbf{B}
such that
B
and
B^{\prime}
lie in the different components of
P_{u}{\smallsetminus}A\mathbf{S}
. Then the geodesic
BB^{\prime}
intersects
A\mathbf{S}
exactly once in
P_{u}
.

Proof.

Since
B
and
B^{\prime}
lie in different connected components of
P_{u}{\smallsetminus}A\mathbf{S}
, the geodesic line segment
BB^{\prime}
must intersect
A\mathbf{S}
due the the convexity of
P_{u}
. On the other hand, By the properties of
\mathbf{S}
,
BA\mathbf{S}
can be considered as a cone with base
A\mathbf{S}
and apex
B
. A geodesic line passes through apex can intersect the base at most once.
∎

Lemma 5.16
.

Let
\mathbf{S}=\{S_{0},\ldots,S_{k}\}\subset\mathbf{B}
be an admissible path. Fix
0\leq i<k
. Let
F
be a geodesic plane that passes through the geodesic line
AB
and intersects
A\mathbf{S}
in the interior of
AS_{i}S_{i+1}\cup AS_{i+1}S_{i+2}
. We call the component of
P_{u}{\smallsetminus}F
containing
S_{0}
the upper component and the other one the lower component. Suppose
E_{i}
and
E_{i+1}
are two vertices of
\mathbf{B}_{L}^{\mathbf{S}}
such that
E_{i}S_{i}S_{i+1}
and
E_{i+1}S_{i+1}S_{i+2}
are two triangles of
\mathbf{B}
(See Figure
23(a)
). Then the following two scenarios cannot occur simultaneously: (1).
BE_{i}
intersects
A\mathbf{S}
at the lower component; (2).
BE_{i+1}
intersects
A\mathbf{S}
at the upper component.

Proof.

By the admissibility of
\mathbf{S}
, the geodesic plane
F
also divides
A\mathbf{S}
into exactly two components. Otherwise, there will be a geodesic ray starting from
B
and lying in
F
that passes through
A\mathbf{S}
at least twice, which contradicts the fact that a geodesic ray starting from apex
B
of cone
BA\mathbf{S}
intersects the base
A\mathbf{S}
at most once.
The vertices
\{S_{0},\ldots,S_{i}\}
lies in the upper component and
\{S_{i+2},\ldots,S_{k}\}
lies in the lower component.
By way of contradiction, assume that
BE_{i}
intersects
A\mathbf{S}
at the lower component and
BE_{i+1}
intersects
A\mathbf{S}
at the upper component. Then
E_{i},S_{i+2}
are in one component and
E_{i+1},S_{i}
are in another component, since both
BE_{i}
and
BE_{i+1}
intersect
F
at
B
.

Consider the tetrahedra
AE_{i}S_{i}S_{i+1}
and
AE_{i+1}S_{i+1}S_{i+2}
. Since
E_{i}
and
S_{i}
are in different components, the geodesic segment
E_{i}S_{i}
must intersect
F
, so

AE_{i}S_{i}S_{i+1}
intersects
F
at a triangle. Similarly,
AE_{i+1}S_{i+1}S_{i+2}
also intersects
F
at a triangle, both lying on the opposite side of
B
across the line
AS_{i+1}
dividing
F
. The two triangles must overlap, but
AE_{i}S_{i}S_{i+1}
and
AE_{i+1}S_{i+1}S_{i+2}
can only overlap along the boundary. Therefore both
E_{i}
and
E_{i+1}
are on
F
, and both
BE_{i}
and
BE_{i+1}
pass through
A\mathbf{S}
along
AS_{i+1}
, contradicting the hypothesis about
F
.
∎

hence cannot occur.

Lemma 5.17
.

Let
\mathbf{S}=\{S_{0},\ldots S_{k}\}
be an admissible path such that
\mathbf{S}\neq\mathbf{S}_{min}
. Then there exists a admissible path
\mathbf{S}^{*}\prec\mathbf{S}
satisfying one of the following conditions:

(1)

\mathbf{S}^{*}=\{S_{0},S_{1},\ldots,S_{i-1},S_{i+1},\ldots,S_{k}\}
, for some
0<i<k
. In addition,
S_{i-1}S_{i}S_{i+1}
form a triangle in
\mathbf{B}_{L}^{\mathbf{S}}
(See Figure
24(a)
).

(2)

\mathbf{S}^{*}=\{S_{0},S_{1},\ldots,S_{i},E_{i},S_{i+1},\ldots,S_{k}\}
for some

and vertex
E_{i}
. In addition,

\mathbf{B}_{L}^{\mathbf{S}}
and
BE_{i}

AS_{i}S_{i+1}
(See Figure

).

(3)

\mathbf{S}^{*}=\{S_{0},S_{1},\ldots,S_{i-1},E_{i},S_{i+1},\ldots,S_{k}\}
for some
i>0
and vertex
E_{i}
. In addition,
S_{i-1}S_{i}E_{i}
and
S_{i+1}S_{i}E_{i}
are triangles in
\mathbf{B}_{L}^{\mathbf{S}}
, and the four vertices
\{S_{i},E_{i},A,B\}
lie in the same geodesic plane (See Figure

).

(4)

\mathbf{S}^{*}=\{E_{0},S_{1},\ldots,S_{i-1},S_{i},S_{i+1},\ldots,S_{k}\}
for some vertex
E_{0}
. In addition,
E_{0}S_{0}S_{1}
form a triangle in
\mathbf{B}_{L}^{\mathbf{S}}
that is adjacent to
\partial\mathbf{B}
(See Figure

).

(5)

\mathbf{S}^{*}=\{S_{0},\ldots,S_{i-1},S_{i},S_{i+1},\ldots,S_{k-1},E_{k-1}\}
for some vertex
E_{k-1}
. In addition,
E_{k-1}S_{k}S_{k-1}
form a triangle in
\mathbf{B}_{L}^{\mathbf{S}}
that is adjacent to
\partial\mathbf{B}
(See Figure

).

Condition 5

Figure 24
:
The relative position of
\mathbf{S}
and
\mathbf{S}^{*}
described in Lemma
5.17
: The red curve denotes the admissible path
\mathbf{S}
, the blue curve denotes the admissible path
\mathbf{S}^{*}
, and the green lines in (d) and (e) denote the intersection
\partial\mathbf{B}\cap\partial F_{1}
and
\partial\mathbf{B}\cap\partial F_{2}
respectively.

Proof.

We provide an algorithm to find a path
\mathbf{S}^{*}
given
\mathbf{S}
which satisfies one of the conditions in the lemma and prove that
\mathbf{S}^{*}
is admissible, namely
A\mathbf{B}_{L}^{\mathbf{S}^{*}}\cup AB\mathbf{S}^{*}\cup B\mathbf{B}_{R}^{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
form a hyperideal triangulation of
P_{u}
.

If there exist three consecutive vertices
S_{i-1},S_{i},S_{i+1}
in
\mathbf{S}
that form a triangle in
\mathbf{B}_{L}^{\mathbf{S}}
, then the geodesic segment
AS_{i}
must intersect
AS_{i-1}S_{i+1}
. We set
\mathbf{S}^{*}\doteq\{S_{0},\ldots S_{i-1},S_{i+1},\ldots S_{k}\}
. Then it satisfies Condition (1).

Since
A\mathbf{B}_{L}^{\mathbf{S}^{*}}\cup AB\mathbf{S}^{*}\cup B\mathbf{B}_{R}^{\mathbf{S}^{*}}

is obtained from
A\mathbf{B}_{L}^{\mathbf{S}}\cup AB\mathbf{S}\cup B\mathbf{B}_{R}^{\mathbf{S}}
by performing the
3
-
2
move that removes
AS_{i}
, the two tetrahedra created in the
3
-
2
move are hyperideal due to the convexity of
P_{u}
. Therefore the path
\mathbf{S}^{*}
is admissible.

Now suppose that there is no
3
consecutive vertices
of
\mathbf{S}
that form a triangle in
\mathbf{B}_{L}^{\mathbf{S}}
.
Let
J=\{0\leqslant j<k:S_{j}S_{j+1}\textrm{ is not in }\mathbf{S}_{min}\}
.

For each
j\in J
, there exists a unique vertex
E_{j}\in\mathbf{B}_{L}^{\mathbf{S}}
such that
E_{j}S_{j}S_{j+1}
form a triangle in
\partial P_{u}
.
Now we define a function
f
on
J
where Lemma
5.14
can be applied.
Write
J
as the (disjoint) union
\cup_{1\leq s\leq r}J_{s}
where each
J_{s}
is a maximal interval of consecutive integers.
By definition, each
J_{s}
is of the form
[m,n-1]\cap J
where
S_{m-1}S_{m}
and
S_{n}S_{n+1}
belong to
\mathbf{S}_{min}
while
S_{j}\notin\mathbf{S}_{min}
for all
m<j<n
. For
j\in J_{s}
, applying Lemma
5.15
to the path
\mathbf{S}
and
B^{\prime}=E_{j}
,
we see that the geodesic
BE_{j}
intersects
A\mathbf{S}
exactly once.
If
BE_{j}\cap A\mathbf{S}\in AS_{k}
, we set
f(j)=k-1
. Otherwise, there exists a unique
\ell
such that

BE_{j}\cap A\mathbf{S}\in AS_{\ell}S_{\ell+1}{\smallsetminus}AS_{\ell+1}
and we set
f(j)=\ell
.
We now claim that
f(j)\in J_{s}
hence
f(J_{s})\subset J_{s}
. To prove this, we apply Lemma
5.15
to
\{S_{m},S_{m+1},\ldots,S_{n}\}
and
B^{\prime}=E_{j}
to see that
BE_{j}\cap A\mathbf{S}
lies in
\cup_{m-1<j<n}AS_{j}S_{j+1}
.
Therefore
f(j)\in[m,n]
, and
f(j)=n
if and only if
BE_{j}\cap A\mathbf{S}\in AS_{n}
.
Since
AS_{n}
lies on the boundary of
P_{u}
and the interior of
BE_{j}
lies in the interior of
P_{u}
, the edges
AS_{n}
and
BE_{j}
do not intersect. Therefore
f(j)\in[m,n-1]=J_{s}
, hence
f(J_{s})\subset J_{s}
.

We now show that on each
J_{s}
the function
f
satisfies the assumption in Lemma
5.14
.
Suppose
j
and
j+1
belong to
J_{s}
.
Let
F
be the geodesic plane in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
passing through the vertices
A,B
and
S_{i+1}
. Then
F
satisfies the condition in Lemma
5.16
. We call the component of
P_{u}{\smallsetminus}F
containing
S_{0}
the upper component and the other one the lower component. Applying Lemma
5.16
to
E_{j}
and
E_{j+1}
, the following two scenarios cannot occur simultaneously: (1).
BE_{j}
intersects
A\mathbf{S}
at the lower component; (2).
BE_{j+1}
intersects
A\mathbf{S}
at the upper component. The first scenario is equivalent to
f(j)\geq j+1
and the second scenario is equivalent to
f(j+1)\leq j
. Therefore
f
satisfies the assumption in Lemma
5.14
.

Suppose
J_{1},\cdots,J_{r}
is ordered increasingly. Let
i
be the smallest fixed point of
f
, namely
i\in J_{1}
and
f(i)=i
.
We now explain how to define
\mathbf{S}^{*}
when no
3
consecutive vertices
of
\mathbf{S}
form a triangle in
\mathbf{B}_{L}^{\mathbf{S}}
.

(1)

If
BE_{i}
intersects
AS_{i}S_{i+1}
in the interior of
AS_{i}S_{i+1}
, we set
\mathbf{S}^{*}=\{S_{0},S_{1}\ldots S_{i},E_{i},S_{i+1},\ldots S_{k}\}
, which satisfies Condition (2).
It remains to show that
\mathbf{S}^{*}
is admissible. Observe
P_{u}^{\mathbf{S}^{*}}\doteq A\mathbf{B}_{L}^{\mathbf{S}^{*}}\cup AB\mathbf{S}^{*}\cup B\mathbf{B}_{R}^{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
is obtained from performing
2
-
3
move to
P_{u}^{\mathbf{S}}\doteq A\mathbf{B}_{L}^{\mathbf{S}}\cup AB\mathbf{S}\cup B\mathbf{B}_{R}^{\mathbf{S}}
by adding edge
BE_{i}
, the
3
tetrahedra created in the Pachner Move are hyperideal since
BE_{i}
intersects in the interior of
AS_{i}S_{i+1}
. Hence the path
\mathbf{S}^{*}
is admissible.

(2)

If
i=0
and the condition in (1) does not occur, then
f(0)=0
implies that

BE_{0}
intersects
AS_{0}
. Therefore
E_{0}
is the vertex in
F_{1}
that is adjacent to
S_{0}
and lies in
\mathbf{B}_{L}^{\mathbf{S}}
. We set
\mathbf{S}^{*}\doteq\{E_{0},S_{1},\ldots,S_{k}\}
, which satisfies Condition (4). Since
P_{u}^{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
is obtained by first performing the
2
-
3
move to
P_{u}^{\mathbf{S}}
that adds edge
BE_{0}
and then removing the flat tetrahedron
ABE_{0}S_{0}
, it is hyperideal hence
\mathbf{S}^{*}
is admissible.

(3)

If
i=k-1
and
BE_{k-1}
intersects
AS_{k}
, then
E_{k-1}
is a vertex in
F_{2}
adjacent to
S_{k}
. We set
\mathbf{S}^{*}=\{S_{0},\ldots,S_{k-1},

E_{k-1}\}
, which satisfies Condition (5).
Since
P_{u}^{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
is obtained by first performing the
2
-
3
move to
P_{u}^{\mathbf{S}}
that adds edge
BE_{k-1}
and then removing the flat tetrahedron
ABE_{k-1}S_{k}
, it is hyperideal hence
\mathbf{S}^{*}
is admissible.

(4)

If
0<i\leq k-1
and the conditions in (1) and (3) do not occur, then
f(i)=i
implies that
BE_{i}
intersects
AS_{i}
. We claim that
BE_{i-1}
also intersects
AS_{i}
. By way of contradiction, suppose
BE_{i-1}
intersects
A\mathbf{S}
in
\cup_{0\leqslant j\leqslant i-1}AS_{j}S_{j+1}{\smallsetminus}AS_{i}
, which means
f(i-1)=i-1
or
f(i-1)<i-1
. If
f(i-1)=i-1
, it contradicts the hypothesis that
i
is the first fixed point; if
f(i-1)<i-1
, by Lemma
5.14
, the function
f
has a fix point smaller than
i-1
, which is again a contradiction.
By way of contradiction, suppose
BE_{i-1}
intersects
A\mathbf{S}
in
\cup_{i\leqslant j\leqslant k}AS_{j}S_{j+1}{\smallsetminus}AS_{i}
at a point
X
. Then
X
and
S_{i+1}
lie in the same component of
A\mathbf{S}{\smallsetminus}AS_{i}
.
Let
F_{0}
be the geodesic plane that passes through
A,B
and
S_{i}
. As argued in the beginning of the proof of Lemma
5.16
, the geodesic plane
F_{0}
divides
A\mathbf{S}
into exactly two components, with vertices

\{S_{0},\ldots,S_{i-1}\}
lies one component and
\{S_{i+1},\ldots S_{k}\}
lies in the other component. Then
X
and
S_{i+1}
lie in the same component of
P_{u}{\smallsetminus}F_{0}
. Let
F
be a geodesic plane obtained by slightly shifting
F_{0}
towards the direction of
S_{i+1}
, such that (1)
F
contains
AB
and passes through the interior of
AS_{i}S_{i+1}
, and (2) the point
X
lies in the same component with
S_{i+1}
in
P_{u}{\smallsetminus}F
.
We call the component of
P_{u}{\smallsetminus}F
containing
S_{0}
the upper component and the other one the lower component. Then
AS_{i}
hence
BE_{i}\cap AS_{i}
lies in the upper component and
BE_{i-1}\cap A\mathbf{S}=X
lies in the lower component. Therefore
BE_{i-1}
lies in the lower component and
BE_{i}
lies in the upper component.
This contradicts Lemma
5.16
because
F
satisfies the condition of that lemma with
i-1
in place of
i
, which means that the previous two scenarios cannot occur simultaneously (see Figure
23(b)
).
To sum up,
BE_{i-1}
intersects
A\mathbf{S}
at
AS_{i}
as claimed.

Since both
BE_{i-1}
and
BE_{i}
intersect
AS_{i}
, both
E_{i-1}
and
E_{i}
lie on the geodesic plane passing through
A,S_{i},B
. Moreover, within this plane
E_{i-1}
and
E_{i}
lie on the same side of the geodesic line passing
AS_{i}
.
Therefore, this two faces
AS_{i}E_{i-1}
and
AS_{i}E_{i}
must be identical, hence
E_{i-1}=E_{i}
. We set
\mathbf{S}^{*}=\{S_{0},\ldots,S_{i-1},

E_{i},S_{i+1},\ldots S_{k}\}
.
Since
BE_{i}
intersects
AS_{i}
and
E_{i}=E_{i-1}
, we see that

\mathbf{S}^{*}
satisfies Condition (3). Observe that
P_{u}^{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
is obtained by performing the
4
-
4
move to
P_{u}^{\mathbf{S}}
that removes edge
AS_{i}
and adds edge
BE_{i}
. (See Figure
24(c)
.) Due to the relative position of
AS_{i}
and
BE_{i}
, the
4
-
4
move involves only hyperideal tetrahedra. Therefore,
\mathbf{S}^{*}
is admissible.∎

Now we finish the proof of Proposition
5.13
by showing that the admissible path
\mathbf{S}^{*}
described in Proposition
5.17
has the desired property.

Proof of Proposition
5.13
.

We prove the proposition by showing that for each pair
\mathbf{S}^{*}
and
\mathbf{S}
described in Lemma
5.17
, one can choose one triangulation from each set of the triangulations been assigned to
\mathbf{S}
and
\mathbf{S}^{*}
respectively, such that they are connected by a single step of
3
-
2
,
2
-
3
, or
4
-
4
move. We first observe that in case (1), case (2), and case (3),
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}

share the same set of flat tetrahedra. Moreover,
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
is obtained from
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}
by a single step of
2
-
3
,
3
-
2
or
4
-
4
move with only hyperideal tetrahedra involved. Hence Proposition
5.13
is verified for those cases.

The remaining cases are where Condition (4) or Condition (5) in Lemma
5.17
is satisfied.
These two cases are completely symmetric (see Figure
24
(d) and (e)). Without loss of generality,
we only treat the case where Condition (4) holds.
In this case,
E_{0}B
intersects
A\mathbf{S}
at
AS_{0}
.
Recall that
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}\neq\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}
is assigend to
\mathbf{S}
when
\mathbf{S}
is special. Also recall that
\mathbf{S}
is special if it satisfies
Condition (A) or Condition (B). If
\mathbf{S}
satisfies Condition (A), then
S_{0}=S_{k}\neq E_{0}
, hence
\mathbf{S}^{*}
does not satisfy condition (A). If
\mathbf{S}
satisfies Condition (A),
then
AB
and
\bar{A}\bar{B}
are not adjacent, hence
\mathbf{S}^{*}
does not satisfy Condition (B). If
\mathbf{S}
satisfies Condition (B), then
AB
and
\bar{A}\bar{B}
is adjacent, hence
\mathbf{S}^{*}
does not satisfy condition (B). If
\mathbf{S}
satisfies Condition (B), then
S_{0}
and
S_{k}
are adjacent, in this case,
E_{0}
can not be adjacent to
S_{k}
, hence
\mathbf{S}^{*}
does not satisfy Condition (B).

To sum up, if
\mathbf{S}
is special, then
\mathbf{S}^{*}
must not be special.
In this case, there exists either zero or one edge in
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
that lies in between the polygons
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
but belongs to neither
F_{1}
nor
F_{2}
(such edges are colored by orange in the illustrative diagrams in
Section
5.2.1
). If
\mathbf{S}
satisfies Condition (A) (resp. Condition (B)), then
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}
(resp.
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}
) has one such edge and
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}
(resp.
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}
) has no such edges. These facts can be checked case by case.
In all scenarios, we choose from
\{\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}},\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}\}
the (unique) triangulation
\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathbf{S}}
that contains the same number of such edges as
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
.

When
\mathbf{S}
is not special, we set
\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathbf{S}}=\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}
.
We now explain how to perform a Pachner move to
\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathbf{S}}
such that the resulting triangulation
belongs to
\{\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}},\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}^{\prime}\}
when
{S}^{*}
is special and equals

\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
when
{S}^{*}
is not.

(1)

If the triangle
ABS_{0}
is adjacent to a hyperideal tetrahedra on the other side, we perform a single step
2
-
3
move adding edge
BE_{0}
(see Figure
26(b)
);

(2)

If the triangle
ABS_{0}
is adjacent to a flat tetrahedra
ABS_{0}\bar{X}
with
\bar{X}\in\{\bar{A},\bar{B}\}
and
\bar{X}\neq E_{0}
, we perform a single step
4
-
4
move removing
AS_{0}
and adding
BE_{0}
(see Figure
25
);

(3)

If the triangle
ABS_{0}
is adjacent to a flat tetrahedra which is equal to
ABS_{0}E_{0}
, we perform a single step
3
-
2
move removing
AS_{0}
(see Figure
26(a)
).

We now check that the resulting triangulation after the above Pachner move satisfies the desired property.

By inspecting the construction of
\mathbf{S}^{*}
in the proof of Lemma
5.17
that meets Condition (4), we see that
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}^{\prime}
share the same set of hyperideal tetrahedra, which can be obtained from the set of hyperideal tetrahedra in
\hat{\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}}
by removing tetrahedra
\{ABS_{0}S_{1},AE_{0}S_{0}S_{1}\}
and adding tetrahedra
\{BE_{0}S_{0}S_{1},ABE_{0}S_{1}\}
.
Therefore after applying the Pachner move described above to
\hat{\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}}
, we obtain the same set of hyperideal tetrahedra as of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}^{\prime}
.

It remains to compare the set of flat tetrahedra lying in between
F_{1}
and the polygon identified with it
in
\hat{\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}}
,
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
, and
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
. Recall the notion of central and edge flat tetrahedra defined above Fact
5.11
.
All edge flat tetrahedra except the ones adjacent to
S_{0}E_{0}
are equal. Hence it suffices to keep track of at most four flat tetrahedra, including the central ones that are adjacent to
S_{0}
, and the edge ones adjacent to
S_{0}S_{0}^{\prime}
. For completeness, we treat the cases when
\mathbf{S}
belongs to Cases (1) and (2) described in Step 1 in detail, and the treatment for Case (3) is identical to Case (2) hence is omitted.

(1)

Case (1a): The triangle
ABS_{0}
is adjacent to the flat tetrahedron
ABS_{0}\bar{A}
if
S_{0}\neq\bar{A}
and adjacent to an hyperideal tetrahedra otherwise. The effect of the Pachner move above depends on the relative position of
S_{0}
and
\bar{A}
:

•

If
S_{0}=B_{t-1}
, the Pachner move is the
3
-
2
move removing
AS_{0}
. Comparing the resulting triangulation to
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
, which belongs to Case (1a), we see that they agree.

•

If
S_{0}=B_{t}
, the Pachner move is the
2
-
3
move adding
BS_{0}^{\prime}
if
S_{0}=B_{t}
.
Comparing the resulting triangulation to
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
, which belongs to Case (1b), we see that they agree.

•

Otherwise, the Pachner move is the
4
-
4
move removing
AS_{0}
and adding
BS_{0}^{\prime}
.
Comparing the resulting triangulation to
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
, which belongs to Case (1a), we see that they agree.

(2)

Case (1b): The triangle
ABS_{0}
is adjacent to the flat tetrahedron
ABS_{0}\bar{A}
and
\bar{A}\neq S_{0}^{\prime}
. Therefore, the Pachner move is the
4
-
4
move removing
AS_{0}
and adding
BS_{0}^{\prime}
.
Comparing the resulting triangulation to
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
, which belongs to Case (1b), we see that they agree.

(3)

Case (2a): The triangle
ABS_{0}
is adjacent to the flat tetrahedron
ABS_{0}\bar{A}
if
S_{0}\neq\bar{A}
and adjacent to an hyperideal tetrahedra on the back side otherwise. The effect of the Pachner move above again depends
on the relative position of
S_{0}
and
\bar{A}
:

•

If
S_{0}=B_{t-1}
, the Pachner move is the
3
-
2
move removing
AS_{0}
.
By comparison, the resulting triangulation to
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
, which belongs to Case (2a).

•

If
S_{0}=B_{t}
, the Pachner move is the
2
-
3
move adding
BS_{0}^{\prime}
if
S_{0}=B_{t}
.
By comparison, the resulting triangulation to
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
, which belongs to Case (2e).

•

Otherwise, the Pachner move is the
4
-
4
move removing
AS_{0}
and adding
BS_{0}^{\prime}
.
By comparison the resulting triangulation to
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
, which belongs to Case (2a).

(4)

Case (2b): The triangle
ABS_{0}
is adjacent to the flat tetrahedron
ABS_{0}\bar{B}
if
S_{0}\neq\bar{B}
and adjacent to an hyperideal tetrahedra on the back side otherwise.

•

If
S_{0}=\bar{B}
, the Pachner move is the
2
-
3
move adding
BS_{0}^{\prime}
. By comparison, the resulting triangulation is
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
, which still belongs to Case (2b).

•

Otherwise, the Pachner move is the
4
-
4
move removing
AS_{0}
and adding
BS_{0}^{\prime}
.
By comparison, the resulting triangulation is
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
, which belongs to Case (2b).

(5)

Case (2c): If
S_{0}=S_{k}
and
B\neq\bar{A}
, then
\mathbf{S}
is special.
In this case

\mathbf{S}^{*}
is always in Case (2c) and
\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathbf{S}}=\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}
by definition.
Note that the triangle
ABS_{0}
is adjacent to flat tetrahedron
ABS_{0}\bar{A}
if
\bar{A}\neq B
and is adjacent to
ABS_{0}B_{s+1}=ABS_{0}S_{0}^{\prime}
otherwise.

•

If
\bar{A}=B
, the Pachner move is the
3
-
2
move removing
AS_{0}
. By comparison, the resulting triangulation is
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
, which still belongs to Case (2c).

•

Otherwise, the Pachner move is the
4
-
4
move removing
AS_{0}
and adding
BS_{0}^{\prime}
. By comparison, the resulting triangulation is
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
, which belongs to Case (2c).

(6)

Case (2d): If
S_{0}=S_{k}
and
A\neq\bar{B}
, then
\mathbf{S}
is special. In this case,
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
belongs to Case (2f) and
\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathbf{S}}=\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{S}}
.
Since the triangle
ABS_{0}
is adjacent to flat tetrahedron
ABS_{0}\bar{A}
in
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}
,
the Pachner move is the
4
-
4
move removing
AS_{0}
and adding
BS_{0}^{\prime}
. By comparision the resulting triangulation is

\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
.

If
\mathbf{S}
is not special, then the triangle
ABS_{0}
is adjacent to the flat tetrahedron
ABS_{0}\bar{B}
if
\bar{B}\neq A
. Otherwise,
ABS_{0}
is adjacent to the flat tetrahedron
ABS_{0}B_{s-1}
if
s\neq 3
and is adjacent to a hyperideal tetrahedron on the back side if
s=3
.

•

If
A\neq\bar{B}
and
S_{0}\neq S_{k}
, the Pachner move is the
4
-
4
move removing
AS_{0}
and adding
BS_{0}^{\prime}
. By comparison, the resulting triangulation is
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
, which belongs to Case (2d).

•

If
A=\bar{B}
and
S_{0}\neq B_{3}
, the Pachner move is the
4
-
4
move removing
AS_{0}
and adding
BS_{0}^{\prime}
. If
S_{0}\neq S_{k}
, by comparison, the resulting triangulation is
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
, which belongs to Case (2d). Otherwise,
\mathbf{S}^{*}
is special.
Comparing the resulting triangulation with
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}^{\prime}
, which belongs to Case (2f), we see that they agree.

•

If
A=\bar{B}
and
S_{0}=B_{3}
, the Pachner move is the
2-3
move adding
BS_{0}^{\prime}
. The resulting triangulation is
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
if
S_{0}\neq S_{k}
, which belongs to Case (2d). Otherwise,
\mathbf{S}^{*}
is special. By comparison the resulting triangulation is
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}^{\prime}
, which belongs to Case (2f).

(7)

Case (2e): If
B=\bar{A}
and
s^{\prime}=s+1
, then
\mathbf{S}
is special. In this case,
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
belongs to Case (2c) and
\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathbf{S}}=\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{S}}
.

Now the triangle
ABS_{0}
is adjacent to the flat tetrahedron
ABS_{0}S_{k}
(See Figure
22
), performing
3
-
2
move removing
AS_{0}
to obtain
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
,
Assume path
\mathbf{S}
is not special. If
\bar{A}=B
and
S_{0}=\bar{B}
, then the triangle
ABS_{0}
is adjacent to a hyperideal tetrahedron on the back side. If
\bar{A}\neq B
and
S_{0}=\bar{B}
, the triangle
ABS_{0}
is adjacent to
ABS_{0}\bar{A}=AB\bar{A}\bar{B}
. Otherwise, the triangle
ABS_{0}
is adjacent to
ABS_{0}\bar{B}
.

•

If
\bar{A}=B
and
S_{0}=\bar{B}
, the Pachner move is the
2
-
3
move adding
BS_{0}^{\prime}
.
Note that
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
belongs to Case (2e) if
s\neq s^{\prime}-1
and belongs to Case (2c) if
s=s^{\prime}-1
.
Comparing
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
with the resulting triangulation, we see that they agree.

•

For all other scenarios, the Pachner move is the
4
-
4
move removing
AS_{0}
and adding
BS_{0}^{\prime}
.
If
s\neq s^{\prime}-1
, then
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
belongs to Case (2e), and by comparison, the resulting triangulation is
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
.
Otherwise, the resulting triangulation is
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}^{\prime}
which belongs to Case (2c).

(8)

Case (2f): If
s=s^{\prime}+1\neq t
and
\bar{B}=A
, then
\mathbf{S}
is special. In this case,
\mathbf{S}^{*}
belongs to Case (2f) and
\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathbf{S}}=\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}
.
Notice that the triangle
ABS_{0}
is adjacent to
ABS_{0}\bar{A}
if
S_{0}\neq\bar{A}
and is adjacent to
ABS_{0}\bar{B}
otherwise.

•

If
s\notin\{t-1,t\}
, the Pachner move is the
4
-
4
move removing
AS_{0}
and adding
BS_{0}^{\prime}
. By comparison, the resulting triangulation is
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
, which belongs to Case (2f).

•

If
s=t-1
, the Pachner move is the
3
-
2
move removing
AS_{0}
. By comparison, the resulting triangulation is
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
, which belongs to Case (2f).

•

If
s=t
, the Pachner move is the
3
-
2
move removing
AS_{0}
. By comparison, the resulting triangulation is
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}^{*}}
, which belongs to Case (2b). ∎

Proof of Proposition
5.3
.

Notice the two triangulations
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
described in Proposition
5.3
are
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}_{max}}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}_{min}}
respectively. Since the number of admissible paths is finite, by Proposition
5.13
, the triangulations
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}_{max}}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}_{min}}
are connected by a sequence of triangulations of form
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}
or
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{S}}^{\prime}
such that adjacent ones differ by a single step of
2
-
3
,
3
-
2
or
4
-
4
move. On the other hand, by Proposition
5.12
, each of the intermediate triangulations admits an angle structure. This gives Proposition
5.3
.
∎
